and he grounds it in the parallel passage in Sūrah al-Nūr: "and when they are called to God and His Messenger that he may judge between them, at once a party of them turn away… nay, those — they are the wrongdoers" (24:48–50). **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zuhrī transmitted from al-Ḥasan that the Prophet said: "Whoever is summoned by his adversary to one of the judges of the Muslims and does not answer is a wrongdoer and has no right" — and Ibn al-ʿArabī declared this ḥadīth unsound: that he is a wrongdoer is correct speech, but "he has no right" does not stand, though it may be understood to mean that he is not upon the truth. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Khuwayz Mindād al-Mālikī said it is obligatory on everyone summoned to a judge's assembly to answer, unless it is known that the judge is corrupt or that there is enmity between the claimant and the defendant. **[Qurṭubī]** The verse also proves that the laws of those before us are law for us except what we know to be abrogated, and that we are obliged to judge by the laws of the prophets before us. **[Qurṭubī]** The reason we do not read the Torah or act by what is in it is that those who hold it are not trustworthy over it and have altered and changed it; were we to know that something of it had not been altered, it would be permissible for us to read it. **[Qurṭubī]** Something similar is related of ʿUmar, who said to Kaʿb: if you know it to be the Torah which God sent down to Moses son of ʿImrān, then read it — and the Prophet knew what of it had not been changed, which is why he called them to it and to judgement by it. **[Study Quran]** Al-Qurṭubī notes that Muslims can read and act according to the Torah except for whatever is known to be abrogated by the Qurʾān or ḥadīth, and that they are obligated to act in accord with the religious laws of previous prophets unless these are abrogated by Islam; for al-Qurṭubī this would have been conditional upon the text being authentic, and although the Prophet would have known what was authentic, we cannot do likewise. **[Study Quran]** Although tempered by questions of abrogation and distortion, this verse and 5:43 can be seen to suggest, together with the Prophet's actions, the continuing religious validity and spiritual efficacy of previous scriptures for the followers of those religions.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** The stoning incident is also referenced in connection with 5:43: "and how is it that they come to thee for judgment, when they have the Torah, wherein is God's Judgment?" **[Qurṭubī]** 24:48–50 and 3:118. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He reads the verse together with 3:24–25 as one rebuke.

**Reflection.** The charge is precise and damaging: they were not asked to accept a foreign book but their own, and the one calling them to it was the man whose description they confessed was in it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. "A portion of the Book" is the telling phrase — they were given a share of it, not the whole, and a share can be used or ignored **[Jalālayn]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The refusal is therefore not a failure of evidence but of nerve, and it is what the verse says it is: turning away while being called **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The legal discussions that branch from it show how seriously the commentators took the principle — that being summoned to what one professes to believe is not optional **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:24 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمْ قَالُوا۟ لَن تَمَسَّنَا ٱلنَّارُ إِلَّآ أَيَّامًۭا مَّعْدُودَٰتٍۢ ۖ وَغَرَّهُمْ فِى دِينِهِم مَّا كَانُوا۟ يَفْتَرُونَ

*That is because they say, "The Fire will not touch us except for days numbered" — and what they used to fabricate has deluded them in their religion.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: these people who were called to the Book of God that it might judge between them in truth in what they disputed with the Messenger of God refused to answer to the judgement of the Torah and the truth in it, on account of their saying, "The Fire will not touch us except for days numbered" — which is forty days, the days in which they worshipped the calf — "then our Lord will bring us out of it", deluding themselves "by what they used to fabricate", that is, by the lies and falsehoods they invented in claiming that they are the sons of God and His beloved, and that God promised their father Jacob not to enter any of his descendants into the Fire except by the discharge of an oath. **[Ṭabarī]** God declared them liars in all these claims and told His prophet Muḥammad that they are the people of the Fire, abiding therein forever, rather than those who believe in God and His messengers and what they brought from Him. **[Jalālayn]** "That turning away and rejection was because they said, 'the Fire shall not touch us except for a number of days' — that is, for forty days only, the length of time their forefathers worshipped the calf, after which it would end; and the lies they used to invent in saying this have deluded them in their religion." **[Saʿdī]** He gives two causes for their turning away. **[Saʿdī]** The first is their false witness on their own behalf to salvation — that the Fire will not touch them except for a few days, which they fixed according to their own corrupt desires, as though the ordering of the Kingdom belonged to them; as when they said, "none shall enter Paradise except those who are Jews or Christians" — and it is known that these are vain wishes, false by the revealed law and by reason. **[Saʿdī]** The second cause is that when they denied God's signs and invented lies against Him, Satan made their evil deeds seem fair to them, and they were deluded by that and it appeared to them to be the truth — as a punishment to them for turning away from the truth. **[Ibn Kathīr]** What made them dare to challenge and defy the truth is their false claim that God will punish them in the Fire for only seven days, a day for every thousand years of life in this world; and he refers to his treatment of the subject in Sūrah al-Baqarah. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And that which they used to invent regarding their religion has deceived them" means: what caused them to remain upon their false creed is that they deceived themselves into believing the Fire would only touch them for a few days on account of their errors — but it is they who invented this notion, and God gave them no authority to support the claim. **[Maʿārif]** The Jews contended that they had worshipped the calf for forty days and so would incur punishment, if any, for that period only; and this is self-deception caused by what they have been forging for themselves, such as the belief that they were the progeny of prophets and would therefore escape punishment in the Fire of Hell.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** He explains *taḥillat al-qasam* — the discharge of an oath — as what expiates one's oath; the sense is that the Fire would touch them only with a light touch, like the discharge of an oath by one who swears. **[Jalālayn]** The clause "it has deluded them in their religion" is semantically connected to "the lies which they used to invent" — that is, the fabricating itself is the deluding. **[Study Quran]** "Days numbered" corresponds to 2:80.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: "They said: the Fire will not touch us except for the discharge of the oath, in which we set up the calf, and then the oath and the punishment are cut off from us"; and God said, "and what they used to fabricate has deluded them in their religion" — that is, their saying "we are the sons of God and His beloved". **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Rabīʿ, through Ibn Abī Jaʿfar from his father, said: they said, "we will not be punished in the Fire except for forty days" — meaning the Jews — and that is the period of the days in which they set up the calf; Qatādah said the same. **[Ṭabarī]** Mujāhid, through Ibn Jurayj, said: "and what they used to fabricate has deluded them" — what deluded them was their saying, "the Fire will not touch us except for days numbered". **[Qurṭubī]** The phrase refers back to the turning away and the aversion, and to their deluding themselves with their saying "we are the sons of God and His beloved" and other such claims; and he refers to his discussion of "the Fire will not touch us" in al-Baqarah. **[Study Quran]** According to some, "days numbered" refers to the Jews' belief that they would be punished for only forty nights, corresponding to the time spent worshipping the calf, after which they would be replaced by another people; others mention seven days, one day for each thousand years of the earth's existence as they calculated it.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** God declared them liars in all of it and informed His prophet that they, and not the believers, are the people of the Fire, abiding therein. **[Study Quran]** Whatever duration they claimed, to believe that their days of punishment in the Hereafter would be "numbered" suggests their unwarranted confidence in avoiding eternal punishment. **[Saʿdī]** These are vain wishes, known to be false by the revealed law and by reason alike.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 2:80 is the parallel for "days numbered". **[Ibn Kathīr]** He refers back to his treatment in Sūrah al-Baqarah. **[Qurṭubī]** He likewise refers back to al-Baqarah, and to 5:18 for the claim "we are the sons of God and His beloved".

**Reflection.** The verse diagnoses unbelief as a bad dose of optimism: not "there is no God" but "the reckoning will be brief, and it will not really reach us" **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The claim rested on two props — an inheritance from prophets and a fixed number of days — and both are fabrications the speakers produced themselves, which is why the verse calls the delusion self-manufactured **[Jalālayn]**, **[Maʿārif]**. What the number was matters less than what it did: it made defiance cheap, and a person who has priced punishment low will refuse any judgement that costs him anything **[Saʿdī]**, **[Study Quran]**. The surah treats this as the engine behind the refusal of 3:23 — they did not reject the Torah because it was unclear, but because they had already arranged to be safe **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:25 — فَكَيْفَ إِذَا جَمَعْنَٰهُمْ لِيَوْمٍۢ لَّا رَيْبَ فِيهِ وَوُفِّيَتْ كُلُّ نَفْسٍۢ مَّا كَسَبَتْ وَهُمْ لَا يُظْلَمُونَ

*How will it be when We gather them for a Day in which there is no doubt, and every soul is paid in full what it has earned, and they are not wronged?*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: what state will be the state of these people who said what they said and did what they did — turning away from the Book of God, deluding themselves about their Lord, and inventing lies? This is a severe threat from God and a heavy warning. **[Ṭabarī]** The meaning is: how great will be the punishment and the exemplary chastisement they meet when He gathers them for a Day on which every worker is paid the recompense of his work according to what he deserves, without being wronged — because on that Day He punishes only for what was incurred and takes to task only for what was done; the doer of good is requited with his good and the doer of evil with his evil, and none of His creatures will fear from Him that Day any wrong or any diminution. **[Jalālayn]** "But how will it be — their predicament — when We gather them for a day, that is to say on a day of which there is no doubt, no uncertainty, that is the Day of Resurrection; and every soul, from among the People of the Scripture and others, shall be paid in full the requital of what it has earned, what it has done of good or evil; and they, that is people, shall not be wronged" — in that no good deed shall be diminished and no evil deed shall be increased. **[Saʿdī]** How will their state be when God gathers them on the Day of Resurrection and pays the doers in full for what they did, and God's justice runs its course among His servants? There — do not ask what punishment they will reach and what good and reward they will have lost, and that is by what their own hands earned; your Lord is no wronger of His servants. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "How will it be when We gather them together on the Day about which there is no doubt" — what will their condition be after they have uttered this lie about God, rejected His messengers and killed His prophets and their scholars who enjoined righteousness and forbade evil? God will ask them about all of it and punish them for what they did. **[Qurṭubī]** It is an address to the Prophet and his community by way of bringing them to a halt and making them wonder: how will their state be, or what will they do, when they are mustered on the Day of Resurrection and all that glitter they claimed in this world has dissolved away from them, and they are recompensed for what they earned of their disbelief, their audacity and the ugliness of their deeds? **[Study Quran]** Some commentators understand "unto a day" to signify all that they will undergo on that day, while for others it simply means the Day of Judgment itself.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** He raises the question: why is it "for a day" (*li-yawmin*) and not "on a day" (*fī yawmin*)? **[Ṭabarī]** Because the sense of *lām* in this position differs from that of *fī*. Were *fī* in place of the *lām*, the sense would be: how will it be when We gather them on the Day of Resurrection — what punishment and chastisement will they have? That is not the sense carried by the *lām*. **[Ṭabarī]** With the *lām* the meaning is: how will it be when We gather them for what will occur on a day in which there is no doubt, and for what will take place on that day of God's decisive judgement between His creatures — what punishment and painful chastisement will they have then? So with the *lām* in "for a day in which there is no doubt" there is an intended verb and a sought predicate whose mention is omitted, the indication of the *lām* entering upon "the day" sufficing for it; and that is not the case with *fī*, which is why the *lām* was chosen. **[Qurṭubī]** The *lām* in "for a day" means *fī*, according to al-Kisāʾī; the Baṣrans said the meaning is "for the reckoning of a day"; and al-Ṭabarī said it is "for what occurs in a day". **[Ṭabarī]** "No doubt in it" means no doubt of its coming. **[Ṭabarī]** "And every soul is paid in full" means: God pays every soul in full what it earned of good and evil. **[Ṭabarī]** "And they are not wronged" means: He does not stint the doer of good the reward of his good, and does not punish an evil-doer for a crime he did not commit. **[Study Quran]** "Paid in full" is a common way of expressing the soul's experience on the Day of Judgment and is related to the word for fulfilling one's oaths, as at 2:40: "Fulfil My covenant, and I shall fulfil your covenant."

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 2:40, for the language of fulfilling. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He reads the verse as the threat that closes the three-verse unit opened at 3:23. **[Qurṭubī]** He relates it back to the dissolution of the glittering claims of 3:24.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The recompense is strictly proportioned: He requites the doer of good with his good and the doer of evil with his evil, and none fears from Him that Day either wrong or diminution. **[Saʿdī]** God's justice will run its course among His servants, and the punishment will be by what their own hands earned — your Lord is no wronger of His servants. **[Jalālayn]** No good deed will be diminished and no evil deed increased.

*Source note:* Maʿārif al-Qurʾān treats 3:23–25 as a single unit; the substance of that block is cited at 3:23 and 3:24, and it has no separate block for 3:25.

**Reflection.** The question is left hanging — "how will it be?" — and every commentary supplies the answer by restating the sentence it closes: they invented a short punishment for themselves, and the Day that is coming has no such provision **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The grammar is made to carry doctrine: "for a day" rather than "on a day", because they are being gathered for the reckoning and for what that day will do, not merely placed inside it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And the clause "they will not be wronged" is the verse's quiet guarantee — the horror of the scene is not that the scales are rigged, but that they are exact **[Jalālayn]**, **[Saʿdī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Those who spent this life arranging to be safe will find that safety was never theirs to arrange **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Study Quran]**.

*Source note: Maʿārif al-Qurʾān runs 3:23–25 as a single unit whose substance — the summons to the Torah, the claim about the Fire and the forty days of the calf — is set out above at 3:23 and 3:24. It has nothing separate for 3:25, so it is not cited again here. As-Saʿdī's 3:23–25 block, by contrast, does reach the question of 3:25 — the state of these people when God gathers them and justice runs its course — and that passage is quoted in the Meaning above.*

## 3:26 — قُلِ ٱللَّهُمَّ مَٰلِكَ ٱلْمُلْكِ تُؤْتِى ٱلْمُلْكَ مَن تَشَآءُ وَتَنزِعُ ٱلْمُلْكَ مِمَّن تَشَآءُ وَتُعِزُّ مَن تَشَآءُ وَتُذِلُّ مَن تَشَآءُ ۖ بِيَدِكَ ٱلْخَيْرُ ۖ إِنَّكَ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍۢ قَدِيرٌۭ

*Say: "O God, Master of the Kingdom, You give the kingdom to whom You will and wrest it from whom You will; You exalt whom You will and abase whom You will. In Your hand is the good. You are powerful over everything."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The meaning of "Say: O God" is: say, O Muḥammad, "O God". **[Ibn Kathīr]** The command is to say it while praising your Lord, thanking Him, relying upon Him in every matter and trusting in Him; "O God, Possessor of the power" means all sovereignty is Yours; "You give power to whom You will, and take power from whom You will, and endue with honour whom You will, and humiliate whom You will" means: You are the Giver, You are the Taker, it is Your will that occurs and whatever You do not will does not occur. **[Jalālayn]** "Say: 'O God, Master of the Kingdom; You give the Kingdom to whom You will from among Your creatures and seize the Kingdom from whom You will; You exalt whom You will by giving the kingdom to him and You abase whom You will by seizing it from him; in Your hand, in Your power, is good — that is, as well as evil. You are able to do all things.'" **[Saʿdī]** God commands His Prophet — and others follow him in this — to speak on behalf of his Lord, declaring His sole command of the disposal of affairs and the ordering of the upper and lower worlds, and His exclusive right to absolute sovereignty and firm disposal: that He gives the kingdom to whom He will, wrests it from whom He will, exalts whom He will and abases whom He will. The matter, then, is not by the wishes of the People of the Book nor of anyone else; the matter is God's and the disposal is His, with none to oppose Him in His disposal and none to assist Him in His determining. **[Maʿārif]** Appearing in the form of a prayer, this verse brings into focus God's most perfect power as it manifests itself in the rise and fall of nations and in the revolutions that rock countries, and at the same time hints that the promise He had made would come to pass and that Persia and Byzantium would fall to the Muslims.

**Context.** **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās and Anas b. Mālik said: when the Messenger of God conquered Makkah and promised his community the dominion of Persia and Byzantium, the hypocrites and the Jews said, "Far-fetched, far-fetched! How should Muḥammad have the dominion of Persia and Byzantium? They are mightier and better defended than that. Was Makkah and Madīnah not enough for Muḥammad, that he should covet the dominion of Persia and Byzantium?" — and God sent down this verse. **[Jalālayn]** The same occasion is given: the Prophet promised his community sovereignty over the lands of Persia and Byzantium and the hypocrites said, "How preposterous!", and this was revealed. **[Maʿārif]** The occasion is placed at the Battle of the Trench: when the disbelievers of Arabia, the Jews and the Christians joined in a united front and resolved on a conclusive battle to eradicate Islam, the Prophet decided, in consultation with his Companions, that a trench be dug around the exposed parts of Madīnah. **[Maʿārif]** According to narrations in al-Bayhaqī, Abū Nuʿaym and Ibn Khuzaymah, each group of ten men was allotted forty hand-spans of a trench several miles long, and the work had to be finished in the shortest possible time, the Companions working on empty stomachs; when the diggers met a huge rock and could not break it, they sent Salmān al-Fārisī to the Prophet, who came, took the pick-axe and struck: the rock shattered and a light rose from it in which he said he saw the palaces and buildings of Ḥīrah in Persia. **[Maʿārif]** A second strike brought a second beam, in which he was shown the red palaces and buildings of the Byzantines; a third showed him the great palaces of Ṣanʿāʾ in Yemen; and he said, "I share with you the good news given by Gabriel that my community will prevail over all these countries." **[Maʿārif]** When the hypocrites of Madīnah heard of it they mocked: here they are, terrified, digging trenches, hungry, not knowing whether their own lives will be safe, yet dreaming of overrunning Persia, Byzantium and Yemen — and it was in answer to a people so unfair that this verse was revealed. **[Qurṭubī]** It has also been said that it was revealed to crush the falsehood of the Christians of Najrān in their claim that Jesus is God, since these descriptions make plain to everyone of sound natural constitution that Jesus has nothing of them. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Isḥāq said: God made known in this verse their obstinacy and their unbelief, and that Jesus, even though God gave him signs proving his prophethood such as raising the dead, is not the one who does these things — God alone is singled out by "You give the kingdom to whom You will and wrest it from whom You will" and by "You make the night enter into the day…"; were Jesus a god, this would belong to him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse encourages thanking God for the favours He granted His Messenger and his community: He transferred prophethood from the Children of Israel to the Arab, Qurashī, Makkan, unlettered Prophet, the last of all prophets and the messenger of God to all mankind and jinn, and He endowed him with the best qualities of the prophets before him and more besides — greater knowledge of God and His law, knowledge of more of the past and the future and of what will occur in the Hereafter — and He let his community reach the east and west of the earth and gave his religion and law dominance over every other religion and law.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** The grammarians differ over the *mīm* of *Allāhumma*, since it is a vocative and the rule for a singular unattached vocative is to be raised. **[Ṭabarī]** One group said the two *mīm*s were added because it is not called with *yā* as names without the article are called — one says "O Zayd" and "O ʿAmr" — so the *mīm* was made a substitute for *yā*, as in *fam*, *ibnam*, *hum*, *zurqum* and *sutahum*, words and epithets from which a letter is dropped and a *mīm* put in its place; in the same way the *yā* was dropped from *Allāhumma* and the *mīm* made to stand in its stead at the end of the name. **[Ṭabarī]** Others rejected this, saying: we have heard the Arabs call *Allāhumma* with *yā*, just as they call it without the *mīm*; were that view right, the Arabs would not have brought in *yā* when they had already produced the substitute for it. **[Ṭabarī]** They cited as heard from the Arabs the line: "And what harm to you if you say, whenever you pray or magnify: O Allāhumma, return our shaykh to us in safety" — also transmitted as "whenever you glorify or magnify". **[Qurṭubī]** The grammarians agreed that the word has the *hāʾ* vowelled with *ḍammah*, the *mīm* doubled and vowelled with *fatḥah*, and that it is a vocative; and it is also transmitted with the *mīm* lightened, in the line of al-Aʿshā. **[Study Quran]** "O God" translates *Allāhumma*, since this construction designates an address to God in the second person, but opinions differ over its etymology: some consider it a shortening of *yā Allāhu ummanā bi-khayr* ("O God, bring us good"), others suggest less plausibly that the *mīm* of the suffix takes the place of the vocative particle *yā*; some speculate it comes from Aramaic or Akkadian. It is not related to the plural form, as is the case with the Hebrew *Elohim*. **[Study Quran]** "Master of Sovereignty" renders *mālik al-mulk*, two words closely related etymologically to each other and to "king" (*malik*) and "dominion" (*malakūt*); *mulk* can also mean "rule" or "possession" — see 67:1.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** ʿAlī said that the Prophet said: when God willed to send down the Opening of the Book, the Throne Verse, "God bears witness" (3:18) and "Say: O God, Master of the Kingdom" up to "without reckoning", they clung to the Throne with no veil between them and God, and they said, "Our Lord, You send us down to the abode of sins and to those who disobey You?" and God said: "By My might and majesty, no servant recites you after every prescribed prayer but I lodge him in the Sacred Enclosure whatever he may have done, and but that I look upon him with My hidden eye seventy times each day, and but that I settle seventy needs for him each day the least of which is forgiveness, and but that I protect him from every enemy and give him victory over him, and nothing bars him from entering Paradise except death" *(a report of disputed standing, transmitted by Abū Nuʿaym)*. **[Qurṭubī]** Muʿādh b. Jabal said that he was kept from the Friday prayer by a Jew to whom he owed an *ūqiyah* of gold dust, who sat at his door watching for him; the Prophet asked him, "Muʿādh, shall I teach you a supplication with which, if you had a debt filling the earth with gold, God would discharge it for you?" — and taught him to recite "Say: O God, Master of the Kingdom" up to "without reckoning", then to say: "Compassionate One of this world and the Hereafter and Merciful One of them both, You give of these two to whom You will and withhold of these two from whom You will — settle my debt for me" *(transmitted by Abū Nuʿaym from ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī as a mursal report from Muʿādh; graded rare)*. **[Study Quran]** In one ḥadīth the Prophet advised a Companion who was in debt: "Shall I inform you of a prayer that, if you used it, and if you had a debt equal to Mt. Uḥud in gold, God would free you of it? Recite 'Say: O God, Master of Sovereignty…' until '… without reckoning' [and then say], 'O Compassionate One of this world and the Hereafter, and Merciful One of them both, Thou givest from these as Thou wilt, and withholdest from these as Thou wilt! Bestow Mercy upon me that will make me beyond need of the mercy of those other than Thee!'" **[Ibn Kathīr]** He cites 43:31–32 — "and they say: why was this Qurʾān not sent down to some great man of the two towns?" and "is it they who would portion out the mercy of your Lord?" — as God's refutation of those who thought they could decide for Him, along with 6:124, "God knows best with whom to place His Message", and 17:21.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** "In Your hand is the good" means: all good is from You, and none brings good deeds and good things but God. As for evil, it is not attributed to God — not in attribute, not in name and not in act — though it enters among the things He does and is subsumed under His decree and His determining; so good and evil are both inside the decree and the determining, and nothing occurs in His dominion except what He willed, yet evil is not attributed to God. One therefore does not say "in Your hand is good and evil"; rather one says "in Your hand is the good", as God said it and as His Messenger said it. **[Saʿdī]** As for those commentators who add "and likewise evil is in God's hand", that is pure illusion; their reasoning was that specifying good by mention conflicts with His all-embracing decree and determining, and the answer is the distinction just set out. **[Maʿārif]** The expression "in Your hand lies the good" needs explanation, since giving and taking of power, and honour and disgrace, were both mentioned side by side, and it would have seemed fitting to couple "evil" with "good". The text elects "good" alone and thereby points to something real: a person or a people may regard something as unwelcome — and it may indeed be so for that particular person or people — but looked at from the wider angle of the whole community of nations it may not really be evil, as al-Mutanabbī put it, "the calamities of one group are the gains of another". **[Maʿārif]** In short, the evil of the things we count evil is partial. **[Study Quran]** Some say *mulk* here refers to prophethood, and the verse could thus be meant to counter objections from various quarters to the selection of Muḥammad as a prophet, objections stemming from the fact that he was not of the Children of Israel, that he was not, before being chosen, the most prominent of the Arabs, or that he was a human being and not an angel. **[Study Quran]** All commentators understand "the good" to mean all possible good.

**Rulings.** **[Saʿdī]** "And You provide for whom You will without reckoning": God has mentioned elsewhere the means by which His provision is obtained, as in "whoever fears God, He will make for him a way out and provide for him from where he does not reckon" and "whoever relies upon God, He suffices him"; so it is upon His servants not to seek provision except from God, and to strive for it by the means He has made easy and permitted.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 43:31–32; 6:124; 17:21. **[Study Quran]** 67:1 for *mulk*. **[Qurṭubī]** 3:18 and 2:255 in the reports on the merits of these verses. **[Maʿārif]** 33:9–27 for the Battle of the Trench.

**Reflection.** The verse is a prayer shaped like an argument: before asking for anything it states that crowns are handed about by Someone, and that this Someone is not consulting the empires **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. That is why it was revealed when it was — at the moment when a community too poor and too hungry to be taken seriously was being promised the palaces of Persia and Byzantium, and being laughed at for it **[Jalālayn]**, **[Maʿārif]**. It also closes the Najrān argument from another direction: whatever marvels Jesus performed, none of them was "I give the kingdom to whom I will", and that is the difference between a servant and a lord **[Qurṭubī]**. And the single word left out — God says "in Your hand is the good" and not "the good and the evil" — is the whole of the etiquette of speaking about God, and the whole of the comfort as well, since what looks like loss from where we stand may be gain in the reckoning of the One who orders it **[Saʿdī]**, **[Maʿārif]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:27 — تُولِجُ ٱلَّيْلَ فِى ٱلنَّهَارِ وَتُولِجُ ٱلنَّهَارَ فِى ٱلَّيْلِ ۖ وَتُخْرِجُ ٱلْحَىَّ مِنَ ٱلْمَيِّتِ وَتُخْرِجُ ٱلْمَيِّتَ مِنَ ٱلْحَىِّ ۖ وَتَرْزُقُ مَن تَشَآءُ بِغَيْرِ حِسَابٍۢ

*You make the night pass into the day and the day pass into the night; You bring forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living; and You provide for whom You will without reckoning.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Tūliju* means "You cause to enter" — one says "so-and-so entered his house" (*walaja*) and "I made him enter" (*awlajtuhu*). **[Ṭabarī]** "You make the night enter into the day" means: You enter what You have decreased of the hours of the night into the hours of the day, adding to the increase of the one from the decrease of the other; and "You make the day enter into the night" means: You enter what You have decreased of the hours of the day into the hours of the night, adding to the hours of the night what You decreased of the hours of the day. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah and al-Suddī said the same: You enter what is diminished from one of the two into the other, so that the day becomes fifteen hours — its longest — and the night nine hours — its shortest; and correspondingly "You make the day enter into the night". This is also the view of al-Kalbī, and is transmitted from Ibn Masʿūd. **[Qurṭubī]** The wording may also include the simple succession of night and day, as though the disappearance of one were an entering into the other. **[Jalālayn]** "You make the night to pass, to enter, into the day and You make the day to pass into the night, each of them increasing by the amount by which the other decreases; You bring forth the living from the dead, such as humans and birds from sperm-drops and eggs respectively; and You bring forth the dead, the sperm-drop and the egg, from the living; and You provide with abundant provision whom You will without reckoning." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "You make the night enter into the day, and You make the day enter into the night" means: You take from the length of one and add it to the shortness of the other so that they become equal, and You take from the length of one and add it to the other so that they are not equal — and this goes on through the seasons of the year: spring, summer, autumn and winter. **[Saʿdī]** He is the One who disposes of time itself, not only of the turns of fortune among people: He makes the day enter into the night and the night into the day — this giving place to that and this taking the place of that, and adding to this what He decreases from that — so as to establish thereby the interests of His creatures. **[Maʿārif]** The verse demonstrates that God controls all spaces and heavenly bodies and employs the sun and the moon to make the days longer than the nights and the nights longer than the days at His will and command.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits the explanation with chains: al-Suddī said the entering continues until the night is fifteen hours and the day nine, and then the reverse; Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿIkrimah, said "what decreases from the day He puts into the night, and what decreases from the night He puts into the day"; Mujāhid said "what diminishes from one of them enters into the other, the two succeeding one another in those hours"; Qatādah, from al-Ḥasan, said "the decrease of the night into the increase of the day, and the decrease of the day into the increase of the night"; al-Ḍaḥḥāk said each of the two takes from the other, so the night is sometimes longer than the day and the day sometimes longer than the night; and Ibn Zayd said "this one is long and this one is short; He took from this and entered it into that, until this became long and that short". **[Ṭabarī]** On "You bring forth the living from the dead", the commentators differed. Some said it means: He brings the living thing out of the dead sperm-drop, and brings the dead sperm-drop out of the living thing — this being the report from ʿAbdallāh b. Masʿūd, through al-Aʿmash from Ibrāhīm: "it is the sperm-drop, which comes out of the man and is dead while he is alive; and the man comes out of it alive while it is dead". **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan said the meaning is that He brings the believer out of the disbeliever and the disbeliever out of the believer, and something similar is transmitted from Salmān al-Fārisī; on this reading, "death" and "life" are metaphors for the death of the disbeliever's heart and the life of the believer's heart. **[Qurṭubī]** Many scholars held that life and death in the verse are literal: ʿIkrimah said it is the bringing out of the chicken, alive, from the egg, which is dead, and of the egg, dead, from the chicken, alive; Ibn Masʿūd said it is the sperm-drop; and ʿIkrimah and al-Suddī said it is the grain coming out of the ear and the ear out of the grain, and the date-stone from the palm and the palm from the date-stone — the life in the palm and the ear being by way of comparison. **[Qurṭubī]** "And You provide for whom You will without reckoning" means without straitening and without stinting, as one says of a man "he gives without reckoning", as though he does not count what he gives. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "You bring the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living" means: You bring out the seed from the plant and the plant from the seed, the date from its stone and the stone from the date, the believer from the disbeliever and the disbeliever from the believer, the chicken from the egg and the egg from the chicken. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And You give provision to whom You will without limit" means: You give to whom You will innumerable amounts of wealth while depriving others of it, out of wisdom and justice.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** Maʿmar related from al-Zuhrī that the Prophet entered upon his wives and there was a woman of fine appearance; he asked who she was and they said, "One of your maternal aunts"; he asked again and they said, "Khālidah daughter of al-Aswad b. ʿAbd Yaghūth"; and the Prophet said, "Glory to Him who brings forth the living from the dead" — she was a righteous woman and her father was a disbeliever. *(weak)* **[Maʿārif]** Imām al-Baghawī reports a ḥadīth from the Prophet: whoever recites after every *sūrah* the Opening of the Book, the Throne Verse, two verses of Āl ʿImrān — "God bears witness that there is no god but He" (3:18) — and this verse from "Say: O God, Master of the Kingdom" (3:26–27), God will make his abode in Paradise, place him in the Sacred Enclosure, bless him with His mercy seventy times every day, fulfil seventy of his needs and protect him against every envier and enemy and make him prevail over them *(weak)*. **[Study Quran]** The debt-relief ḥadīth attached to verses 26–27 is given in full at 3:26.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** The theme of night and day passing into one another also appears at 22:61; 31:29; 35:13; 57:6; on the cycle of life and death see 2:28 and, for the revival of dry earth by rain, 2:164. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He relates the alternation to the seasons of the year. **[Ṭabarī]** He refers to his earlier treatments of the vocabulary of "earning" and "recompense".

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** If "the living" and "the dead" are taken in a broad and general sense, this includes the learned and the ignorant, the perfect and the imperfect, and the believer and the disbeliever — showing that God's perfect power exercises absolute control over all phenomena, physical and spiritual, through which He can make a Muslim out of a disbeliever, a perfect believer out of a staunch disbeliever and a scholar out of an ignorant person if He wills; and if He wills, He can let a believer turn into a disbeliever, or a rustic into a scholar. He brought Abraham out of an idol-worshipper, and He let the son of Noah remain an infidel; the son of a scholar can remain illiterate and the son of an illiterate person can become a scholar. **[Study Quran]** Some interpret the notion that God provides without reckoning, and that God does as He wills (2:253; 14:27), to imply that God is free to be what appears to human beings to be unjust, while others claim that God can reward and exalt only those who are deserving; but the supremacy of God's will does not detract from or contradict His goodness and compassion, and at the same time God could never be bound by a human conception of merit, since the span of any life includes both this world and the next and human beings do not possess God's measures. **[Study Quran]** For the commentators, "without reckoning" can mean that none can hold God to account, that His giving is limitless, or that He can give without regard to the merit of human beings; it can also allude to God's infinite mercy beyond any human measure. **[Saʿdī]** He brings the living from the dead as He brings crops and diverse trees from their seeds, the believer from the disbeliever, and the dead from the living as He brings grains and date-stones and crops and trees and eggs from birds — He is the One who brings opposites out of one another, and all the elements have yielded to Him.

**Reflection.** The verse answers the empires with astronomy and biology: the same hand that moves a crown moves the solstice, and the same hand that moves the solstice splits a seed **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**. That is the argument against the ridicule at the Trench — if the One promising you Persia is the One who already governs the lengthening of a day, the promise is not the improbable part **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The middle clause is the one the commentators linger on, because it works at once in the seed, in the egg and in the human heart: life coming out of death is the ordinary miracle they are asked to notice daily **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. And "without reckoning" ends the prayer by saying what every petitioner needs to hear — that the Giver is not keeping score as a creditor would, and that what He gives cannot be measured against what we think we deserve **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:28 — لَّا يَتَّخِذِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ أَوْلِيَآءَ مِن دُونِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ ۖ وَمَن يَفْعَلْ ذَٰلِكَ فَلَيْسَ مِنَ ٱللَّهِ فِى شَىْءٍ إِلَّآ أَن تَتَّقُوا۟ مِنْهُمْ تُقَىٰةًۭ ۗ وَيُحَذِّرُكُمُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسَهُۥ ۗ وَإِلَى ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمَصِيرُ

*Let not the believers take the disbelievers as allies in place of the believers. Whoever does that has nothing to do with God — unless you guard yourselves against them in prudence. God warns you of Himself, and to God is the homecoming.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** This is a prohibition from God to the believers against taking the disbelievers as helpers, supporters and backing; the *dhāl* of *yattakhidh* is vowelled with *kasrah* because the verb is in the jussive of prohibition and meets a following quiescent letter. **[Ṭabarī]** The meaning is: do not, O believers, take the disbelievers as a backing and supporters, allying with them in their religion and aiding them against the Muslims in place of the believers, and exposing the Muslims' weak points to them; for whoever does that "has nothing to do with God" — meaning he is quit of God and God is quit of him, by his apostasy from his religion and his entry into unbelief. **[Jalālayn]** "Let not the believers take the disbelievers as patrons rather than, that is instead of, the believers — for whoever does that, that is whoever takes them as patrons, does not belong to the religion of God in any way — unless you protect yourselves against them as a safeguard." **[Ibn Kathīr]** God prohibited His believing servants from becoming supporters of the disbelievers, or taking them as comrades with whom they develop friendships, rather than the believers. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And whoever does that will never be helped by God in any way" means whoever commits this act that God has prohibited, God will discard him. **[Saʿdī]** This is a prohibition from God and a warning to the believers against taking the disbelievers as allies in place of the believers, for the believers are allies of one another and God is their ally; and "whoever does that" — that is, that allegiance — "has nothing to do with God", meaning he is quit of God and God is quit of him, as in "whoever among you allies with them is one of them". **[Maʿārif]** In these verses Muslims have been instructed not to take disbelievers as their friends; those who act against this instruction have been sternly warned, for they will find that their bond of love and friendship with God has been cut off. **[Maʿārif]** Any emotionally involved friendship that comes from the heart is absolutely forbidden; a formal friendship at the level of mutual dealings is, no doubt, permissible, though that too is not favoured if unnecessary. **[Study Quran]** In this context "protectors" renders *awliyāʾ*, a word that can mean "protector", "ruler", "ally", "client" or "friend" depending on context; the subject matter is not friendship in the ordinary sense but allegiance and group alignment in which the political stakes go beyond mere confessional identity. **[Study Quran]** "Has no bond with God" could be translated literally "is not of God in aught", the phrase "of someone" meaning to be allied or associated with them, to be part of their community — to be "one of them".

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits from Ibn Isḥāq, from Muḥammad b. Abī Muḥammad, from ʿIkrimah or Saʿīd b. Jubayr, from Ibn ʿAbbās: al-Ḥajjāj b. ʿAmr, the confederate of Kaʿb b. al-Ashraf, Ibn Abī al-Ḥuqayq and Qays b. Zayd had insinuated themselves into the intimacy of a group of the Anṣār in order to turn them from their religion. Rifāʿah b. al-Mundhir b. Zanbar, ʿAbdallāh b. Jubayr and Saʿd b. Khaythamah said to those men: "Keep away from these Jews, and beware of keeping their company and their intimacy, lest they turn you from your religion." But those men refused anything but their intimacy and their company, and God sent down this verse. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḍaḥḥāk related from Ibn ʿAbbās that the verse was revealed concerning ʿUbādah b. al-Ṣāmit al-Anṣārī, a man of Badr, who had a confederate pact with the Jews: when the Prophet went out on the day of the Confederates, ʿUbādah said, "O Prophet of God, I have five hundred men of the Jews with me, and I think they should go out with me so that I may strengthen myself with them against the enemy" — and God sent down this verse. **[Qurṭubī]** It is also said that it was revealed concerning ʿAmmār b. Yāsir, when he spoke some of what the idolaters wanted from him. **[Study Quran]** According to one view the verse was revealed in connection with hypocrites in Madīnah who were collaborating with the Makkans against the Prophet; others believe it referred to a friendship between some Helpers and certain Jewish men who wanted to lead them away from their religion; still others mention the case of a prominent Companion at the Battle of the Trench who wanted to marshal several hundred Jews to fight on the side of the Prophet.

**Rulings.** **[Ṭabarī]** The exception is: unless you are under their authority and fear for yourselves, in which case you may show them allegiance with your tongues while inwardly hostile — without following them in their unbelief and without aiding them against any Muslim by deed. **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭalḥah: God forbade the believers to be soft towards the disbelievers or to take them as intimate confidants in place of the believers, unless the disbelievers have the upper hand over them, in which case they may show them softness while differing from them in religion. **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās, through Ibn Jurayj, said: *al-tuqāh* is speech with the tongue while the heart is at rest in faith. **[Ṭabarī]** ʿIkrimah said: "as long as no blood of a Muslim is shed and no property of his is made lawful". **[Ṭabarī]** Mujāhid said: "except by way of accommodation in this world and affable dealing". **[Ṭabarī]** Abū al-ʿĀliyah, through al-Rabīʿ, said: *al-taqiyyah* is with the tongue and not with deeds. **[Qurṭubī]** Muʿādh b. Jabal and Mujāhid said: *al-taqiyyah* belonged to the early days of Islam before the Muslims grew strong; today God has made Islam too mighty for them to need to guard themselves against an enemy. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: it is that one speak with his tongue while his heart is at rest in faith, and that he neither kill nor commit a sin. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan said: *al-taqiyyah* is permissible for a person until the Day of Resurrection, and there is no *taqiyyah* in killing. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that when a believer is living among disbelievers he may be affable with them in speech if he fears for his life and his heart is at rest in faith; and *taqiyyah* is not lawful except in the case of fear of being killed, of mutilation, or of grave harm. **[Qurṭubī]** If one is coerced into unbelief, the sound view is that he may hold firm and not consent to utter the word of unbelief, though it is permissible for him to do so. **[Jalālayn]** The dispensation is that one may show patronage to them through words but not in the heart; this was before the ascendancy of Islam, and the dispensation applies to any individual residing in a land in which he has no say. **[Study Quran]** Those who guard against them out of prudence are exempted if they fear death or great harm through persecution at the hands of the community in which they live, in which case they can hide their faith through words but not through actions — they can deny to others that they are Muslim but they cannot then violate the major rules of Islam by murdering, stealing or committing adultery under cover of that denial. **[Study Quran]** Others mention an exception made for blood relatives, in which case disassociation in religion is still necessary even if the familial connection is maintained. **[Maʿārif]** He sets out four degrees of relation with non-Muslims. **[Maʿārif]** The first is *muwālāt*, close friendship of the heart involving intense emotional commitment, and this is restricted to true Muslims; a Muslim is not permitted to have this kind of relationship with a non-Muslim. **[Maʿārif]** The second is *muwāsāt*, relationship based on sympathy, kindness and concern, including charitable help, condolence and well-wishing; barring disbelievers who are at war with Muslims, this is permissible with all other non-Muslims, as set out at 60:8. **[Maʿārif]** The third is *mudārāt*, relations based on customary cordiality, courtesy, pleasant behaviour and politeness — and it is precisely this degree that "unless you guard yourselves against them in prudence" means, so that *muwālāt* with disbelievers is not permissible except when one is defending oneself against them; and since *mudārāt* somewhat resembles *muwālāt*, it was exempted from the category of *muwālāt*. **[Maʿārif]** The fourth is *muʿāmalāt*, dealings and transactions in business, employment, wages, industry and technology; these too are permissible with non-Muslims except where such dealings harm the general body of Muslims, which is why Muslim jurists have prohibited the sale of arms to disbelievers at war with Muslims while permitting trade and allied activity, employment in their firms and institutions.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** Jābir b. Zayd, Mujāhid and al-Ḍaḥḥāk read *tuqātan*; Ḥamzah and al-Kisāʾī read it with *imālah* and the rest without. **[Qurṭubī]** The root is *wuqayatun* on the pattern of *tuʾadah* and *tuhamah*, the *wāw* being changed to *tāʾ* and the *yāʾ* to *alif*. **[Study Quran]** "Prudence" translates *tuqāh*, which some read as *taqiyyah*; the latter is more widely known and often translated "dissimulation", though the word is etymologically related to "taking care" and "being wary" and is lexically related to *taqwā*, or reverence.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Bukhārī recorded that Abū al-Dardāʾ said: "We smile in the face of some people although our hearts curse them"; and al-Bukhārī said that al-Ḥasan said, "The *tuqyah* is allowed until the Day of Resurrection." **[Study Quran]** The commentators mention an incident in which two Companions were captured by Musaylimah, a man who claimed to be a prophet, and were asked to affirm his prophetic status alongside that of Muḥammad; one did and the other did not, but neither lost faith in his heart, and the Prophet said, "As for the first victim, he stayed with his certainty and truth, and happy is he. As for the second, he received the leniency of God, and there is no blame on him." **[Maʿārif]** He records from the Prophet's own conduct the proof that the prohibition of *muwālāt* does not close the door to kindness: when Makkah was gripped by famine he went out personally to help the enemies who had driven him from his town; at the conquest of Makkah, when those same enemies fell into his power, he released them all saying, "No reproach upon you this day" — not only granting amnesty but not even reproaching them for past tyranny. **[Maʿārif]** Prisoners of war were treated by him with a tenderness many cannot claim even towards their own children; the injuries the disbelievers inflicted on him were met with no revenge and not even a wish of harm; a delegation of Thaqīf who had not yet accepted Islam were given the honour of staying in the Prophet's mosque. **[Maʿārif]** ʿUmar gave stipends and allowances to needy non-Muslim *dhimmīs* — and all of this was *muwāsāt*, *mudārāt* or *muʿāmalāt*, having nothing to do with forbidden *muwālāt*. **[Maʿārif]** The ḥadīth in al-Bukhārī and Muslim is cited: "Whoever loves for the sake of God and hates for the sake of God alone has perfected his faith" — from which we know that faith remains incomplete unless a person subordinates his love and friendship and his hatred and enmity to God.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "Has nothing to do with God" means he is not of God's party nor of His allies in anything, comparable to "ask the town" — and Sībawayhi cites "he is of me two leagues", meaning of my companions and with me. **[Qurṭubī]** On "God warns you of Himself", al-Zajjāj said it means God warns you of Him, and usage settled on this form, as in "You know what is in my soul and I know not what is in Your soul" (5:116), meaning You know what is with me and in my reality and I know not what is with You nor what is in Your reality. **[Qurṭubī]** Others said the meaning is "God warns you of His punishment", on the pattern of "ask the town". **[Qurṭubī]** "And to God is the homecoming" means to God's recompense is the homecoming, and in it is an affirmation of the resurrection.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 60:1, "take not My enemies and your enemies as friends, showing affection towards them… and whosoever of you does that has gone astray from the straight path"; 4:144; 5:51; and 8:73, "and those who disbelieve are allies of one another; if you do not behave the same there will be *fitnah* on the earth and great corruption". **[Study Quran]** 4:88–90 for the political context of a verse with similar language, and 4:144; 5:51; 5:57; also 3:118: "take not intimates apart from yourselves; they will not stint you in corruption. They wish you to suffer". **[Study Quran]** Similar prohibitions apply to those who reject God but take another agent as their *walī* (7:30; 11:20; 13:16; 18:102; 29:41; 39:3; 42:6, 9, 46). **[Study Quran]** The Qurʾān excuses the one who is coerced while his heart is at peace in faith (16:106), revealed in connection with the earliest martyrs in Makkah. **[Qurṭubī]** 3:118 for "take not as confidants those other than yourselves". **[Jalālayn]** He notes the dispensation applies to any individual residing in a land in which he has no say.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** "And God warns you of Himself": so fear Him and stand in awe of Him and give priority to fearing Him over fearing people, for He is the One who takes charge of the affairs of His servants and has taken them by their forelocks, and to Him they return and to Him they will come — and He will reward with a tremendous reward whoever gives precedence to fear and hope of Him over fear and hope of anyone else, and punish the disbelievers and those who allied with them with a dreadful punishment. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "God warns you against Himself" means He warns you against His anger and the severe torment He prepared for those who give their support to His enemies and who have enmity with His friends; and "to God is the final return" means the return is to Him and He will reward or punish each person according to their deeds. **[Maʿārif]** The wisdom behind the prohibition is that Islam does not see man as existing in this world like common animals or jungle trees which sprout, grow, flourish and die and that is the end of it; human life is purposeful, and every stage of it — eating, drinking, standing, sitting, sleeping, waking, even living and dying — revolves around a central purpose, as the Qurʾān makes human beings declare: "My prayer and my sacrifice and my life and my death are all for God, the Lord of the Worlds" (6:162). **[Maʿārif]** Those who are against that purpose are the worst enemies of man, which is why the Qurʾān says, "Surely Satan is your enemy, so take him as enemy" (35:6); and those who follow the alluring dictates of Satan and oppose the injunctions of God can hardly be the kind of people to deserve deep love and friendship.

**Reflection.** The verse draws a line, and the commentaries spend most of their energy on where exactly the line falls — because the prohibition is about the heart's allegiance, not about courtesy, trade or kindness **[Maʿārif]**, **[Study Quran]**. The occasion fixes the sense: men were being drawn away from their religion by people whose company they enjoyed, and the danger was drift rather than decision **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The exception is narrow and honest — words may be spoken under duress, but no deed that harms a Muslim is licensed by them, and the heart stays where it was **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**, **[Study Quran]**. And the examples the commentators reach for to show the other side are all from the Prophet's own life: feeding a famine-struck enemy, freeing a conquered one, housing a delegation that had not yet believed — which is the surest reading of what the verse is forbidding, and of what it is not **[Maʿārif]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

## 3:29 — قُلْ إِن تُخْفُوا۟ مَا فِى صُدُورِكُمْ أَوْ تُبْدُوهُ يَعْلَمْهُ ٱللَّهُ ۗ وَيَعْلَمُ مَا فِى ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَمَا فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍۢ قَدِيرٌۭ

*Say: "Whether you hide what is in your breasts or disclose it, God knows it; and He knows what is in the heavens and what is in the earth; and God is powerful over everything."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: say, O Muḥammad, to those whom I commanded not to take the disbelievers as allies in place of the believers — "if you hide what is in your breasts", of alliance with the disbelievers, keeping it secret, "or disclose it", from yourselves with your tongues and your deeds, making it manifest, "God knows it", and it is not hidden from Him. **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is: do not harbour affection for them inwardly nor show alliance to them outwardly, lest there reach you of your Lord's punishment what you have no strength to bear — for He knows your secret and your open and nothing of it is hidden from Him, and He is counting it against you until He recompenses you for it: good with good, and an evil deed with its like. **[Jalālayn]** "Say to them: 'Whether you hide what is in your breasts, in your hearts, of patronage to them, or disclose it, manifest it, God knows it; and He knows what is in the heavens and what is in the earth; and God is able to do all things' — and this includes punishing those who patronise them." **[Ibn Kathīr]** God tells His servants that He knows the secrets and the apparent matters and that nothing concerning them escapes His observation; rather, His knowledge encompasses them in every condition, time, day and instant, and encompasses all that is in heaven and earth, and nothing escapes it — not the weight of an atom, nor anything smaller than that, in the earth, the seas or the mountains. **[Saʿdī]** God informs us that His knowledge encompasses what is in the breasts, whether His servants hide it or disclose it, just as His knowledge encompasses everything in the heavens and the earth, so that nothing hidden is hidden from Him; and along with His all-encompassing knowledge He is the Tremendous, powerful over everything, and no existent thing is beyond His will. **[Qurṭubī]** He is the Knower of the hidden things of breasts and of what they contain, and of what is in the heavens and the earth and what they hold — Knower of the unseen, from whom not the weight of a mote escapes and from whom nothing is absent; Glory to Him, there is no god but He, Knower of the unseen and the seen. **[Maʿārif]** Since close friendship relates to the heart, and the affairs of the heart are known to none but God, it is possible that a person may in fact be intensely in love with and friendly towards disbelievers yet deny it verbally — and so this verse has already covered it by saying, "whether you conceal what is in your hearts or disclose it, God shall know it". No denial or false claim is tenable before Him.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** On "and He knows what is in the heavens and what is in the earth": since nothing in a heaven or an earth, or wherever it may be, is hidden from Him, how could there be hidden from Him — O you who take the disbelievers as allies in place of the believers — what is in your breasts of inclination towards them in affection and love, or what you make manifest to them of aid in deed and word? **[Ṭabarī]** On "and God is powerful over everything": God is able to hasten the punishment upon you for your allying with them and your backing them against the believers, and for whatever else He wills of all matters; nothing He intends is beyond Him and nothing He seeks is withheld from Him. **[Study Quran]** "God is Powerful over all things" — mention of His omniscience is often combined in the Qurʾān with mention of His omnipotence.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits from al-Suddī, through Asbāṭ: He informed them that He knows what they kept secret of that and what they made public, and so He said, "whether you hide what is in your breasts or disclose it". **[Ibn Kathīr]** This verse alerts God's servants that they should fear Him enough not to commit what He prohibits and dislikes, for He has perfect knowledge of all they do and is able to punish them promptly; and He gives respite to some of them and then punishes them, and He is swift and mighty in taking account.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** Compare 28:69; and see 31:16 — "if it be but the weight of a mustard seed, be it in a rock, in the heavens or on the earth, God will bring it forth". **[Study Quran]** The Qurʾān frequently describes how God knows what lies within breasts (3:119; 5:7; 8:43; 11:5; 29:10; 31:23; 39:7; 40:19; 42:24; 57:6; 64:4; 67:13; 100:10). **[Ṭabarī]** He reads the verse as addressed specifically to those commanded in 3:28.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** His knowledge encompasses all things and His ability encompasses everything. **[Saʿdī]** Along with all-encompassing knowledge, He is the Tremendous, powerful over everything, and nothing that exists is beyond His will. **[Qurṭubī]** Not the weight of a mote escapes Him and nothing is absent from Him.

**Reflection.** The verse closes the loophole that the previous verse's exception opened: if *taqiyyah* is permitted in words, and the heart is where allegiance really lives, then someone could say all the right things and mean all the wrong ones — and this is the verse that says such a person is not hidden **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The argument runs from the greater to the lesser: He knows the heavens and the earth, so how could He not know a leaning in a breast? — a move that makes concealment look foolish rather than merely wrong **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Study Quran]**. It ends with power rather than with knowledge alone, because knowledge that cannot act would not be a warning **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. Read in sequence, then, the three verses say: do not ally with them; if you must speak, your heart is still yours; and your heart is the thing being watched **[Jalālayn]**, **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:30 — يَوْمَ تَجِدُ كُلُّ نَفْسٍۢ مَّا عَمِلَتْ مِنْ خَيْرٍۢ مُّحْضَرًۭا وَمَا عَمِلَتْ مِن سُوٓءٍۢ تَوَدُّ لَوْ أَنَّ بَيْنَهَا وَبَيْنَهُۥٓ أَمَدًۢا بَعِيدًۭا ۗ وَيُحَذِّرُكُمُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسَهُۥ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ رَءُوفٌۢ بِٱلْعِبَادِ

*On the Day when every soul finds what it has done of good brought present, and what it has done of evil — it will wish that between it and that there were a far distance. God warns you of Himself, and God is Kind to the servants.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: and God warns you of Himself on a day when every soul finds what it has done of good brought present, made full, "and what it has done of evil, it will wish that between it and that there were a far distance" — that is, a far goal — for your return, O people, on that Day is to Him, so beware for yourselves because of your sins. **[Jalālayn]** "And remember the day every soul shall find what it has done of good present before it, and what it has done of evil — the last statement constitutes the subject, the predicate of which is what follows — it will wish that between it and that there were a great distance, an extremely lengthy distance, so that it, the evil, could never reach it. God warns you of His Self — this is repeated for emphasis — and God is Kind to His servants." **[Ibn Kathīr]** On the Day of Resurrection God brings the good and the evil deeds before the servant, just as He said, "on that Day man will be informed of what he sent forward and what he left behind" (75:13); and when the servant sees his good deeds he becomes happy and delighted, and when he sees the evil deeds he committed he becomes sad and angry, and then he will wish that he could disown his evil work and that a long distance separated it from him. **[Saʿdī]** Having mentioned of His greatness and the vastness of His attributes what obliges His servants to be mindful of Him in every state of theirs, He mentions also another motive to mindfulness and godliness: that all of them are coming to Him, and their deeds, of good and evil, will be brought present on that Day. Then the people of good will rejoice in what they sent ahead for themselves, and the people of evil will grieve when they find what they did brought present, and will wish that between them and it there were a far distance. **[Maʿārif]** The verse says that God warns you of Himself lest you should indulge in friendship with disbelievers for the sake of fleeting interests and objectives and thus invite the anger of God. **[Study Quran]** Some understand "on the day when" as a continuation of the warning in 3:28, so that together they would mean "God warns you about Himself concerning a day when…"; others connect it to "the journey's end" in 3:28 — "and unto God is the journey's end… on the day when…"; still others read it as having an elided verb, meaning something like "and remember a day when…".

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said of "brought present" (*muḥḍaran*): "made full" (*muwaffaran*). **[Ṭabarī]** Some of the Arabists held that the sense is "and remember the day when every soul finds…", and that it came in this form because the Qurʾān was sent down for command and remembrance, as though it were said to them "remember such and such", since the Qurʾān says in more than one place "fear a day such and such" and "at a time such and such". **[Ṭabarī]** The *mā* with "it has done" is in the sense of "that which", and cannot be a conditional, because "it finds" bears upon it; and "and what it has done of evil" is conjoined to the first *mā*, with "it has done" as a relative clause in the position of a nominative since "it will wish" was said. **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is therefore: on the day when every soul finds that which it did of good brought present, and that which it did of evil, it will wish that between it and that there were a far distance. **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-amad* is the goal at which one arrives, as in the line of al-Ṭirimmāḥ: "every living thing completes the full tale of its life, and perishes when its *amad* is spent". **[Qurṭubī]** "The day" is in the accusative, attached either to "God warns you of Himself", or to "and to God is the homecoming", or to "and God is powerful over everything"; and it may also be detached, with an implied "remember", as in "God is Mighty, Lord of retribution — on the day when the earth is changed" (14:47–48). **[Qurṭubī]** "Brought present" is a circumstantial phrase referring to the pronoun elided from the relative clause — "on the day when every soul finds that which it did of good brought present" — this assuming "finds" is the finding of a lost thing; alternatively, if "finds" means "knows", then "brought present" is the second object, and "it will wish" likewise stands as a second object. **[Qurṭubī]** The second *mā* may also be in the nominative as a subject with "it will wish" as its predicate; it cannot be a conditional, because "it will wish" is in the nominative. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-amad* is the goal, its plural *āmād*; one says "he gained the *amad*", meaning he won by coming first, as in the line of al-Nābighah: "only for the like of you, or for one you outstrip, as the racehorse outstrips when it gains the *amad*". **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-amad* is also anger: one says "he was angry" (*amida amadan*) when he grew angry. **[Study Quran]** "Term" (*amad*) in this context refers not to a span of time but to an appointed moment, and so the wish here is for a postponement.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 75:13, "on that Day man will be informed of what he sent forward and what he left behind"; and 43:38, which he says the servant will address to the devil who used to accompany him and encourage him to evil: "would that between me and you were the distance of the two easts — a horrible companion indeed!" **[Study Quran]** The verse evokes 99:6–8, when human beings will see every mote's weight of evil or good they committed, and 18:49, when the book will be handed to them in the Hereafter in which they will find present whatsoever they did. **[Study Quran]** Of related meaning is 43:38: "Till, when he comes unto Us, he will say, 'Would that there were between me and Thee the distance of the two easts!'" **[Qurṭubī]** 14:47–48 for the detached accusative with an implied verb.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And God warns you against Himself" means He warns you against His punishment; and then, bringing hope to His servants so that they do not despair of His mercy or feel hopeless of His kindness, He says "and God is full of kindness with the servants". **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī said: "God is so kind with them that He warns them against Himself." Others said: "He is merciful with His creation and likes for them to remain on His straight path and chosen religion and to follow His honourable Messenger." **[Saʿdī]** Despite the severity of His punishment, He is Kind and Merciful — and of His kindness and mercy it is that He has frightened His servants and deterred them from error and corruption, as He said when mentioning the punishments, "that is what God frightens His servants with: so fear Me, O My servants". His kindness and mercy have smoothed for them the paths by which they attain good things, and His kindness and mercy have warned them against the paths that lead to what is hateful. **[Jalālayn]** "God warns you of His Self" is repeated here for emphasis, and the verse closes with His kindness. **[Ṭabarī]** The verse is read as God's warning of Himself on that day, with the return of all people to Him.

**Reflection.** Two warnings in three verses, and the second one ends with the word "Kind" — which is the whole shape of the passage: the same God who threatens is the God who bothers to threaten, and the warning is itself the mercy **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The scene is drawn with domestic precision: not a speech from a throne but a person coming face to face with what they did, glad at one part of the file and wishing the other part were on another continent **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. The wish is for distance, not for a defence — nobody in the picture argues, because there is nothing to argue about when the deed is standing there **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. And the closing name is placed deliberately after the threat: fear and hope are meant to arrive together, since either one alone would distort the life the verse is trying to produce **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**, **[Maʿārif]**.

## 3:31 — قُلْ إِن كُنتُمْ تُحِبُّونَ ٱللَّهَ فَٱتَّبِعُونِى يُحْبِبْكُمُ ٱللَّهُ وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ

*Say: "If you love God, then follow me; God will love you and forgive you your sins. And God is Forgiving, Merciful."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The commentators differed over the occasion of the verse. **[Ṭabarī]** Some said it was revealed concerning people who said in the Prophet's time, "We love our Lord"; and God then commanded His prophet to say to them: "If you are truthful in what you say, then follow me, for that is the mark of your truthfulness." He transmits this from al-Ḥasan through two chains, and through Ibn Jurayj: there were people who claimed that they loved God, saying "we love our Lord", and God commanded them to follow Muḥammad and made following him the mark of His love. **[Ṭabarī]** He also transmits from al-Ḥasan, through ʿAbbād b. Manṣūr: some people in the time of the Messenger of God claimed that they loved God, and God wanted to make for their saying a confirmation in deed, so He said this; and following Muḥammad was the confirmation of their saying. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said it is a command to the Prophet to say to the delegation of Christians from Najrān: if what you say about Jesus is said out of veneration for God and love of Him, then follow Muḥammad — transmitted from Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr through Ibn Isḥāq. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers the second: no mention has occurred in this sūrah, before this verse, of any people who claimed to love God or to venerate Him, such that this verse would be an answer to them on the view of al-Ḥasan; and there is no sound report establishing the first occasion. Since whatever precedes and follows in the sūrah is news about the Najrān delegation and God's argument against their claim about the Messiah, this verse too must be turned to the same meaning. **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is therefore: say, O Muḥammad, to the delegation of the Christians of Najrān — if you are as you claim, loving God, and you venerate the Messiah and say about him what you say out of love for your Lord, then make your saying true, if you are truthful, by following me; for you know that I am God's messenger to you as Jesus was a messenger to those he was sent to. And if you follow me and affirm me in what I have brought you from God, He will forgive you your sins and pardon you the punishment for them and efface what has passed of them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** This noble verse judges against everyone who claims to love God yet does not follow the way of Muḥammad; such a person is not true in his claim until he follows the *sharīʿah* of Muḥammad and his religion in all his statements, actions and states. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Allah will love you" means: what you will earn is much greater than what you sought in loving Him, for God will love you. **[Jalālayn]** "Say, O Muḥammad: 'If you love God, follow me and God will love you' — meaning that He will reward you — 'and forgive you your sins; God is Forgiving as regards the sins committed previously by one who now follows me; Merciful to him.'" **[Saʿdī]** This verse is the balance by which one who truly loves God is known from one who merely claims it: the mark of the love of God is following Muḥammad, whose following, and all that he calls to, has been made the path to His love and good pleasure. The love of God, His good pleasure and His reward are attained only by affirming what the Messenger brought of the Book and the Sunnah, complying with their command and avoiding their prohibition; whoever does that, God loves him and rewards him with the reward of the lovers, forgives his sins and covers his faults. **[Maʿārif]** Love is something hidden — whether a person loves another, and more or less, cannot be answered precisely, and there is no measure to determine the truth of the matter except an educated guess based on visible conditions and dealings, for love leaves traces and signs by which it can be recognised. **[Maʿārif]** In these verses God tells those who claimed that they loved God and hoped to be loved by Him the real criterion of His love: if a person claims that he loves his Creator and Master, he must test it on the touchstone of his obedience to the Messenger, matching it against the frequency and quality of where and how he has followed him; and the genuine and the fake will be exposed right there.

**Context.** **[Jalālayn]** The verse was revealed when they said, "We only worship idols out of our love for God, that they might bring us close to Him." **[Qurṭubī]** It was revealed concerning the delegation of Najrān when they claimed that what they alleged about Jesus was love for God — this being the saying of Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan and Ibn Jurayj said it was revealed concerning people of the People of the Book who said, "We are the ones who love our Lord." **[Qurṭubī]** It is also related that the Muslims said, "O Messenger of God, by God we love our Lord," and God sent down "Say: if you love God, then follow me". **[Ṭabarī]** He weighs the two occasions and prefers the Najrān reading on the grounds of the sūrah's internal coherence.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-ḥubb* is love, as is *al-ḥibb* with *kasrah*; *al-ḥibb* is also the beloved, like *al-khidn* and *al-khadīn*. One says "he loved him" (*aḥabbahu*) and he is a lover (*muḥibb*), and "he loved him" (*ḥabbahu*, with *kasrah*) and he is beloved (*maḥbūb*) — and al-Jawharī called this last irregular. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿArafah said: love, with the Arabs, is willing a thing with intent towards it. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Azharī said: the servant's love of God and His messenger is obeying the two of them and following their command, as in this verse; and God's love of His servants is His favouring them with forgiveness, as in "God does not love the disbelievers" — that is, He does not forgive them. **[Study Quran]** The verse is often cited in this connection; God's love is likewise sought and described elsewhere in the Qurʾān.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** It is recorded in the *Ṣaḥīḥ* that the Messenger of God said: "Whoever performs a deed that does not conform to our matter (religion), it will be rejected of him." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and several of the Salaf said: some people claimed that they loved God, so God tested them with this verse. **[Maʿārif]** The Prophet said: "He who obeyed Muḥammad obeyed God, and he who disobeyed Muḥammad disobeyed God." **[Qurṭubī]** Abū al-Dardāʾ related that the Messenger of God said of "then follow me, God will love you" — "upon righteousness, God-fearingness, humility and lowliness of the soul" — transmitted by al-Tirmidhī. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that he said: "Whoever wants God to love him, let him be truthful in speech, render back the trust, and not harm his neighbour." **[Qurṭubī]** In the *Ṣaḥīḥ* of Muslim, from Abū Hurayrah: when God loves a servant He calls Gabriel and says, "I love so-and-so, so love him," and Gabriel loves him, then a call goes out in heaven, "God loves so-and-so, so love him," and the people of heaven love him, and then acceptance is placed for him on earth; and when He hates a servant He calls Gabriel and says, "I hate so-and-so, so hate him," and Gabriel hates him, then a call goes out among the people of heaven, and they hate him, and then hatred is placed for him on earth. **[Qurṭubī]** Sahl b. ʿAbdallāh said: the mark of the love of God is love of the Qurʾān; the mark of love of the Qurʾān is love of the Prophet; the mark of love of the Prophet is love of the Sunnah; the mark of love of God, the Qurʾān, the Prophet and the Sunnah is love of the Hereafter; the mark of love of the Hereafter is to love one's own self; the mark of loving one's self is to hate this world; and the mark of hating this world is to take from it nothing but provision and sufficiency. *(digression: Sahl's chain of marks)*
