**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** Abū Rajāʾ al-ʿUṭāridī read *fa-ittabiʿūnī* with the *bāʾ* opened; "and He will forgive you" is conjoined to "He will love you". **[Qurṭubī]** Maḥbūb related from Abū ʿAmr b. al-ʿAlāʾ that he assimilated the *rāʾ* of *yaghfir* into the *lām* of *lakum*; al-Naḥḥās said al-Khalīl and Sībawayhi do not permit assimilating *rāʾ* into *lām*, but Abū ʿAmr is too great to err in such a thing, and he was probably hiding the vowel as he does in many cases.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** "God is Oft-Forgiving, Most Merciful" — by your following the Messenger you will earn all this through the blessing of his mission. **[Ṭabarī]** God forgives the sins of His believing servants and is merciful to them and to others of His creation. **[Maʿārif]** The verse establishes that denial of prophethood or refusal to obey the Prophet is an act of unbelief like the denial of God's oneness.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** He points forward to 3:81, "and when God took the covenant of the prophets", for the point that if the previous prophets and mighty messengers had been alive in his time they would have had no choice but to follow him. **[Qurṭubī]** 5:54, "He loves them and they love Him", cited as linguistic evidence for the form *yuḥibbukum*. **[Qurṭubī]** 3:32, "God does not love the disbelievers", read as "He does not forgive them".

**Reflection.** The verse turns an inward claim into a public test: love of God, which nobody can audit, is given an observable mark — following the Messenger **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. It is addressed to people who were entirely sincere in their language: the Najrān Christians said their high doctrine of Jesus was reverence, and the verse takes them at their word and asks what reverence costs **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The promise is asymmetric in the believer's favour — you aimed at loving God and you get God loving you, which is worth more than what you were reaching for **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. And the hadith of Gabriel places the whole thing in a cosmos: acceptance set down on earth for the one God loves, which means the love named here is not a private feeling but a public standing **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:32 — قُلْ أَطِيعُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَٱلرَّسُولَ ۖ فَإِن تَوَلَّوْا۟ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَا يُحِبُّ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ

*Say: "Obey God and the Messenger." But if they turn away — God does not love the disbelievers.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: say, O Muḥammad, to this delegation of the Christians of Najrān — obey God, and obey the Messenger, Muḥammad; for you know with certainty that he is My messenger to My creatures, whom I have sent with the truth, and you find him written with you in the Gospel. And if they turn away — if they turn their backs on what you called them to and turn aside from it — then inform them that God does not love those who disbelieve by rejecting what they have recognised of the truth and denying it after knowing it, and that they are of them, by their rejection of your prophethood and their denial of the truth you are upon after knowing the soundness of your affair and the reality of your prophethood. **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits this from Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr through Ibn Isḥāq: "Say: obey God and the Messenger" — you know him, meaning the delegation of the Christians of Najrān, and you find him in your Book — "and if they turn away" upon their unbelief, "then God does not love the disbelievers". **[Jalālayn]** "Say to them: 'Obey God and the Messenger' as regards belief in the oneness of God which he enjoins upon you. But if they turn their backs — if they object to obedience — God loves not the disbelievers, meaning that He will chastise them." **[Saʿdī]** It is as though it were said: and with all that, what is the reality of following the Messenger and its description? And He answered with His saying "Say: obey God and the Messenger" — by complying with the command, avoiding the prohibition and affirming the report; "and if they turn away" from that, then this is unbelief, and God "does not love the disbelievers". **[Ibn Kathīr]** God next commands everyone: "Say: obey God and the Messenger. But if they turn away" — by defying the Prophet — "then God does not love the disbelievers", thus testifying that defiance of the Messenger's way constitutes unbelief. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God does not love whoever does this, even if he claims that he loves God and seeks a means of approach to Him, unless and until he follows the unlettered Prophet, the Final Messenger from God to the two creations, mankind and jinn. **[Maʿārif]** The verse belongs to the same passage as 3:31, in which belief in prophethood and the necessity of following the Messenger are affirmed alongside the affirmation of God's oneness.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "But if they turn away" is a condition, though it is in the perfect tense and so is not inflected; the underlying sense is "and if they turn away upon their unbelief and turn aside from obeying God and His Messenger". **[Qurṭubī]** He said "God does not love" and did not say "He does not love", because the Arabs, when they magnify a thing, repeat the mention of it; and Sībawayhi cited the line, "I do not see death outstripping death in anything — death embittered the rich and the poor alike". **[Jalālayn]** The third-person pronominalisation is replaced by the overt noun "the disbelievers".

*Source note:* Maʿārif and Ibn Kathīr each treat 3:31–32 as a single unit; the Maʿārif material on the criterion of love and the Ibn Kathīr material on the "deed that does not conform to our matter" are cited at 3:31, and the parts of both that bear on obedience and on unbelief are cited here.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** He defers the detailed exposition of "obey God and the Messenger" to his commentary on Sūrah al-Nisāʾ. **[Saʿdī]** Obedience is spelt out as three things: complying with the command, keeping away from the prohibition, and affirming the report — so that the verse is at once a rule of action and a rule of belief.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Defiance of the Messenger's way is unbelief, and God does not love whoever does it, whatever he claims about loving God. **[Ṭabarī]** What is condemned is rejecting what one has recognised of the truth and denying it after knowledge — unbelief as refusal rather than as ignorance. **[Saʿdī]** Turning away from obeying God and the Messenger *is* the unbelief named in the verse. **[Study Quran]** Such passages describe obedience to the Prophet as second only to obedience to God and set in place the normative character of the Prophet's teachings and actions. **[Study Quran]** Conformity with the sayings and doings of the Prophet is an essential aspect of Islamic Law, and gave rise to the genre of *aḥādīth* that records the Sunnah, or Wont of the Prophet. **[Study Quran]** For the Sufis the Prophet is a living presence through whom God acts to provide the spiritual traveller the strength to move along the path toward God. **[Study Quran]** In the eyes of all Muslims, those who claim to love God and His Prophet but fail to follow them fall short in their faith.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 4:80, "Whosoever obeys the Messenger obeys God"; 4:59, "Obey God and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you"; 3:132, 5:92, 8:2, 8:20, 8:46, 24:54, 33:33, 47:33, 49:14 and 64:12 for the parallel obedience verses; and 33:21, "Indeed, you have in the Messenger of God a beautiful example". **[Qurṭubī]** He refers to Sūrah al-Nisāʾ for the fuller treatment of obedience to God and the Messenger. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He points forward to 3:81 for the covenant of the prophets. **[Ṭabarī]** He reads the verse as continuing the address to the Najrān delegation opened at 3:31.

**Reflection.** Having been offered the highest possible motive at 3:31 — be loved by God — the addressees are now given the plain command, as though love were tested precisely at the point where a command is unwelcome **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The verse names the sin as *turning away* rather than as denying: it is a movement of the back, not an argument, and it is diagnosed as unbelief even in people who would describe themselves as lovers of God **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The shift from "He" to "God" in the closing clause is noticed by the grammarians as a mark of magnification — the sentence will not let the subject be a pronoun **[Qurṭubī]**. And the address is to people who possess a scripture that names the Messenger, which is why the verse can say that they know, and be severe about it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**.

## 3:33 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ ٱصْطَفَىٰٓ ءَادَمَ وَنُوحًۭا وَءَالَ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَءَالَ عِمْرَٰنَ عَلَى ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ

*God chose Adam and Noah and the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān above all the worlds.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: God elected Adam and Noah and chose them for their religion, and the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān for the religion they were upon — because they were people of submission (*islām*) — and God informed us that He chose the religion of those He mentioned above all the religions that opposed it. **[Ṭabarī]** By "the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān" He means the believers. **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās, through ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭalḥah: they are the believers of the house of Abraham, the house of ʿImrān, the house of Yāsīn and the house of Muḥammad, as God says, "the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him" (3:68) — and they are the believers. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: two prophets whom God chose above all the worlds. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said elsewhere: God mentioned two righteous houses and two righteous men and preferred them above all the worlds, and Muḥammad was of the house of Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ḥasan said: God preferred them above all the worlds by prophethood, above all people; they were the prophets, God-fearing, chosen for their Lord. **[Jalālayn]** "God preferred — He has chosen — Adam and Noah and the House of Abraham and the House of ʿImrān, meaning He preferred their selves, that is Abraham and ʿImrān, above the worlds, by making prophethood reside in them and their progeny." **[Ibn Kathīr]** God states that He has chosen these households over the people of the earth: He chose Adam, created him with His hand, blew life into him, commanded the angels to prostrate before him, taught him the names of everything and let him dwell in Paradise, then sent him down from it out of His wisdom. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He chose Noah and made him the first messenger to the people of the earth, at a time when people worshipped idols and associated others with God; He avenged the way Noah was treated after he called his people day and night, publicly and secretly, for a very long time, and His calling only made them shun him more — and then Noah supplicated against them, and God drowned them, and none was saved except those who followed the religion He had sent to Noah. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He chose the household of Abraham, among them the master of all mankind and the Final Prophet, Muḥammad; and He chose the household of ʿImrān, the father of Mary daughter of ʿImrān, the mother of Jesus — so Jesus is of the offspring of Abraham. **[Saʿdī]** God has, among His servants, chosen ones whom He selects and singles out, favouring them with lofty virtues, sublime attributes, beneficial knowledge, righteous deeds and varied distinctions; and He mentioned these great houses and the perfect men they contained, who gathered the attributes of perfection, and that excellence and good ran on through their descendants, taking in their males and their females — and this is among the greatest of His favours and the best sites of His generosity and bounty. **[Saʿdī]** "And God is Hearing, Knowing" — He knows who deserves favour and preferment, and places His favour where His wisdom requires. **[Study Quran]** The language of "being chosen by God" is used in connection with several figures in the Qurʾān (3:42; 6:84–87; 7:144; 38:47); one of the names of the Prophet Muḥammad is *al-Muṣṭafā*, "the Chosen One". **[Study Quran]** "House" is used here in the sense of familial line.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Iṣṭafā* means "He chose"; the underlying sense is that God chose *their religion*, which is the religion of submission, with the annexed word elided. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj said: He chose them for prophethood above the worlds of their time. **[Qurṭubī]** On *Nūḥ*: it is said to be derived from "he wailed" (*nāḥa yanūḥu*), and is a non-Arabic name that is nevertheless fully declinable because it is of three letters; he is the elder of the messengers and the first messenger God sent to the people of the earth after Adam. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that he was sent to forbid daughters, sisters, paternal and maternal aunts and the rest of the close kin; and whoever says, among the historians, that Idrīs was before him has erred. **[Study Quran]** "Above the worlds" could also be read as "above all peoples" and is used in connection with other figures as well (2:47; 6:86; 7:140).

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** In al-Bukhārī, from Ibn ʿAbbās: "the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān" are the believers among the house of Abraham, the house of ʿImrān, the house of Yāsīn and the house of Muḥammad — citing 3:68. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that the house of Abraham is Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the tribes, and that Muḥammad is of the house of Abraham; and it is said that "the house of Abraham" means Abraham himself, and likewise "the house of ʿImrān", as in "a remnant of what the house of Moses and the house of Aaron left" (2:248). **[Qurṭubī]** In the ḥadīth: "He has been given a flute of the flutes of the house of David."

**Context.** **[Maʿārif]** In these verses a reference has been made to some past prophets in order to comfort the Prophet ﷺ, since the disbelievers did not obey him because they doubted his prophethood; and it is for their guidance that the names of some past prophets have been cited, to help remove such doubts. **[Maʿārif]** However, these prophets have been referred to very briefly, because the main purpose is to mention Jesus, which follows immediately — for he will come again to this world and will work with the followers of the Prophet ﷺ, and it is therefore necessary for the Muslims to know his identification. **[Maʿārif]** The mention of these houses thus serves both to console the Prophet ﷺ and to prepare the reader for what is about to be said about the Messiah.

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** 3:68, "the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him". **[Qurṭubī]** 2:248; and he refers to his treatment of the word "house" in al-Baqarah and of the derivation of Adam there. **[Study Quran]** 3:42; 6:84–87; 7:144; 38:47; 2:47; 6:86; 7:140; 21:71. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He refers forward to Sūrah al-Anʿām for the point that Jesus is of the offspring of Abraham.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** What is chosen is the religion they were upon — submission — and God chose it above every religion that opposed it; and the "house" of a man is his followers, his people and whoever is upon his religion. **[Saʿdī]** God knows who deserves favour and preferment and places His favour where His wisdom requires. **[Study Quran]** ʿImrān is the name of Mary's father (also at 3:35 and 66:12), who is not named in the Bible but is known in extra-Biblical literature as Joachim, "he whom Yahweh set up"; commentators who equate ʿImrān with the Hebrew Amram, the name of Moses' father, and so hold that both the father of Moses and the father of Mary were separately named ʿImrān, do not seem to be aware of the name Joachim.

**Reflection.** The sūrah turns here from argument to narrative, and it begins by saying what the stories are about: not miracle but election, a line of people God picked **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Study Quran]**. The choosing is stated as a choosing of *religion* first — Adam, Noah, then two houses — so that what is inherited down these houses is not blood but submission, which is why Ibn ʿAbbās can say the "house" means the believers **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Naming ʿImrān's house alongside Abraham's is the point of the whole passage: the family about to be told about is placed in the same chosen line as the patriarchs, and the argument with Najrān will be conducted from inside a shared reverence **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. And the verse closes by pairing hearing with knowledge — God heard the vow that is about to be made and knew what was in the heart that made it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**.

## 3:34 — ذُرِّيَّةًۭ بَعْضُهَا مِنۢ بَعْضٍۢ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ

*Progeny, one from another. And God is Hearing, Knowing.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: God chose the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān as "progeny, one from another". **[Ṭabarī]** The "one from another" lies in alliance in religion and in mutual support for submission and the truth, as God said, "the believing men and believing women are allies of one another" (9:71), and elsewhere, "the hypocrites, men and women, are of one another" (9:67) — meaning their religion is one and their way is one. So likewise "progeny, one from another" means: progeny the religion of one of whom is the religion of the other, their word one and their creed one in the singling out of God and obedience to Him. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: in intention, and deed, and sincerity, and the singling out of Him. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Akhfash said it is a circumstantial phrase — in the state of some of them being from others, that is, progeny some of whom are the child of others. **[Qurṭubī]** The Kūfans said it is a "cut" (accusative of specification); al-Zajjāj said it is a substitute, meaning "He chose progeny, one from another"; and the meaning of "one from another" is mutual support in religion, as in "the hypocrites, men and women, are of one another" (9:67) — meaning in misguidance — according to al-Ḥasan and Qatādah. **[Qurṭubī]** It is also said to mean in election, choosing and prophethood; and it is said to mean descent, which is the weakest of the readings. **[Jalālayn]** "As progeny, one from another" — and God is Hearing, Knowing. **[Saʿdī]** Excellence and good ran on through their descendants and took in their males and their females — and this is among the greatest of God's favours. **[Study Quran]** "As progeny, one from another" is understood in terms of bloodlines, in that the house of ʿImrān is descended from the house of Abraham, which is descended from Noah, who descended from Adam. **[Study Quran]** It is also understood as a spiritual filiation, in that these houses and figures are connected through their worship of and obedience to the One God. **[Study Quran]** The verse can also be read as a way of not elevating one family line over another, since the phrase rendered "one from another" appears as "the one of you is as the other" at 4:25.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Dhurriyyatan* is in the accusative as a "cut" from "the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān", because *dhurriyyah* is indefinite and "the house of ʿImrān" is definite; and if one said it was put in the accusative by the repetition of "the choosing" — the meaning being "He chose progeny, some of them from others" — that would also be correct. **[Qurṭubī]** The meaning and derivation of *dhurriyyah* were given in al-Baqarah. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ṭabarī records the view of al-Farrāʾ in his *Maʿānī al-Qurʾān* that the accusative is by the repetition of "the choosing". **[Qurṭubī]** He cross-refers to his commentary on 9:67 for the parallel phrase, and to al-Baqarah for the root of *dhurriyyah*. **[Ṭabarī]** The two permissible grammatical readings — the "cut" and the repetition of the verb — do not differ in sense, since both yield "He chose progeny, some of them from others".

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And God is Hearing, Knowing" means: God has hearing for the saying of the wife of ʿImrān, and has knowledge of what she concealed in herself when she vowed to Him what was in her womb, consecrated. **[Saʿdī]** God knows who deserves favour and preferment and places His favour where His wisdom requires. **[Study Quran]** The spiritual reading makes the connection between these houses a matter of worship and obedience rather than descent.

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** 9:71 and 9:67 for the parallel use of "one from another". **[Qurṭubī]** 9:67, and he refers back to al-Baqarah for *dhurriyyah*. **[Study Quran]** 4:25 for the alternative sense of the phrase. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He reads 3:33–34 together as a single statement about the chosen ones.

**Rulings.** **[Ṭabarī]** Because "the house of a man" is his followers, his people and whoever is upon his religion, membership of the house of Abraham and the house of ʿImrān in this verse is a matter of religion rather than of birth alone. **[Ṭabarī]** This is why Ibn ʿAbbās could identify "the house of Abraham" with the believers of the house of Abraham, the house of ʿImrān, the house of Yāsīn and the house of Muḥammad, on the basis of 3:68. **[Qurṭubī]** On the same principle the phrase "one from another" is glossed by al-Ḥasan and Qatādah as mutual support in religion, and by others as shared election, choosing and prophethood.

*Source note:* Maʿārif treats 3:33–34 as a single unit; its comments on the purpose of naming these prophets are cited above at 3:33, and it has no separate block for 3:34. The Study Quran likewise comments on 3:33–34 together, and the parts of its note that bear on "one from another" are cited at 3:34 above.

**Reflection.** The verse makes the family-tree do religious work: the line runs through people, but what travels along it is a creed, not a bloodline **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Study Quran]**. That is why al-Ḥasan and Qatādah can read "one from another" as shared intention, deed and sincerity rather than as mere descent, and why al-Qurṭubī records the descent reading only to call it the weakest **[Qurṭubī]**. It also quietly removes the basis for boasting: if membership is by worship and obedience, no house can claim the others **[Study Quran]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. And the closing pair of names is placed exactly where the story needs it — hearing for the vow that is about to be spoken, knowledge for the hope behind it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**.

## 3:35 — إِذْ قَالَتِ ٱمْرَأَتُ عِمْرَٰنَ رَبِّ إِنِّى نَذَرْتُ لَكَ مَا فِى بَطْنِى مُحَرَّرًۭا فَتَقَبَّلْ مِنِّىٓ ۖ إِنَّكَ أَنتَ ٱلسَّمِيعُ ٱلْعَلِيمُ

*When the wife of ʿImrān said: "My Lord, I have vowed to You what is in my womb, consecrated. So accept it from me; You are the Hearing, the Knowing."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is: when the wife of ʿImrān said, "My Lord, I have vowed to You what is in my womb, consecrated, so accept from me" — and the particle *idh* is connected to "Hearing" in the previous verse. **[Ṭabarī]** "The wife of ʿImrān" is the mother of Mary daughter of ʿImrān, the mother of Jesus; her name, as we have been told, was Ḥannah daughter of Fāqūdh. **[Ṭabarī]** Her husband ʿImrān was ʿImrān son of Yāshhum, of the line of David — transmitted from Ibn Isḥāq. **[Ṭabarī]** "I have vowed to You what is in my womb, consecrated" means: I have made it a vow binding on me that what is in my womb is Yours, freed (*muḥarrar*) for Your worship — that is, I have confined it to Your service and the service of Your sanctuary in the church, released from the service of everything other than You, and set apart for You alone. **[Ṭabarī]** *Muḥarraran* is in the accusative as a circumstantial phrase from the pronoun in the prepositional phrase. **[Jalālayn]** "When the wife of ʿImrān said: 'My Lord, I have vowed to You what is in my womb, freed for Your service, so accept it from me — and I never expected to bear a female — You are the Hearing, the Knowing.'" **[Ibn Kathīr]** The wife of ʿImrān mentioned here is the mother of Mary, and her name is Ḥannah bint Fāqūdh. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Isḥāq mentioned that Ḥannah could not have children, and that one day she saw a bird feeding its chick; she wished she could have children and supplicated to God to grant her offspring, and God accepted her supplication, and when her husband slept with her she became pregnant — and she vowed to make her child concentrate on worship and serving Bayt al-Maqdis, once she became aware that she was pregnant. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Verily, You are the All-Hearer, the All-Knowing" means: You hear my supplication and You know my intention; she did not know then what she would give birth to, a male or a female. **[Saʿdī]** The wife of ʿImrān said, humbling herself before her Lord and drawing near to Him with this act of nearness that He loves, in which is the magnification of His house and constancy in obeying Him: "I have vowed to You what is in my womb, consecrated" — that is, a servant for the house of worship, filled with those who devote themselves to worship; "so accept from me" this deed, that is, make it founded upon faith and sincerity, productive of good and reward; "You are the Hearing, the Knowing". **[Maʿārif]** In the religious code of the earlier prophets there was a form of worship through which a child, of one's children, could be freed from all worldly service and devoted exclusively to God; following this practice, the mother of Mary made a vow during her pregnancy that she would let the newborn serve Bayt al-Maqdis in the way of God and would not put the child to worldly chores. **[Maʿārif]** Mary's mother thought she would deliver a male child, but when she delivered a girl she was full of remorse, thinking that a girl would not be able to do this duty; but God, out of consideration for her sincerity, accepted that very girl and thereby made her status distinct from any other girl in the world. **[Study Quran]** "[Remember] when" renders the particle *idh*, which indicates attachment to something that has preceded; here it is said to be related, as is often the case, to the implied injunction "remember" or "mention". **[Study Quran]** It is reported that Mary's father, ʿImrān, died before she was born, and her mother, Ḥannah (Anne), promised to dedicate her child to the service of the Temple, to study and learn from the Book and to be free of other responsibilities outside of service and worship.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** Abū ʿUbaydah said *idh* is redundant; Muḥammad b. Yazīd said the underlying sense is "remember when"; al-Zajjāj said the meaning is "and He chose the house of ʿImrān when the wife of ʿImrān said". **[Qurṭubī]** She is Ḥannah — with the unpointed *ḥāʾ* and *nūn* — daughter of Fāqwadh son of Qunbul, the mother of Mary and the grandmother of Jesus; the name is not Arabic, and no woman's name Ḥannah is known in Arabic. **[Qurṭubī]** Among the Arabs there is Abū Ḥannah al-Badrī, of whom it is also said "Abū Ḥabbah" with a single *bāʾ*, which is more correct, his name being ʿĀmir; and there is Dayr Ḥannah in Syria as well as another monastery of the same name. *(digression: the Arabic lexicography of the name Ḥannah)*

*Source note:* Qurṭubī treats 3:35–36 as a single unit and Maʿārif likewise; the material bearing on the vow, the naming and the mother's guardianship is cited at 3:35, and the material bearing on the birth and on *ʿaqīqah* at 3:36. Saʿdī comments on 3:33–55 as one continuous passage, and his remarks are divided between the verses by content.

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** From this we learn that a mother holds a certain measure of guardianship over her child in respect of his or her education and training — for Mary's mother would never have vowed if the mother had no right of guardianship over her child. **[Maʿārif]** Similarly, it proves that a mother too has the right to give a name to her child.

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** He connects *idh* back to "Hearing" at 3:34. **[Qurṭubī]** 2:133 and his treatment of "chose" in al-Baqarah; and he refers to al-Baqarah for the meaning of "house". **[Study Quran]** 3:42 and 19:2–15 for the fuller narrative of Zachariah and John.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** The vow is described as an act of nearness that God loves, in which lies the magnification of His house and constancy in obeying Him; and the mother asks that it be founded upon faith and sincerity and made productive of good and reward. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God hears the supplication and knows the intention — the two names are invoked because the vow was spoken in uncertainty about what would be born. **[Study Quran]** The dedication is to service and worship, and to study of the Book, free of other responsibilities.

**Reflection.** The story begins with a woman who had been unable to conceive, watching a bird feed its young — the smallest possible scene, and the one that starts everything **[Ibn Kathīr]**. What she vows is not a portion but the whole: the child is to be set apart, released from every other service **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. She asks to have it accepted rather than simply given, which is the difference between a gift offered and a gift received, and the two names she invokes are exactly the two that fit — hearing for a prayer said aloud, knowledge for a hope not yet formed **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. And the vow is made before she knows what she is vowing, which is the whole of the drama: she will have to keep it when it turns out to be a daughter **[Maʿārif]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:36 — فَلَمَّا وَضَعَتْهَا قَالَتْ رَبِّ إِنِّى وَضَعْتُهَآ أُنثَىٰ وَٱللَّهُ أَعْلَمُ بِمَا وَضَعَتْ وَلَيْسَ ٱلذَّكَرُ كَٱلْأُنثَىٰ ۖ وَإِنِّى سَمَّيْتُهَا مَرْيَمَ وَإِنِّىٓ أُعِيذُهَا بِكَ وَذُرِّيَّتَهَا مِنَ ٱلشَّيْطَٰنِ ٱلرَّجِيمِ

*Then when she gave birth to her she said: "My Lord, I have given birth to a female" — and God knew best what she had given birth to — "and the male is not like the female. I have named her Mary, and I seek refuge for her and for her offspring with You from the accursed Shayṭān."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And when she gave birth to her" — when Ḥannah gave birth to the child she was carrying — "she said: My Lord, I have given birth to a female". **[Ṭabarī]** Ḥannah was hoping for a male when she vowed, because in their religion only males were dedicated, and when she gave birth to a female she grieved, saying what is described here, and asked God to accept her from her and to shelter her and her offspring from Shayṭān. **[Ṭabarī]** "And God knew best what she bore" — He knew better than she what she bore: whether it was male or female, and what He had in store for what she bore, of the bounty by which He honoured her above the women of the worlds by the Messiah whom He brought forth from her without a father, and by the sign He placed in her and her son. **[Jalālayn]** "She gave birth to a female" — and God was very knowing of what she gave birth to — "and the male is not like the female" as regards companionship in God's service. **[Jalālayn]** God then says "and I have called her Mary, and I commend her to You with her progeny, to protect them from the accursed Devil". **[Ibn Kathīr]** God tells about Ḥannah's condition when she gave birth: she said, "My Lord, verily I have given birth to a female" — this is an exclamation of regret over the birth of a female, as if apologising to God, because she had vowed what was in her womb to be free for the service of Bayt al-Maqdis, and a female in those times did not have the strength for the worship and service of Bayt al-Maqdis. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God then says "and God knew best what she bore" — as an exaltation of her status: do not think that she who gave birth to Mary is like other women who give birth to females; God knew best what she bore, and the boy she had wished for was not better than her. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "and the male is not like the female" — that is, do not think that by wishing for a boy I sought to replace the girl, for the boy she was given is not like the girl she was given. **[Ibn Kathīr]** She said: "And I have named her Mary" — this proves that it is permissible to name the newborn on the day of its birth, as is established in the *Ṣaḥīḥayn* that Anas b. Mālik took his brother, when his mother gave birth to him, to the Messenger of God, and the Messenger of God chewed a date and applied it to his palate and named him ʿAbdallāh. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I seek refuge with You for her and her offspring from the outcast Satan" — and God accepted her supplication. **[Saʿdī]** "And when she gave birth to a female" — and she had hoped for a male — "God knew very well what she had given birth to": do not think that He did not know that she was a female, or that she had given birth to something unfit for the purpose; rather He knew that what she had given birth to was better than a male and more perfect for the purpose, and God's knowledge encompasses the apparent and the hidden, the past and the future. **[Saʿdī]** "And the male is not like the female" is her own saying, by way of seeking acceptance and making excuses; and the meaning is that the male and the female are not alike in suitability for this act of worship and for the freeing required there, and that she hoped that this girl would be accepted in place of the male by God's grace and generosity. **[Saʿdī]** Then she named her Mary and sought protection for her and her progeny with God from the accursed Shayṭān. **[Maʿārif]** She was on the horns of a dilemma: she had made the vow, but she was expecting a male child who would be fit to serve Bayt al-Maqdis day and night, and the child born was a girl. **[Maʿārif]** Mary's mother thought that a girl would not be able to do this duty; but God, out of consideration for her sincerity, accepted that very girl and thereby made her status distinct from any other girl in the world — and the words "and God knew very well what she had given birth to" are a description of the vastness of Mary's station. **[Study Quran]** The phrase "and God knew best what she gave birth to" is understood as a rejoinder to Mary's mother's disappointment or regret at giving birth to a female rather than a male. **[Study Quran]** Some say the phrase "the male is not like the female" is a continuation of her words meaning that a male would also have been better suited to the task of service in the Temple; others understand it as a continuation of God's response, meaning the female that she gave birth to was superior to the male she had hoped for. **[Study Quran]** On this reading, the female may connote the superiority of the female she gave birth to over the male she had hoped for. **[Study Quran]** The commentator al-Zamakhsharī opines that the male she had hoped for was not as good as the female she was given, since she gave birth to one who was to be the mother of one of the greatest messengers.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** The pronoun in "and God knew best what she gave birth to" is read with *tāʾ* as the address of the mother, and with *yāʾ* as a report about her. **[Qurṭubī]** Whoever reads "wa-llāhu aʿlamu" says it is more eloquent than "I know better than you", because "I know better than you" would be polite address, whereas "God knows best" is exalting the matter; and it is said that the meaning is "God knows best what she gave birth to" as regards what came of it, and in what He singled her out for of honour — namely bearing the Messiah without a father. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-Unthā* is the female, from "softness" (*līn*) — the opposite of the male; its plural is *ināth* and *unuth*.

*Source note:* Maʿārif and Qurṭubī each treat 3:35–36 as a single unit; the material bearing on the vow and on the mother's guardianship is cited at 3:35, and the material bearing on the birth, the readings and the naming is cited here.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** The reading with *tāʾ* is that of the people of Madīnah and the people of Baṣrah, and it is a report from the mother; the reading with *yāʾ* is that of the rest, and it is God's saying. **[Qurṭubī]** This is the reading of Ibn Masʿūd, Ubayy and al-Kisāʾī. **[Qurṭubī]** Whoever reads the *tāʾ* makes it God's address to her — "God knows best what you have given birth to" — and God is not ignorant of the sex of the child before or after birth; the meaning is that He knew that what she gave birth to was better than what she had wished for.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** It is confirmed in the *Ṣaḥīḥayn* that Abū Hurayrah narrated that the Messenger of God said: "Every son of Adam is touched by Satan when he is born, and he starts crying because of this touch, except Mary and her son." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Abū Hurayrah then said: "Recite, if you wish: 'I seek refuge with You for her and her offspring from the outcast Satan'." **[Ibn Kathīr]** This ḥadīth is clear evidence that God protected Mary and her son from the touch of Satan, and this is among the things in which the supplication of Ḥannah was answered. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related in the Ṣaḥīḥ from ʿĀʾishah that the Messenger of God named Ḥasan and Ḥusayn and sacrificed a sheep for each of them on the seventh day, and the jurists of the people of Madīnah follow this. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn ʿUmar that the naming of children on the seventh day is recommended. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that the Messenger of God said: "Whoever has a child born to him and he names him Muḥammad, seeking blessing by his name, he and his child are in Paradise."

**Rulings.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The verse proves that it is permissible to name the newborn on the day of its birth, as is established in the *Ṣaḥīḥayn* from Anas b. Mālik. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ṭabarī related from Qatādah and al-Ḥasan that they permitted naming a child before the seventh day. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ṭabarī preferred naming on the seventh day, following the ḥadīth of Ḥasan and Ḥusayn, and said it is more preferable to us; and most of the people of knowledge go by his view. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ṭabarī mentioned the disagreement over who names the child when the parents differ and said that ʿAbdallāh b. ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ ruled that the father names. *(digression: the juristic detail on naming disputes)*

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:37, "so her Lord accepted her with goodly acceptance", and the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah. **[Qurṭubī]** He refers to al-Baqarah for the *ʿaqīqah* question. **[Study Quran]** 19:2–15 for the annunciation to Zachariah. **[Saʿdī]** 3:37 for the acceptance that follows.

**Reflection.** The sentence "and God knew best what she gave birth to" arrives like a correction spoken over a woman's disappointment, and it is the hinge of the entire passage: the child she is apologising for is the one God has chosen above the women of the worlds **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Whether "the male is not like the female" is her voice or God's, both readings end in the same place — that what she got was better than what she asked for **[Saʿdī]**, **[Study Quran]**. She does two things next: she names the child and she asks for shelter for her and for her descendants, and the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah is the record of that second prayer being answered in a way she could not have foreseen **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And the mother's agency here is real — she vows, she names, she seeks refuge — which is why the commentators can draw from this verse rulings about a mother's guardianship and right of naming (see 3:35) **[Maʿārif]**, **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:37 — فَتَقَبَّلَهَا رَبُّهَا بِقَبُولٍ حَسَنٍۢ وَأَنۢبَتَهَا نَبَاتًا حَسَنًۭا وَكَفَّلَهَا زَكَرِيَّا ۖ كُلَّمَا دَخَلَ عَلَيْهَا زَكَرِيَّا ٱلْمِحْرَابَ وَجَدَ عِندَهَا رِزْقًۭا ۖ قَالَ يَٰمَرْيَمُ أَنَّىٰ لَكِ هَٰذَا ۖ قَالَتْ هُوَ مِنْ عِندِ ٱللَّهِ ۖ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يَرْزُقُ مَن يَشَآءُ بِغَيْرِ حِسَابٍ

*So her Lord accepted her with a goodly acceptance, and made her grow a goodly growth, and placed her in the care of Zachariah. Whenever Zachariah entered the sanctuary to her, he found provision with her. He said: "O Mary, from where does this come to you?" She said: "It is from God. God provides for whom He wills without reckoning."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God says: and her Lord accepted the gift of Ḥannah with a goodly acceptance, and made Mary grow a goodly growth, and gave her into the care of Zachariah. **[Ṭabarī]** "And Zakariyyā took care of her" — the *hāʾ* in "kafallahā" refers to Mary, meaning: We made Zakariyyā her guardian in whose care she was. **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over the guardianship of Zakariyyā: some said it was because the child's mother died and the Israelites cast lots for her care, and Zakariyyā's lot came out, so he took charge of her. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: rather she was, so long as her mother was alive, in her care, and when her mother died her aunt — Zakariyyā's wife — took charge of her. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: rather Ḥannah brought her, when she was weaned, to the learned men of the children of Israel, who were the successors of Moses in the Temple, and they disputed about her; and the learned men drew lots with their pens, and Zakariyyā's lot came out, and he was her maternal aunt's husband, and so he took charge of her. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers this last: the report from the Messenger of God and the consensus of the people of tafsīr support it. **[Ṭabarī]** "Whenever Zakariyyā entered upon her in the sanctuary" — "the sanctuary" (*al-miḥrāb*) is the place of honour of kings and chiefs, the noblest place of their gatherings, and is the *miḥrāb* of the mosque. **[Ṭabarī]** That was because they used to build high places for the one whose rank they exalted, so that people would recognise his rank and know his place of eminence; and from this is the *miḥrāb* of the mosque, because it is the noblest place in it. **[Jalālayn]** "And He made her grow with a goodly growth" — and He put her in the custody of Zachariah, her maternal aunt's husband. **[Jalālayn]** "Whenever Zachariah entered the sanctuary to her he found that she had food" — "He said: 'O Mary, whence comes this to you?' She said: 'It is from God's bounty; surely God provides for whom He will without reckoning.'" **[Ibn Kathīr]** God states that He accepted Mary as a vowed gift for His service, and that He made her grow and develop in a good and complete manner and made good her affairs, out of His bounty to her. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And Zakariyyā took care of her" — this means God made it easy for Zakariyyā to take care of her and provided for her needs, and Zakariyyā was a prophet and a great man who was like a king over the Children of Israel, calling them to God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Several of the Salaf stated that Zakariyyā was the husband of Mary's maternal aunt, and others said that he was the husband of her sister; Ibn Isḥāq said her mother's sister was called Shabū. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Every time Zakariyyā entered upon her in the sanctuary" — meaning a private room, just as a host honours his guest with a private room. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "he found that she had food" — he found with her the fruits of summer in winter and the fruits of winter in summer. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "He said: 'O Mary! From where have you got this?' She said: 'This is from God'" — meaning, that which I have is from God, for He provides for whomever He wills without due measure. **[Saʿdī]** God accepted her and made her grow a goodly growth, and put her in the care of her maternal aunt's husband Zakariyyā — that is, He made Zakariyyā love her and be kind to her and ensure for her everything that would complete her upbringing and education. **[Maʿārif]** The words "wherefrom do you get this" show that Zakariyyā saw before him fruits that were out of season, and he therefore asked where they came from; Mary said, "This is from Allah," which is a description of the state of her heart — that is, this is not something strange, for it is from the One who is ever used to giving without measure, and His grace has no limit. **[Maʿārif]** Actually, the words of Mary are a confession and declaration of the omnipotence of God and a most perfect argument for it, and they also refute the slanders of those who thought that this was the result of magic or astrology. **[Study Quran]** "Her Lord accepted her with a fair acceptance" is understood to mean that God accepted her vow to serve in the Temple by allowing her to serve there, though she was female. **[Study Quran]** "Made her grow in a fair manner" means that God raised her in an excellent way and provided her a goodly, pure, healthy, and wholesome upbringing. **[Study Quran]** Stories of Mary's upbringing in the Temple have parallels in the *Protoevangelium of James*, an apocryphal Christian text, though there are also differences: in the *Protoevangelium*, Mary is raised in the Temple for only three years, until she is twelve, and then married off to Joseph, whereas Muslim accounts have Mary live in the Temple until she reaches puberty and then leave the service of the Temple because of menstruation. **[Study Quran]** Some say that Mary was fed by suckling her thumb, that the food is from Paradise, or that God provided Zakariyyā with sustenance for Mary when he could not; an inscription of this verse is said to have appeared over the *miḥrāb* of Mary in the mosque in Damascus. **[Study Quran]** "He said, 'O Mary, whence comes this to you?' She said, 'It is from God'" — this is an allusion to the fact that God provides sustenance (*rizq*) not just in the physical sense, but also in a spiritual sense, meaning knowledge, wisdom, obedience, and the purification of one's soul.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Qabūl* is the verbal noun derived from other than the form of the verb it is derived from: *qabila* — *qabūlan*, like *shakara shukūran* and *kafara kufrānan*, and one says *qabūl* and *qibāl* with *kasrah*; *qabūl* is the saying of Abū ʿAmr and is the more eloquent. **[Qurṭubī]** *Anbatahā nabātan ḥasanan* is likewise the verbal noun derived from other than the form of its verb: *nabata* — *nabātan*, like *shaʾama shāman* and *rajʿa rujʿan*; and *nabāt* is the name for what grows from the earth, and the imperative of *anbata* is *anbit* and of *nabata* is *unbut* — with *ḍammah*. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-miḥrāb* was discussed at 2:91 and at length in his treatment of Dāwūd, where he said that the Arabs call it the *ghurfah* (upper room). **[Qurṭubī]** The *tāʾ* of *qālat* is sound, inserted in the verb because it is a woman's speech.

*Source note:* Qurṭubī treats 3:37–38 as a single unit; the grammatical and narrative material bearing on Mary's upbringing is cited here, and the material bearing on Zachariah's supplication at 3:38.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Wa-kaffalahā* is the reading of the people of Madīnah and the people of Makkah, and *kafalahā* — with *takhfīf* — is the reading of the people of Kūfah; whoever lightens it makes the verb belong to Zakariyyā, and the sense is "and Zakariyyā took care of her" (*kafalahā*), and whoever intensifies it makes the verb belong to God. **[Qurṭubī]** All of them read *wa-kaffalahā*, and al-Ḥasan read *kafalahā* with *takhfīf*; and al-Zajjāj said the meaning is "and God caused Zakariyyā to take care of her", so it is in the accusative by the elision of the preposition.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: "He made her grow a goodly growth" means He put her on the path of the felicitous. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan said: God never punished her for an hour of the night or the day — a report transmitted by ʿAbūd b. Manṣūr and Yūnus. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī said: never was she given to the breasts, but God made for her provision without that. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: he found with her the fruits of winter in summer and the fruits of summer in winter. **[Qurṭubī]** Mujāhid said: he used to find with her, in the height of summer, the fruits of winter, and in the depth of winter the fruits of summer. **[Qurṭubī]** ʿIkrimah said: he found with her fruits of the fruits of Paradise. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that Zakariyyā found with her, in the winter, grapes and fresh dates, and in the summer, cucumbers and pomegranates. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the reports about the identity of the out-of-season provision)*

**Belief.** **[Study Quran]** The provision is understood by some as spiritual — knowledge, wisdom, obedience and the purification of the soul — and not only physical. **[Maʿārif]** Mary's answer is a confession and declaration of God's omnipotence and a most perfect argument for it, and it refutes the slanders of those who thought this was the result of magic or astrology. **[Saʿdī]** The verse shows that God provides for whom He wills without reckoning.

**Cross-references.** **[Qurṭubī]** 2:91 and the commentary on Dāwūd for *al-miḥrāb*; he also refers to his treatment of *miḥrāb* in Sūrah Maryam. **[Study Quran]** 3:38 for Zachariah's supplication; 19:2–15 for the annunciation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He refers forward to 3:38, "Then and there Zachariah called upon his Lord".

**Reflection.** The verse answers the mother's prayer of 3:36 in three strokes: accepted, grown, and placed in the care of a prophet **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The guardianship is settled by casting pens, which means the child who was vowed is now chosen by God a second time, this time in public **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The out-of-season fruit is the sign by which the household learns who she is, and Zachariah's question is not suspicion but wonder **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Her answer — "it is from God" — is given as theology rather than as explanation: she names the source and refuses to name a mechanism, which is why the commentators hear in it both a proof of omnipotence and a refutation of magic and astrology **[Maʿārif]**, **[Study Quran]**. And it is this child's provision, seen by an old prophet in a private room, that moves him to ask for a child of his own in the next verse **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:38 — هُنَالِكَ دَعَا زَكَرِيَّا رَبَّهُۥ ۖ قَالَ رَبِّ هَبْ لِى مِن لَّدُنكَ ذُرِّيَّةًۭ طَيِّبَةً ۖ إِنَّكَ سَمِيعُ ٱلدُّعَآءِ

*There and then Zachariah called upon his Lord. He said: "My Lord, grant me from Yourself goodly offspring. You are the Hearer of supplication."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over the referent of "there and then". Some said: "there and then" means "when Zakariyyā entered the sanctuary upon Mary and found provision with her, he wished for a child" — transmitted from al-Suddī and from Ibn ʿAbbās. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: "there and then" means "when he asked his Lord for offspring after he had seen Mary's merit with her Lord and her status, and had despaired of offspring because of his age and his wife's barrenness, he called upon his Lord secretly" — transmitted from Qatādah. **[Ṭabarī]** "He said: My Lord, grant me from Yourself goodly offspring" — meaning: give me from Yourself. **[Ṭabarī]** "Goodly offspring" means pure offspring — that is, offspring of the people of the religion of God with which He is well pleased, pure of the defilements of unbelief and disobedience. **[Ṭabarī]** "You are the Hearer of supplication" — You hear the supplication of whoever supplicates to You, so answer me in what I ask of You. **[Jalālayn]** "Then — that is, when Zacharias saw Mary's status with God — Zacharias prayed to his Lord, saying: 'Lord, give me of Thy bounty a goodly offspring — a righteous child. Truly You are the Hearer of prayer.'" **[Ibn Kathīr]** When Zakariyyā saw that God provides sustenance for Mary and gives her the ripest fruits in their out-of-season, and witnessed the extent of God's honour for her, Zakariyyā was eager to have a child of his own, and this is why he called upon God in secret, saying, "My Lord! Grant me from Ladunka (You), a good offspring". **[Ibn Kathīr]** Some scholars said that Zakariyyā did not have offspring at that time; other scholars said he did have children, but they were not righteous, and he desired that God give him a pious son who would take over leadership of the rabbinical position after him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** His request was for a son who would inherit prophethood from him, in addition to inheriting the property he owned. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Verily, You are the All-Hearer of invocation" — the reason for mentioning this quality of God is to make the acceptance of his request more likely, as God says elsewhere, "All praise is due to God who has given me in old age Ishmael and Isaac. Verily, my Lord is indeed the All-Hearer of invocations." **[Saʿdī]** When Zakariyyā saw the extent of God's grace to Mary and His kindness to her, his desire was stirred and his hope revived, so he asked his Lord in his heart and in his speech: "My Lord, grant me from Yourself goodly offspring" — that is, pure, free of any fault or flaw; "You are the Hearer of supplication" — so grant me what I have asked of You. **[Maʿārif]** The word translated as "a purified child" also carries the sense of "a child who is free of any defect" — and this means that the request was not merely for a son, but for a son who would be good and purified in both his outward and inward states, and who would in turn purify others through the knowledge and wisdom God would grant him. **[Maʿārif]** The desire for a child is by no means condemnable — rather it is encouraged, for through it are attained the benefits of the continuity of the species, and the training and education of the child, and the sunnah of the prophets. **[Study Quran]** In 3:38–41 Zachariah, the husband of Mary's maternal aunt and her guardian, requests an heir from God and is given news of the birth of John, the precursor to Jesus. **[Study Quran]** It is said that when Zachariah observed that God provided for Mary in this manner, he was moved to ask God for a son in spite of the fact that he and his wife were advanced in age.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Hunālika* is "in that place", and it is said: at that time and in that place. **[Qurṭubī]** *Hunālika* is an adverb of place. **[Qurṭubī]** *Dhurriyyatan* is the object, and *min ladunka* means "from You" — and *ladun* is a noun indicating the beginning of a limit in place, and it is annexed; and it is said that "from ladun" means "from Your grace" and "from Your bounty".

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:38–40 as a single narrative unit; his material on Zachariah's existing children and on the inheritance of prophethood is cited here, and his comments on the question "how" and on God's power at 3:40. The Study Quran comments on 3:38–39 together; the part of its note bearing on "then and there" is cited here, and the part bearing on the annunciation at 3:39.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Maʿārif]** It is reported that the Prophet said: "Marry those who are loving and fertile, for I will be proud of your large numbers before the other communities." **[Maʿārif]** The Prophet said: "When one of you sees a woman and likes her, let him go to his wife and fulfil his need with her, for that repels what is in his soul." *(digression: the marriage and children ḥadīths appended to this verse in Maʿārif)*

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** "Goodly offspring" means offspring pure of the defilements of unbelief and disobedience, of the people of the religion of God. **[Maʿārif]** The request is for a child good in both outward and inward state, who will in turn purify others by the knowledge and wisdom God grants him. **[Saʿdī]** God is the Hearer of supplication, so grant what was asked.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 14:39, "All praise is due to God who has given me in old age Ishmael and Isaac. Verily, my Lord is indeed the All-Hearer of invocations" — cited for the verse-final epithet. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 19:2–15 for the annunciation narrative. **[Study Quran]** 3:39–41 for the continuation; 19:2–15 and 21:89–90.

**Reflection.** The particle "there and then" is the whole psychology of the verse: an old man standing in a room looking at a child's out-of-season fruit, and something in him starts hoping again **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. What he asks for is not simply a son but a *good* son — pure, sound, free of the defects that had made his own existing children unfit to inherit the rabbinical position **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The commentators note that he asks quietly and that he invokes God as the one who hears supplication, as though choosing the name most likely to bring the answer **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And the request is provoked by grace seen in someone else's life rather than by his own need, which is the most generous possible origin for a prayer **[Saʿdī]**, **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:39 — فَنَادَتْهُ ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةُ وَهُوَ قَآئِمٌۭ يُصَلِّى فِى ٱلْمِحْرَابِ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُبَشِّرُكَ بِيَحْيَىٰ مُصَدِّقًۢا بِكَلِمَةٍۢ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ وَسَيِّدًۭا وَحَصُورًۭا وَنَبِيًّۭا مِّنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ

*Then the angels called to him as he stood praying in the sanctuary: "God gives you good news of John, confirming a word from God — a master, chaste, a prophet among the righteous."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And the angels called to him" — and Zakariyyā was standing at prayer in the sanctuary, and the angels said: "God gives you good news of Yaḥyā, confirming a word from God". **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes disagreed over the meaning of "a word from God". Some said: it means the Book of God and His revelation, which is His word — and the meaning is: confirming Jesus son of Mary and believing in him and in what he brought of the revelation of God. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: the meaning of "a word" is Jesus son of Mary, and he is called a word because God brought him into being with His word "Be", and he was — transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās through several chains, al-Ḥasan, Qatādah, ʿIkrimah, Mujāhid, al-Suddī, al-Rabīʿ b. Anas, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Zayd b. Aslam and al-Ḥusayn b. ʿAlī. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers this: the agreement of the people of tafsīr upon it, and the report from the Messenger of God. **[Ṭabarī]** "And a master" means: a chief to whom his people resort in religion and in the needs of their affairs and in inheritance, and in every affair of the world and the Hereafter. **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over the meaning of "master": some said the master is the one who surpasses his people in good; others said he is the noble one; others said he is the one who is not overcome by anger; others said he is the one who restrains himself from sins; and others said he is the wise one. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers: the master, in the speech of the Arabs, is the chief to whom people resort — and God described Yaḥyā as such, and it is said that it is a description of him as one who is not overcome by anger. **[Ṭabarī]** "And chaste (*ḥaṣūr*)" — the exegetes differed: some said it is one who does not come to women, transmitted from ʿAbdallāh b. Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās; others said it is one who has no desire for women, transmitted from Saʿīd b. al-Musayyib. **[Jalālayn]** "And the angels called to him, standing in the sanctuary at worship: 'God gives you good tidings of John, who shall confirm a Word of God' — that is, Jesus, who shall be brought into being through the word *kun*, 'Be' — 'a chief, and chaste, refraining from women, despite his ability; and a Prophet of the righteous'." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Then the angels called to him" is a glad tiding from God to Zakariyyā of a son named Yaḥyā, who would be blessed as a believer in one of God's Words, meaning ʿĪsā. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Qatādah, Ibn Jurayj and Ibn ʿAbbās said "a Word from God" refers to ʿĪsā; al-Ḥasan, Qatādah, ʿIkrimah and Mujāhid said the same. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "A master" — Ibn ʿAbbās, Saʿīd b. Jubayr, Qatādah, ʿIkrimah, Ibn Abī Najīḥ and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said he is the wise one; Saʿīd b. al-Musayyib said he is the devout scholar and leader; ʿAṭāʾ al-Khurāsānī said he is the noble, God-fearing man; and Mujāhid said he is the forbearing, pious man. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And chaste" — Ibn ʿAbbās, ʿIkrimah, al-Ḍaḥḥāk and Abū Ḥātim said it means one who does not have sexual relations with women; Saʿīd b. Jubayr said it means one who restrains himself from fornication; and al-Ḥasan said it means one who restrains himself from illicit sexual relations. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Some said *al-ḥaṣūr* is derived from *al-ḥaṣr*, which means confinement and retention; the meaning is that he has confined himself from sexual relations with women, as God says elsewhere, "and those who guard their private parts" (23:5). **[Ibn Kathīr]** It is not correct to infer from this that John was impotent or castrated, but rather he was created with a natural lack of desire for women, and he was naturally chaste and naturally averse to women. **[Ibn Kathīr]** It is said that John was not lacking in sexual desire, but rather he was naturally chaste and naturally averse to women, and this was a blessing from God to him. **[Saʿdī]** God answered his supplication and gave him the good news of a son, Yaḥyā, and described him with the attributes of perfection and beauty — that he would be a confirmer of the word of God, Jesus son of Mary, believing in him and testifying to his truth; and a master, that is, a leader and chief to whom people resort in their needs and in their religion; and chaste, that is, one who restrains himself from desires, and is safe from what God has made safe for him of the private parts and the illicit; and a prophet among the righteous. **[Maʿārif]** The word *ḥaṣūr* has been translated as "one who is chaste and purified", and the meaning in *Bayān al-Qurʾān* is that John never committed any sin, and moreover he was not inclined towards women and did not marry. **[Maʿārif]** The exegetes have differed on this: one group holds that John never married a woman and never had any sexual relationship; the other holds that he did marry and had a relationship, but was not inclined towards it and did not let it distract him from worship. *(weak: the second view is not borne out by any sound report)* **[Study Quran]** The phrase "a word from God" is also understood as a reference to God's promise of the coming of Jesus.

*Source note:* The Study Quran comments on 3:38–39 together; the part of its note that bears on the annunciation and on the words "noble" and "chaste" is cited here, and the part that bears on "then and there" at 3:38.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "Then the angels called to him" — *nādathu* is also read *nādāhu* with *takhfīf*, and the feminine of *nādāhu* is because it is attributed to the angels, and it is permissible in Arabic to say *qālat al-malāʾikah*; and it is said that the caller was Gabriel alone, and the attribution to all of them is by way of the plural of magnification. **[Qurṭubī]** *Bi-yaḥyā* is fully declined because it is a foreign name of three letters, the middle of which is a *yāʾ* with *fatḥah*; and it is said that it is derived from *ḥayya yaḥyā* — that is, "he lives" — because God revived his father's loins and his mother's barrenness by him. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj said: it is permissible to decline it, and the reason it is given the *nūn* of the broken plural is that it is a name for a *sūrah* or for a prophet. *(digression: the grammar of *Yaḥyā*)*

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** The reading *fa-nādāhu* with *takhfīf* is that of Ibn Masʿūd and al-Aʿmash, and the reading of the rest is *nādathu* with the feminine *tāʾ*. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Masʿūd read *fa-nādāhu Jibrīl*. **[Qurṭubī]** The people of Madīnah and Abū ʿAmr read *yuba shshiruka* with *tashdīd* in one reading, and Ibn ʿĀmir read *yubshiruka*; and the *takhfīf* of *yabshuru* is like *yaʿkifu* and *yaʿkufu*. **[Qurṭubī]** Whoever reads *an* with *kasrah* makes it a report, and whoever reads it with *fatḥah* makes it "that", and both are good Arabic.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** John confirms Jesus, who is the "word" because God brought him into being by His saying "Be", and he was. **[Ibn Kathīr]** John was naturally chaste and averse to women, and it is not correct to infer that he was impotent or castrated — the correct meaning is that he was immune from illicit sexual relations, not that he was incapable of them. **[Maʿārif]** *Bayān al-Qurʾān* takes *ḥaṣūr* to mean that John never committed any sin and was not inclined towards women. **[Saʿdī]** The four attributes given to John — confirming the word of God, master, chaste, prophet — are attributes of perfection and beauty.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 23:5, "and those who guard their private parts". **[Qurṭubī]** 19:7 and Sūrah Maryam for the fuller narrative; and he refers to his treatment of *nādā* in al-Baqarah. **[Study Quran]** 3:45 and 4:171 for the phrase "a word from God" used of Jesus; 19:2–15 for the annunciation. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:45 for the parallel use of "word".

**Reflection.** The annunciation comes while he is at prayer, which is where the request was made — the answer is delivered into the posture in which it was asked **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. The child is described before he is conceived with four words that are all about his future: he will confirm someone greater than himself, he will be a man people turn to, he will be chaste, and he will be a prophet **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. Calling Jesus "a word from God" is the theological centre of the sūrah's Christology, and the commentators are careful about what it does and does not say: he is a word because God said "Be" and he was, not because a word became a man **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. And the one disputed word — *ḥaṣūr* — is settled by Ibn Kathīr against the cruder reading: a man immune from what is unlawful, not a man incapable of what is lawful **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**.

## 3:40 — قَالَ رَبِّ أَنَّىٰ يَكُونُ لِى غُلَٰمٌۭ وَقَدْ بَلَغَنِىَ ٱلْكِبَرُ وَٱمْرَأَتِى عَاقِرٌۭ ۖ قَالَ كَذَٰلِكَ ٱللَّهَ يَفْعَلُ مَا يَشَآءُ

*He said: "My Lord, how shall I have a son when old age has reached me and my wife is barren?" He said: "Thus does God do what He wills."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God says: and Zakariyyā said, when the angels gave him the good news from God of Yaḥyā: "My Lord, how shall I have a son when old age has reached me and my wife is barren?" **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over the meaning of "how shall I have a son". **[Ṭabarī]** Some said: it is a question about the *manner* — meaning: how shall I have a son when my wife is barren and I am an old man, and from where shall I have him, and by what means? — and this is not an expression of impossibility on the part of God, but a question about the way in which the son will come to him, from which of the two of them, or from other than the two of them. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: it is a question of astonishment at the glad tiding, and a request for a sign by which he might know its truth — transmitted from al-Suddī and ʿIkrimah, who said that Shayṭān came to him and made him doubt the voice, so he asked for a sign. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers the first: the report from the Messenger of God and the consensus of the people of tafsīr, and because the angels had already given him the good news from God, and it is not fitting for a prophet to doubt after that. **[Ṭabarī]** "He said: thus does God do what He wills" — meaning: as God has given you this son while you are as you are, and your wife as she is, so does God do what He wills: He gives offspring to whom He wills, and withholds from whom He wills; nothing is impossible for Him, and nothing that He wills escapes Him. **[Jalālayn]** "He said, 'Lord, how shall I have a son, seeing that I am an old man and my wife is barren?' He said, 'Even so, God does what He will' — and she was made fertile, and he was made capable of sexual intercourse, and she bore him John." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "He said, 'O my Lord! How can I have a son when I am very old, and my wife is barren?'" — this is a question about the manner in which the son will be born, not about whether it is possible. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "He said, 'Thus does God do what He wills'" — meaning, thus is the matter; God is capable of whatever He wills and nothing is impossible for Him. **[Saʿdī]** When the angels gave him the good news, he said in wonder at God's power and His bounty, and asking about the manner by which the child would come to him: "My Lord, how shall I have a son when old age has reached me and my wife is barren?" — and two obstacles were present: his old age and his wife's barrenness. **[Saʿdī]** "He said: thus does God do what He wills" — that is, as God has given you this son from these two obstacles, so does God do what He wills; nothing is beyond Him and nothing escapes Him, and His wisdom and mercy are vast. **[Maʿārif]** The question "how shall I have a son" was not a question of doubt or denial — for the prophets are protected from that — but rather a question of enquiry, as to whether the child would be born in the natural way or by some other means, or whether he would be given a son by the restoration of his youth and his wife's fertility. **[Maʿārif]** *Bayān al-Qurʾān* states that the question was asked in a spirit of enquiry and wonder, not of doubt, and that this is why the answer came as it did. **[Study Quran]** "How shall I have a son when old age has reached me and my wife is barren?" — this is understood as a question about the manner of the birth rather than an expression of doubt about God's power. **[Study Quran]** "Thus does God do what He wills" — this is God's response, meaning that God creates and does whatsoever He wills, and His command is not subject to the ordinary course of things.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Anā* means "from where" and "how", and it is a question about the manner. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-ʿāqir* is the woman who does not bear, and its plural is *ʿawāqir* and *ʿuqqar*; the man is *ʿāqir* too, and it is from *ʿaqara* — meaning "he cut" — as though she has cut off the hope of offspring. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Kalbī said: "My Lord" here means Gabriel, not God — that is, Zakariyyā addressed the angel. *(weak: the apparent sense is that he addressed his Lord)*

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:38–40 as a single narrative unit; his material on the manner of the question and on God's power is cited here, and his material on Zachariah's existing children at 3:38.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Wa-qad balaghaniya al-kibaru* is read with *takhfīf* by Nāfiʿ and with *tashdīd* by the rest.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Suddī and ʿIkrimah said that Shayṭān came to Zakariyyā and made him doubt the voice, so he asked for a sign — transmitted through Asbāṭ and through Ḥusayn b. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān. *(weak: al-Ṭabarī prefers the reading that makes it a question about the manner, not doubt)* **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that he was ninety-nine and his wife ninety-eight — or ninety — and she was barren, and that he was one hundred and twenty and she ninety-eight. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the reports about their ages)* **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that his wife said: I am barren and I have never borne, and I am ninety-eight years old. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the same reports on her age)* **[Study Quran]** The reports of the ages of Zachariah and his wife vary widely in the sources.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** God gives offspring to whom He wills and withholds from whom He wills; nothing is impossible for Him and nothing that He wills escapes Him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God is capable of whatever He wills and nothing is impossible for Him. **[Maʿārif]** The prophets are protected from doubting a divine promise, so the question must be read as enquiry, not doubt. **[Saʿdī]** Nothing is beyond God and nothing escapes Him, and His wisdom and mercy are vast.

**Cross-references.** **[Maʿārif]** 3:39 for the annunciation that prompts the question. **[Study Quran]** 19:2–15 for the annunciation to Zachariah, where he asks for a sign and is given the sign of silence for three days. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He refers back to 3:38–39 for the context of the request. **[Qurṭubī]** He refers to Sūrah Maryam for the sign of three days' silence.

**Reflection.** The question is asked by a man who had just spent a lifetime wanting this child and who had, moments earlier, watched God feed a girl in a locked room — so it is not disbelief, and the commentators are emphatic that it is not **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. What he asks is *how*: which door will this come through, mine or my wife's, the natural one or another **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The answer declines to explain and simply names the agent — "thus does God do what He wills" — which is either a refusal of the question or the only answer to it **[Saʿdī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Two obstacles are named side by side, his age and her barrenness, and both are removed at once, which is the point: the impediment was never the interesting part **[Jalālayn]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And the whole exchange sits inside a sūrah arguing with people who thought a virgin birth was the hard case — here is a birth from a ninety-year-old woman and a man past it, offered first **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

## 3:41 — قَالَ رَبِّ ٱجْعَل لِّىٓ ءَايَةًۭ ۖ قَالَ ءَايَتُكَ أَلَّا تُكَلِّمَ ٱلنَّاسَ ثَلَٰثَةَ أَيَّامٍ إِلَّا رَمْزًۭا ۗ وَٱذْكُر رَّبَّكَ كَثِيرًۭا وَسَبِّحْ بِٱلْعَشِىِّ وَٱلْإِبْكَـٰرِ

*He said: "My Lord, appoint for me a sign." He said: "Your sign is that you shall not speak to the people for three days except by gestures. And remember your Lord much, and glorify Him in the evening and at the morning."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means, reporting Zachariah's saying: Zachariah said, "My Lord, if this call I was called with, and the voice I heard, is the voice of Your angels and a glad tiding from You, then appoint for me a sign" — meaning a mark — "that it is so, so that there may depart from me what Shayṭān has whispered and cast into my heart: that it is the voice of other than the angels, and a tiding from other than You." **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits this from al-Suddī: "'My Lord, appoint for me a sign' — he said, meaning Zachariah: O Lord, if this voice is from You, then appoint for me a sign." **[Jalālayn]** "He said 'My Lord! Appoint for me a sign' — that is, an indication of my wife's pregnancy." **[Jalālayn]** "He said 'Your sign for this is that you shall not speak to men' — that is, you shall refrain from speaking to them, but not from remembrance of God — 'save by tokens', gestures, 'for three days and nights. And remember your Lord often and glorify', perform prayer, 'at evening and dawn' — at the end of the day and at its beginning." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "He said: 'O my Lord! Make a sign for me'" means: make a sign that alerts me that the child will come. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "'Your sign is that you shall not speak to the people for three days except by signals'" means: you will not be able to speak except with signals, although you are not mute — as God says elsewhere, "for three nights, though having no bodily defect" (19:10). **[Saʿdī]** "He said: 'My Lord, appoint for me a sign'" — so that joy and gladness may be attained; and though, my Lord, I am certain of what You have told me, the soul rejoices and the heart is reassured by the preliminaries of mercy and kindness. **[Saʿdī]** "He said: 'Your sign is that you shall not speak to people for three days except by gestures', and during that period 'remember your Lord much and glorify Him in the evening and at the morning'" — the first of the day and its last. **[Maʿārif]** Zachariah's purpose behind requesting a sign of pregnancy was to be happy at the prospect, and thus to be enabled to show gratitude all along, even prior to the actual birth of the child; so God gave him the sign stated above. **[Maʿārif]** The sign given was remarkably suitable to its purpose: his request was prompted by his wish to show gratefulness, and the sign set for him leaves him incapable of doing anything but this — even a hundred signs would not have done what this one sign did, and the noble purpose he had in mind was accomplished.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Āyah* means sign, as he has already established with evidence. **[Ṭabarī]** He then records at length the disagreement of the Arab grammarians over why the word is not hamzated, since a *yāʾ* following a silent *alif* is normally hamzated: some said it was originally *ayyah* and the doubling was felt as heavy, so they substituted an *alif*; others said it is a defective *fāʿilah*, and were answered that its diminutive is *uyayyah* not *uwayyah*; others that it is a *faʿlah* whose first *yāʾ* was turned into an *alif*, as in *ḥājah* and *qāmah* — and were answered that the Arabs do that only with triliteral nouns. *(digression: the long grammatical excursus on the morphology of āyah)* **[Qurṭubī]** *Jaʿala* here has the sense of *ṣayyara*, "make", since it takes two objects, and *lī* is in the position of the second object. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-ramz* in the language is gesturing with the lips, though it is also used of gesturing with the eyebrows, the eyes and the hands; its root is "movement". **[Qurṭubī]** *Ramzan* is in the accusative as a disconnected exception, according to al-Akhfash; al-Kisāʾī said *ramaza yarmizu wa-yarmuzu*.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** The variant readings *ramzan* with *fatḥah* on the *mīm*, and *rumuzan* with *ḍammah* on both the *rāʾ* and the *mīm*, are recorded, the singular being *ramzah*.

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** This verse is a proof that gesture takes the place of speech, and that is found in much of the Sunnah — the weightiest of gestures being the judgement the Prophet ﷺ gave in the matter of the slave-woman, when he said to her "Where is God?" and she pointed with her head towards the sky, and he said "Free her, for she is a believer". **[Qurṭubī]** He thus validated Islam by a gesture — Islam being the root of the religion by which blood and property are secured, by which Paradise is deserved and from the Fire one is saved — so a gesture must be operative in the rest of the religion too, and this is the position of the generality of the jurists. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn al-Qāsim related from Mālik that if a mute person gestures a divorce, it is binding upon him. **[Maʿārif]** The phrase "except through gestures" in this verse tells us that where speech is not possible because of a valid reason, gesture is deemed its substitute — as in the ḥadīth in which the Prophet ﷺ asked a maid-servant "Where is Allah?" and she pointed to the sky, and he said "This maid-servant is a Muslim". **[Saʿdī]** He was prevented from speech during that period, and there was a fittingness in it: a child coming from a very old man and a barren woman, and his being unable to address human beings while his tongue was free with the remembrance of God — another sign.
