**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Most of the exegetes said that God punished him by striking him with silence from addressing people, because he asked for a sign after the angels had spoken to him face to face with the glad tiding; they said that even if it was not an illness, dumbness or the like, there is in it some punishment in any case. **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits from Qatādah: "he was only punished with that because [he asked] after the angels had addressed him directly." **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Zayd said: when his wife conceived John by him, he woke unable to speak to anyone, while all the while reading the Torah and remembering God; when he wanted to converse with someone he could not manage it. **[Qurṭubī]** Others said he asked for the sign for an increase of reassurance — "complete the favour by appointing for me a sign, and let that sign be an increase of favour and honour" — and al-Naḥḥās chose this view, saying that Qatādah's view that Zachariah was punished by being unable to speak is something to be turned away from, because God has not told us that he sinned or that He forbade him this; the meaning is "appoint for me a sign indicating that the child exists, since it is hidden from me", and the evidence for this reading is 19:9, "and I created you before, when you were nothing" — that is, I brought you into being by My power, and so I will bring the child into being for you. *(weak: the view that he was punished, rejected by al-Naḥḥās)* **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan said: he was only silenced as a discipline, not as a punishment.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** God then commanded Zachariah to supplicate, thank and praise Him often in that condition — "and remember your Lord much and glorify Him in the afternoon and in the morning". **[Saʿdī]** The purpose of the sign was joy and reassurance, not doubt: he was certain of what he had been told, and asked so that the soul might rejoice. **[Ṭabarī]** Whatever the reason for the request, the sign itself is a mark from God confirming that the tiding was from God.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 19:10, "for three nights, though having no bodily defect"; and he promises to elaborate in the beginning of Sūrah Maryam. **[Qurṭubī]** 19:9, "and I created you before, when you were nothing", adduced for the non-punitive reading. **[Qurṭubī]** 5:3 on the *azlām*. **[Ṭabarī]** He refers back to his earlier treatment of *āyah* and of *ghayb*.

**Reflection.** The verse is unusually honest about the psychology of faith: a man who has been addressed by angels asks for a sign anyway, and the Qurʾān neither hides that nor flatters him for it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The commentators split sharply over whether this was a failing — al-Ṭabarī and the majority calling it a discipline for asking after the angels had spoken face to face, al-Naḥḥās refusing that reading outright on the ground that God has not told us he sinned **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Either way the sign chosen is the same, and it is a strange one: silence **[Jalālayn]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Maʿārif's observation is the sharpest — the punishment, if it is one, is also the perfect instrument of what he wanted, since a man who cannot speak to anyone can do nothing but remember **[Maʿārif]**. And the verse quietly legislates: the gesture of a woman who could not answer in words was accepted as sufficient for the whole of the religion, which is why the jurists reach for it here **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Maʿārif]**.

*Source note:* The Study Quran has no separate block for 3:41; its note on Zachariah's request and sign forms part of its 3:38–39 and 3:40–41 commentary, cited at 3:40.

## 3:42 — وَإِذْ قَالَتِ ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةُ يَٰمَرْيَمُ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ ٱصْطَفَىٰكِ وَطَهَّرَكِ وَٱصْطَفَىٰكِ عَلَىٰ نِسَآءِ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ

*And when the angels said: "O Mary, God has chosen you and purified you, and chosen you above the women of the worlds."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "God has chosen you" means: He elected you and selected you for His obedience and for what He singled you out for of His honour; "and purified you" means: He purified your religion of doubt and of the filths that are in the religions of the women of the children of Adam; "and chosen you above the women of the worlds" means: in your time, by your obedience to Him, so He preferred you over them — as is related from the Messenger of God ﷺ: "the best of its women is Mary daughter of ʿImrān, and the best of its women is Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid" — and by "the best of its women" he means the best of the women of the people of Paradise. **[Jalālayn]** "And mention when the angels — namely Gabriel — said: 'O Mary, God has preferred you' — He has elected you — 'and made you pure of the touch of men; He has preferred you above all women of the worlds', that is, the inhabitants of your time." **[Ibn Kathīr]** God states that the angels spoke to Mary by His command and told her that He chose her because of her service to Him, and because of her modesty, honour, innocence and conviction; and that He chose her because of her virtue over the women of the world. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The angels commanded Mary to increase acts of worship, humbleness, submission, prostration and bowing, so that she would acquire what God had decreed for her — as a test for her, yet a test that also earned her a higher grade in this life and the Hereafter, for God demonstrated His might by creating a son inside her without male intervention. **[Saʿdī]** God returned to the mention of Mary, and that she had reached in worship and perfection a tremendous measure: "O Mary, God has chosen you" — that is, He has elected you and granted you magnificent attributes and beautiful character — "and purified you" from vile traits — "and chosen you above the women of the worlds". **[Saʿdī]** The angels called her by God's command with that, so that she would rejoice in God's blessings, thank God, stand to His rights and busy herself in His service. **[Study Quran]** In this verse Mary is chosen, purified, and then chosen a second time. **[Study Quran]** Some understand the first election to refer to the first part of her life, when she was given to devotion and service in the Temple and was granted miraculous sustenance (see 3:37), and the second to her being given the miracle of Jesus' birth; *purified* is understood as her purity of intention and worship, but can also mean being kept pure from the defilement of the evil of others. **[Study Quran]** The verse has language similar to Luke 1:42, where it is said to Mary, "Blessed art thou among women." **[Maʿārif]** The sentence "and has chosen you over women of all the worlds" refers to all women in that period of time; therefore the statement in the ḥadīth that "Fāṭimah is the foremost among the women of Paradise" does not contradict it.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Iṣṭafāki* means "He chose you", as has preceded. **[Qurṭubī]** *Ṭahharaki* — from unbelief, according to Mujāhid and al-Ḥasan; al-Zajjāj said: from all filths, of menses, post-natal bleeding and the like. **[Qurṭubī]** "Above the women of the worlds" means the world of her time, according to al-Ḥasan, Ibn Jurayj and others; and it is said "all of them together until the Day of the Trumpet", which is the sound view according to al-Zajjāj and others. **[Qurṭubī]** The choosing is repeated because the meaning of the first is choosing for His worship and the meaning of the second is for the birth of Jesus. **[Study Quran]** The "and remember when" at the start of this verse is continued with the "when" at the start of 3:45, both of which set up "she said" in 3:47 — so that it would mean, "When the angels said what they said, she said..."

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Through ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib, from Hishām b. ʿUrwah from his father from ʿAbdallāh b. Jaʿfar: I heard ʿAlī say in Iraq, "I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: the best of its women is Mary daughter of ʿImrān, and the best of its women is Khadījah" — a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī, Muslim and al-Tirmidhī. **[Ṭabarī]** Through ʿAbdallāh b. Jaʿfar b. Abī Ṭālib directly: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "the best of the women of Paradise is Mary daughter of ʿImrān, and the best of the women of Paradise is Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid". **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: we were told that the Prophet of God ﷺ used to say, "sufficient for you, of the women of the worlds, are Mary daughter of ʿImrān, the wife of Pharaoh, Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid and Fāṭimah daughter of Muḥammad". **[Qurṭubī]** In the Ṣaḥīḥ of Muslim, from Abū Mūsā: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "Many of the men achieved perfection, and none of the women achieved perfection other than Mary daughter of ʿImrān and Āsiyah the wife of Pharaoh; and the superiority of ʿĀʾishah over women is like the superiority of *tharīd* over all other food". **[Qurṭubī]** Through Abū Hurayrah: "the best women of the worlds are four: Mary daughter of ʿImrān, Āsiyah daughter of Muzāḥim the wife of Pharaoh, Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid and Fāṭimah daughter of Muḥammad". **[Qurṭubī]** Through Ibn ʿAbbās: "the most excellent women of the people of Paradise are Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid, Fāṭimah daughter of Muḥammad, Mary daughter of ʿImrān and Āsiyah daughter of Muzāḥim"; and in another version, "the mistress of the women of Paradise after Mary is Fāṭimah, then Khadījah". **[Study Quran]** A ḥadīth states: "Mary the daughter of ʿImrān, Khadījah bint Khuwaylid, Fāṭimah bint Muḥammad, and Āsiyah the wife of Pharaoh suffice you among the women of the worlds"; such aḥādīth speak of these four women as the best or most perfect of all women, and most Muslims believe that they lead the soul of blessed women to Paradise. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Tirmidhī recorded from ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib: "The best woman (in her time) was Mary, daughter of ʿImrān, and the best woman (of the Prophet's time) is Khadījah, daughter of Khuwaylid" — recorded in the Two Ṣaḥīḥs.

**Belief.** **[Qurṭubī]** The apparent sense of the Qurʾān and the ḥadīths requires that Mary is more excellent than all the women of the world, from Eve to the last woman upon whom the Hour arises; for the angels conveyed revelation to her from God — with obligation, information and glad tidings — as they conveyed it to the rest of the prophets, so she is a prophetess, and the prophet is more excellent than the saint. **[Qurṭubī]** The "perfection" mentioned in the ḥadīth is taken by some to mean prophethood, which would entail that Mary and Āsiyah were both prophetesses, and that has been said; the sound view is that Mary is a prophetess, because God revealed to her through the angel as He revealed to the rest of the prophets; as for Āsiyah, nothing has come indicating her prophethood clearly, rather her truthfulness and merit. **[Qurṭubī]** Absolute perfection belongs to God alone, and the most perfect of the human kind are the prophets, followed by the friends of God among the truthful, the martyrs and the righteous. **[Saʿdī]** God chose her and granted her magnificent attributes and beautiful character, and purified her from vile traits.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 2:116, "Nay, to Him belongs all that is in the heavens and on earth, and all surrender with obedience to Him", cited for *qunūt*. **[Study Quran]** 3:37 for the miraculous sustenance; 3:45 and 3:47 for the continuation of the "when". **[Qurṭubī]** He refers to Sūrah Maryam for the fuller treatment of Mary's prophethood and to Sūrah al-Taḥrīm for Āsiyah. **[Ṭabarī]** He cross-refers his earlier treatment of *iṣṭafā* and of *al-ʿālamīn*.

**Reflection.** The angels speak to Mary directly, and what they say is that she has been chosen — twice, which the commentators refuse to treat as mere repetition: one choosing for worship, one for the child **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Purity here is religious before it is physical — her religion purified of doubt, or she herself kept from the defilement of others' evil — which is why the verse can be spoken to a woman about to be the subject of slander **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Study Quran]**. The arguments about scope are small and telling: "the women of the worlds" as her own time or as all time, and whether the ḥadīths about Fāṭimah conflict with it, answered by reading the two as different frames **[Maʿārif]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And the choice is immediately followed by a command to work, as though election were a job description rather than a medal **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**.

## 3:43 — يَٰمَرْيَمُ ٱقْنُتِى لِرَبِّكِ وَٱسْجُدِى وَٱرْكَعِى مَعَ ٱلرَّٰكِعِينَ

*"O Mary, be devoutly obedient to your Lord, and prostrate, and bow with those who bow."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means, reporting the saying of His angels to Mary: "O Mary, be devoutly obedient (*uqnuti*) to your Lord" — make your obedience sincere to your Lord alone. **[Jalālayn]** "O Mary be obedient to your Lord, be compliant before Him, prostrating and bowing with those who bow" — that is, pray with those who pray. **[Ibn Kathīr]** *Qunūt* here means to submit with humbleness, as God says elsewhere, "Nay, to Him belongs all that is in the heavens and on earth, and all surrender with obedience (*qānitūn*) to Him" (2:116). **[Saʿdī]** "Be devoutly obedient to your Lord" — that is, increase in obedience, submission and humility to your Lord and make it constant; "and bow with those who bow" — that is, pray with those who pray; and she stood to everything she was commanded and excelled and surpassed in her perfection. **[Study Quran]** Regarding *qānit*, which carries the sense of conformity, but also of constancy, being in a standing position, and silence, see 4:34c. **[Maʿārif]** In the command "and bow down with those who bow", the imperative has been qualified with the words "with those who bow" — but no such qualification appears with the command to prostrate.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over *qunūt* here as they did elsewhere: some said it means "lengthen the standing (*aṭīlī al-rukūd*)" — transmitted from Mujāhid through several chains, and from al-Rabīʿ, who glossed it as "stand for your Lord in prayer"; others said "be sincere to your Lord" — transmitted from Saʿīd b. Jubayr; others said "obey your Lord" — transmitted from Qatādah and al-Suddī; and al-Ḥasan said "worship your Lord". **[Ṭabarī]** *Rukūʿ* and *sujūd* mean humility to God and lowliness before Him in obedience and servitude. **[Qurṭubī]** Mujāhid said: "lengthen the standing in prayer"; Qatādah said: "make obedience constant"; and al-Awzāʿī said: when the angels said that to her she stood in prayer until her feet swelled and ran with blood and pus. **[Qurṭubī]** The prostration is mentioned before the bowing here because the *wāw* does not require order — as when you say "Zayd and ʿAmr stood", ʿAmr may have stood first — so the meaning is "bow and prostrate"; and it is also said that their law had prostration before bowing. **[Qurṭubī]** "With those who bow": it is said the meaning is "do as they do even if you do not pray with them"; and it is said the reference is to congregational prayer.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Mujāhid said: when it was said to her "O Mary, be devoutly obedient to your Lord", she stood until her ankles swelled; and in another chain, until her feet swelled. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Awzāʿī said: she used to stand until pus ran from her feet. **[Ṭabarī]** From Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, from the Prophet ﷺ: "every letter in which *qunūt* is mentioned in the Qurʾān is obedience to God." **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Awzāʿī related that when the angels said that to her she stood in prayer until her feet swelled and bled with blood and pus. *(digression: the reports of the length of Mary's standing)*

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** The qualification "with those who bow", attached to the command to bow but not to the command to prostrate, apparently hints that people generally do not observe due concern while bowing: they tend to bend a little and then resume the standing position, as if in a hurry, so that their bowing is closer to standing. **[Maʿārif]** By mentioning "those who bow", God has given a model to be followed, so that one's bowing in prayer may become like the bowing of those who bow fully and perfectly as is due. **[Qurṭubī]** The mention of bowing "with those who bow" is taken by some as a reference to congregational prayer.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** The command is to constancy in obedience, submission and humility, and Mary fulfilled it completely. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The angels commanded her to increase acts of worship so that she would acquire what God had decreed for her. **[Ṭabarī]** *Rukūʿ* and *sujūd* are humility to God and lowliness before Him in obedience and servitude.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 2:116 for *qunūt* as surrender with obedience. **[Study Quran]** 4:34c for the semantic range of *qānit*; 2:125, where prostration and bowing are mentioned together in a command to Abraham and Ishmael; and 2:43, where "bow with those who bow" also appears. **[Qurṭubī]** 2:158 for the question of whether the *wāw* requires order, and his treatment of *qunūt* in al-Baqarah. **[Saʿdī]** 3:42, where the angels' address to Mary begins.

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:42–44 as a single unit; the material on the angels' testimony and on the casting of lots is cited at 3:42 and 3:44, and the material on *qunūt* here. Saʿdī comments on 3:33–55 as one continuous passage, and his remarks are divided between the verses by content.

**Reflection.** The verse is a command, not a description, and it is given to the one person in the narrative who has just been told she is chosen above all women — election arrives as instruction **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The word *qunūt* is opened up by the commentators into a whole cluster: standing long, sincerity, obedience, worship — and the reports about Mary's feet swelling are the community's way of saying what that looked like **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Maʿārif's reading of the asymmetry between prostration and bowing is the kind of detail that only a working piety notices: the qualifier is attached to the act people rush **[Maʿārif]**. And bowing is commanded "with those who bow", which places even the most singular of women inside a congregation **[Jalālayn]**, **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:44 — ذَٰلِكَ مِنْ أَنۢبَآءِ ٱلْغَيْبِ نُوحِيهِ إِلَيْكَ ۚ وَمَا كُنتَ لَدَيْهِمْ إِذْ يُلْقُونَ أَقْلَٰمَهُمْ أَيُّهُمْ يَكْفُلُ مَرْيَمَ وَمَا كُنتَ لَدَيْهِمْ إِذْ يَخْتَصِمُونَ

*That is from the tidings of the Unseen which We reveal to you. You were not with them when they cast their pens [to decide] which of them should take care of Mary, and you were not with them when they were disputing.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "That" refers to the news God told His servants about the wife of ʿImrān and her daughter Mary, and Zachariah and his son John, and all else He narrated in the verses from "God chose Adam and Noah" onwards; God then gathered all of it with the word "that", saying: these tidings are "from the tidings of the Unseen", that is, from the hidden reports of the people which neither you, O Muḥammad, nor your people came to know, and which none knew but a few of the rabbis and monks of the two scriptures. **[Ṭabarī]** God then informed His prophet Muḥammad ﷺ that He revealed that to him as a proof (*ḥujjah*) of his prophethood, as verification of his truthfulness, and as a cutting-off of the excuse of those who deny his message among the disbelievers of the People of the Two Scriptures — who know that Muḥammad did not come to knowledge of these tidings, given their hiddenness, and did not attain knowledge of them given their obscurity even among their own people, except by God's informing him of it. **[Ṭabarī]** That is because it is known to them that Muḥammad ﷺ was unlettered (*ummī*), not writing so that he might read the books and so arrive at knowledge of that through books, and not a companion of the People of the Book so that he might take his knowledge from them. **[Jalālayn]** "That which has been mentioned of the matter of Zachariah and Mary is of the tidings of the Unseen — of the news of what was unknown to you. We reveal it to you, O Muhammad; for you were not with them when they were casting quills in the water, drawing their lots, so that it be manifested to them which of them should have charge of Mary; nor were you with them when they were disputing about her custodianship, such that you might have known it and related it; but truly you know it only through revelation." **[Saʿdī]** This story, and others like it, is among the greatest proofs of the message of Muḥammad ﷺ, since he told of it in detail, verified, with no addition and no omission — and that is only because it is revelation from God, the Mighty, the Wise, not learning from people. **[Saʿdī]** "You were not with them when they cast their pens..." — her mother brought her and they disputed over which of them would take care of her, because she was the daughter of their imām and their leader, and each of them wanted the good and the reward from God, until the dispute reached the point that they drew lots and cast their pens as lots, and the lot fell to Zachariah, as a mercy from God to him and to her. **[Saʿdī]** You, O Messenger, did not attend that situation so as to know it and relate it to the people; rather, God informed you of it — and this is the greatest purpose of setting out the stories: that the lesson be obtained by them, and the greatest of lessons and the argument by them for God's oneness, the message, the resurrection and the other great fundamentals. **[Study Quran]** *Unseen* (*ghayb*) can mean what is absent from us through being invisible or hidden, but also what is absent by being in the past, in the future, or in another state of being; the Qurʾān also points out that the prophet was not witness to the events at Sinai in 28:44 and 28:46. **[Maʿārif]** This verse presents an argument to prove the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ by referring to happenings in the lives of Zachariah and Mary which took place in the remote past, with no likelihood of their being seen, heard or read — specially by the Prophet ﷺ — which by itself proves that his being informed of the unknown was beyond the ordinary course of learning. **[Maʿārif]** The text very eloquently states that the Prophet ﷺ was not there to see them drawing lots, nor was he present when they were initially quarrelling about Mary's custody before agreeing upon the drawing of lots.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Ghayb* is a verbal noun from "so-and-so was absent from such-and-such, *ghāba ʿan kadhā fa-huwa yaghību ʿanhu ghayban wa-ghībatan*". **[Ṭabarī]** "We reveal it to you" means "We send it down to you"; the root meaning of *wahi* in speech is the caster's casting to the one cast to, and that may be by writing, by sign, by gesture, by inspiration or by message. **[Ṭabarī]** He cites 16:68, "and your Lord revealed (*awḥā*) to the bee", as inspiration; 5:111, "and when I revealed (*awḥaytu*) to the disciples", as inspiration; 19:11, "then he gestured to them: glorify morning and evening", as signing; 6:121, "the devils reveal (*la-yūḥūna*) to their friends", as whispering; and 6:19, "this Qurʾān has been revealed to me", as the coming of Gabriel with it from God. **[Ṭabarī]** The Arabs called writing *wahy* because it is fixed and established in what is written, and he cites the line of Kaʿb b. Zuhayr: "poems of his reached the non-Arabs and the horizons, remaining as *wahy* remains in stone". **[Qurṭubī]** *Waḥy* in the language is "informing secretly", which is why inspiration is called *waḥy*; and Ibn Fāris said: *waḥy* is the sign, the writing, the message, and everything you cast to another so that he knows it is *waḥy*, however it is done. **[Qurṭubī]** *Aqlām* is the plural of *qalam*, from *qalamahu* "he cut it"; it is said to mean their arrows, and it is said to mean the pens with which they wrote the Torah — and this is better, since the *azlām* were forbidden by God (5:3), unless they did that in a manner other than that of the pre-Islamic Arabs. **[Qurṭubī]** "Which of them should take care of Mary" is a subject and predicate in the position of the accusative by an implied verb indicated by the speech — the sense being "they were looking to see which of them would take care of Mary" — and the verb does not govern the wording of *ayy* because it is interrogative.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** Zachariah said, "I have more right to her, her maternal aunt is with me" — she was Ashyaʿ daughter of Fāqūdh, the sister of Ḥannah the mother of Mary — and the Children of Israel said, "we have more right to her, she is the daughter of our scholar". So they drew lots, each of them bringing his pen, and agreed to put the pens into running water: whoever's pen stood still and was not carried away by the water would be her guardian. **[Qurṭubī]** The Prophet ﷺ said: "the pens flowed, and Zachariah's pen rose" — and that was a sign for him, because he was a prophet through whom signs occur. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Jarīr recorded that ʿIkrimah said: Mary's mother left with Mary, carrying her in her infant cloth, and took her to the rabbis from the offspring of Aaron, who were responsible for Bayt al-Maqdis at that time; she said to them, "Take this child whom I vowed, I have set her free, since she is my daughter, for no menstruating woman should enter the mosque, and I shall not take her back home." They said, "She is the daughter of our imām" — for ʿImrān used to lead them in prayer — "who took care of our sacrificial rituals". Zachariah said, "Give her to me, for her maternal aunt is my wife." They said, "Our hearts cannot bear that you take her, for she is the daughter of our imām." So they drew lots with the pens with which they wrote the Torah, and Zachariah won the lot and took Mary into his care. **[Ibn Kathīr]** ʿIkrimah, al-Suddī, Qatādah, al-Rabīʿ b. Anas and others said that the rabbis went into the Jordan river and cast lots there, deciding to throw their pens into the river: the pen that remained afloat and idle would indicate that its owner would take care of Mary; the water took all the pens under except Zachariah's pen, which remained afloat in its place. **[Study Quran]** The most widely accepted account is that the priests of the Temple desired to be the custodians of Mary after she entered the Temple and cast lots to decide who would have the privilege; in many places the Bible mentions that lots were cast on a regular basis for matters of varying importance — choosing persons, who would be king, who would offer incense, who would sacrifice an animal — and also for divination. **[Study Quran]** Some commentators believe, however, that Zachariah was not disputed as the custodian or caretaker of Mary, since he was the husband of her maternal relative; on the latter interpretation the disputation could have been about her ongoing care or about who would serve her in the Temple. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the details of how the lots were cast)*

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** According to Ḥanafī jurists, the rule governing *qurʿah* (drawing lots) is that it cannot be given a decisive role in determining rights that are known and fixed in Islamic Law; drawing lots in such a situation falls under *qimār* (gambling) — for example, if something is jointly owned by several persons and a lot is drawn so that the one whose name comes out takes the whole thing, or in the case of a child whose parentage is disputed, a lot is drawn and the one whose name comes out is taken to be the father; drawing lots in such cases is not allowed in the Sharīʿah. **[Maʿārif]** As for rights which depend upon opinion, *qurʿah* is permissible in them — for instance, when allotting portions of a joint property, A is given the eastern portion and B the western by drawing a lot; this is permissible because it would have been permissible without drawing lots, by mutual agreement or by a judge's decree. **[Maʿārif]** In other words: where the rights of all parties are equal and the purpose of the lot is only to allocate equal portions and to decide which portion is whose, drawing a lot is permissible.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The revelation of these hidden narratives is a proof of Muḥammad's prophethood, a verification of his truthfulness, and a cutting-off of the excuse of his deniers among the People of the Book. **[Saʿdī]** It is revelation, not learning from people; and the greatest purpose of the Qurʾānic stories is that the lesson be obtained by them and the argument established for God's oneness, the message and the resurrection. **[Qurṭubī]** "We reveal it to you" contains proof of Muḥammad's prophethood, in that he told of the story of Zachariah and Mary although he had not read the books, and the People of the Book confirmed him in it. **[Study Quran]** The prophecy of the account is precisely that the prophet was not present.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 28:44 and 28:46 for the parallel "you were not with them" about Sinai; 3:37 for Mary's entry into the Temple. **[Qurṭubī]** 5:111 and 16:68 for *waḥy* as inspiration; 5:3 for the *azlām*; 2:158 for the ordering question. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:37 for the custodianship, and Sūrah Maryam for the fuller narrative. **[Ṭabarī]** He cross-refers his earlier treatment of *ghayb*.

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:42–44 as a single unit; his material on the drawing of lots and on Mary's mother's presentation of her to the rabbis is cited here, and his material on the angels' command to Mary at 3:42–43.

**Reflection.** This is the verse in which the sūrah turns and addresses its own reader: the whole story just told is offered as evidence, and the evidence is that the teller was not there **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The argument is specifically designed for an audience that possesses the same archive — People of the Book who know the story is obscure and know the teller could not have read it **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The detail chosen to make the point is wonderfully unglamorous: not the annunciation or the virgin birth, but a group of men throwing pens into a river to decide who gets to raise a child **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Study Quran]**. And the passage carries a legal payload as well — the drawing of lots, permitted in some settings and gambling in others, is adjudicated right here, in the middle of a nativity **[Maʿārif]**.

## 3:45 — إِذْ قَالَتِ ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةُ يَٰمَرْيَمُ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُبَشِّرُكِ بِكَلِمَةٍۢ مِّنْهُ ٱسْمُهُ ٱلْمَسِيحُ عِيسَى ٱبْنُ مَرْيَمَ وَجِيهًۭا فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ وَمِنَ ٱلْمُقَرَّبِينَ

*When the angels said: "O Mary, God gives you glad tidings of a Word from Him, whose name is the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, high honoured in this world and in the Hereafter, and one of those brought near."*

**Meaning.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** This verse contains the glad tidings the angels brought to Mary that she would give birth to a mighty son who would have a great future. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "God gives you glad tidings of a Word from Him" — a son who would come into existence with a word from God, "Be", and he was. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "His name will be the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary" — and he will be known by this name in this life, especially by the believers; he was called *al-Masīḥ* because when he touched (*masaḥa*) those afflicted with an illness, they would be healed by God's leave; and "son of Mary" relates him to his mother because he had no father. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Held in honour in this world and in the Hereafter, and one of those who are near" — meaning he will be a leader, honoured by God in this life because of the law God reveals to him and the scripture He sends down to him, along with the other bounties He grants him; and he will be honoured in the Hereafter and will intercede with God, by His leave, on behalf of some people, just as his brethren the mighty messengers do. **[Jalālayn]** "Mention when the angels — namely Gabriel — said: 'O Mary, God gives you good tidings of a Word from Him', that is, a boy, 'whose name is the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary' — He addresses her, attributing him to her, in order to point out that she will give birth to him without a father, for the custom is to attribute the child to its father — 'honoured shall he be in this world', through prophethood, 'and the Hereafter', through his intercession and the high stations, 'and of those brought close to God'." **[Saʿdī]** "When the angels said, 'O Mary, God gives you glad tidings of a Word from Him, whose name is the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, high honoured in this world and the Hereafter, and of those brought near'" — that is, he has standing and great rank in this world and the next, with creation, and with all of that he is, with God, among those brought near, who are the closest of creatures to God and the highest of them in degree; and this is a glad tiding like no other glad tiding. **[Ṭabarī]** "High honoured" means possessed of a face and high station with God, of nobility and generosity; a man who is noble and whom kings and people magnify is called *wajīh*. **[Ṭabarī]** "And of those brought near" means: of those whom God brings near on the Day of Resurrection, settling him in His neighbourhood and drawing him close to Him — transmitted from Qatādah and al-Rabīʿ b. Anas. **[Study Quran]** *When* renders the particle *idh*, which some take as a continuation of the "when" at the beginning of 3:42, although it is read by others as connected to 3:44, so that it could be read "You were not with them when... and when..."; *when* may also serve as a connecting particle that does not necessarily give the sense of "when" or any other concrete meaning. **[Study Quran]** Some interpret Jesus' description as *a Word from Him* to mean that Jesus was a means by which God's Word became manifest in the world, in a manner analogous to the way one refers to the sultan as the "shadow of God on earth" to indicate that he is a means by which God's justice is manifest on earth; since Jesus was created "directly" by the word "Be!", one might also say that his association with the Word is stronger and more unmediated, in the way one says that a generous person is generosity itself. **[Maʿārif]** The previous verse, 3:44, appeared parenthetically between verses 45–46 and verses 42–43, and its purpose was to affirm the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ; the present verse resumes the story of Mary, which actually prepares the ground for the fuller account of the birth, mission and miracles of Jesus.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Wajīhan* is in the accusative as a "cut" from "ʿĪsā", because ʿĪsā is definite and *wajīh* indefinite, and it is his adjective; and if it were read in the genitive as following "the Word", that too would be permissible. **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-jāh* is a metathesis of *al-wajh*, the *wāw* having been moved from its beginning to the position of its *ʿayn*. **[Qurṭubī]** *Wajīhan* means noble, possessed of *jāh* and standing, and it is in the accusative as a ḥāl, according to al-Akhfash; *wa-min al-muqarrabīn* is conjoined to *wajīhan*, meaning "and brought near", also according to al-Akhfash; the plural of *wajīh* is *wujahāʾ* and *wijhāʾ*. **[Qurṭubī]** "His name is the Messiah" — and He did not say "her name", because the meaning of *kalimah* here is "child"; and *al-Masīḥ* is a title of Jesus meaning *al-ṣiddīq* (the truthful one), according to Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī; it is said to be arabicised, its origin being with *shīn*, and it is a shared word. **[Qurṭubī]** The exegetes differed over the derivation of "the Messiah son of Mary": it is said because he traversed (*masaḥa*) the earth, going through it without settling in a shelter; it is related from Ibn ʿAbbās that he never touched anyone afflicted but he was healed, so he was named *masīḥ* for that — *faʿīl* in the sense of *fāʿil*; it is said because he was anointed (*mamsūḥ*) with the oil of blessing with which the prophets used to anoint, of fragrant scent, so that when one was anointed with it, it was known that he was a prophet; it is said because he was flat-soled; it is said because beauty "touched" him; it is said because he was wiped with purity from sins. **[Qurṭubī]** Abū al-Haytham said: *al-masīḥ* is the opposite of *al-masīkh* — one says "God *masaḥahu*", meaning He created him with a good, blessed creation, and "*masakhahu*", meaning He created him accursed and ugly; Ibn al-Aʿrābī said: *al-masīḥ* is the truthful one and *al-masīkh* is the one-eyed, and thereby the Dajjāl is named; and Abū ʿUbayd said: *al-masīḥ* is originally, in Hebrew, *mashīḥā* with *shīn*, arabicised just as *Mūshā* was arabicised as *Mūsā*. **[Qurṭubī]** It is also said that *al-Masīḥ* is a name for Jesus that is not derived, by which God named him, in which case *ʿĪsā* is a substitution of the "he is he" type; and *ʿĪsā* is a non-Arabic name, which is why it does not take *tanwīn* — and if you make it Arabic it still does not, in the definite or the indefinite, because it contains the *alif* of feminisation; and it may be derived from *ʿāsahu yaʿūsuhu*, "he governed and looked after him". **[Qurṭubī]** The Dajjāl is called *masīḥ* because one of his eyes is wiped (*mamsūḥ*); and it is said *mīsīḥ* with *kasrah* and *shaddah* on the *sīn*; some say it with a pointed *khāʾ*, and some say *masīkh* with *fatḥah* and *takhfīf*, the first being the most famous and the one most hold — he is called that because he travels (*yasīḥu*) the earth, entering all its lands except Mecca, Medina and Bayt al-Maqdis; so the Dajjāl wipes the earth as a trial, and the son of Mary wipes it as a gift; and on the reading that he is wiped of the eye, it is *faʿīl* in the sense of *mafʿūl*. *(digression: the long lexicography of al-Masīḥ and the Dajjāl)*

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** Abū al-Sammān read *bi-kalimatin minhu*. **[Qurṭubī]** *Idh* is connected to "they were disputing" (*yakhtaṣimūn*), and it is permissible for it to be connected to "you were not with them".

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Qurṭubī]** In the Ṣaḥīḥ of Muslim, from Anas b. Mālik: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "There is no town but the Dajjāl will enter it, except Mecca and Medina"; and in the ḥadīth of ʿAbdallāh b. ʿAmr, "except the Kaʿbah and Bayt al-Maqdis", reported by Abū Jaʿfar al-Ṭabarī; Abū Jaʿfar al-Ṭaḥāwī added "and the Mosque of Ṭūr". **[Qurṭubī]** In the Ṣaḥīḥ of Muslim: "while he is like that, God will send the Messiah son of Mary, and he will descend at the white minaret to the east of Damascus, between two *mahrūdahs*, placing his palms on the wings of two angels; when he lowers his head, drops fall, and when he raises it, beads like pearls fall from him; no disbeliever who finds the scent of his breath but dies, and his breath reaches as far as his sight reaches; and he will seek him until he catches him at the gate of Ludd, and will kill him." **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, via Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "high honoured" means in this world and the next, with God. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said, on "and of those brought near": of those brought near to God on the Day of Resurrection; al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said the same. **[Study Quran]** *Messiah* renders *al-Masīḥ*, which corresponds closely in meaning to the Hebrew *mashiach*, "anointed one"; the commentators do not mention the later eschatological associations of this word in the Judeo-Christian tradition — even in Judaism the word was first used to designate anointed kings or priests and is used in the Bible as a title for the Persian king Cyrus, the high priest, David, the patriarchs, and Israel as a whole.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** The Word from God with which Jesus is described is a noble word, one God singled out Jesus for; otherwise it is among the words of God by which He brought creatures into being, as He said, "the likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam: He created him from dust, then said to him 'Be', and he was" (3:59). **[Study Quran]** The title *Messiah* is used here without the later Judeo-Christian eschatological associations. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The name "son of Mary" relates him to his mother because he had no father. **[Qurṭubī]** Mary is a prophetess, since the angels conveyed revelation to her from God as they did to the rest of the prophets; Jesus is honoured in this world by the law and scripture given him, and in the Hereafter by intercession.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 4:171c on Jesus as Word; 56:11 and 56:88, 83:21 and 83:28 for "those brought nigh" as the foremost believers; 4:172 for the angels as those brought nigh; 7:114 and 26:42 for Pharaoh's worldly use of the idea. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:39, "believing in the Word from God", said of John. **[Maʿārif]** 3:44, which interrupts the narrative to affirm Muḥammad's prophethood. **[Qurṭubī]** Sūrah Maryam for the descent of Jesus. **[Saʿdī]** 3:59, "the likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam".

**Reflection.** The annunciation to Mary is told with an exactness that is doing theological work: "a Word from Him", "the Messiah", "Jesus son of Mary" — each phrase narrowing or widening a claim **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Study Quran]**. Calling him "son of Mary" rather than naming a father is the Qurʾān's own emphasis, and the commentators say why: the custom is to attribute the child to the father, and there is no father to name **[Jalālayn]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. "A Word" is the phrase the sūrah will return to at 3:59 and 4:171, and both al-Ṭabarī's reading (created by the word "Be") and the Study Quran's reading (the one through whom God's Word became manifest) keep it on the creature side of the line **[Study Quran]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The lexicographers spend pages on *al-Masīḥ* — anointed, healer, traveller, wiped clean — and in the middle of it the Dajjāl appears, the false messiah who wipes the earth as a trial where the true one wipes it as a gift **[Qurṭubī]**. And the whole announcement is bracketed by 3:44, which broke in just before it to say that none of this was witnessed by the man now reciting it **[Maʿārif]**.

## 3:46 — وَيُكَلِّمُ ٱلنَّاسَ فِى ٱلْمَهْدِ وَكَهْلًۭا وَمِنَ ٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ

*"And he will speak to the people in the cradle and in manhood, and he will be one of the righteous."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The meaning of "and he will speak to the people in the cradle" is: God gives you glad tidings of a word from Him, named the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, high honoured with God, and one who will speak to people in the cradle. **[Ṭabarī]** "And he informed His servants of this regarding the Messiah, and that it was so — even though the general way of people is that they speak as mature men and as old men — as an argument thereby against them." **[Jalālayn]** "He shall speak to mankind in the cradle" — that is to say, as a child before the age of speech — "and in his manhood, and he is of the righteous." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "He will speak to the people, in the cradle and in manhood" — calling to the worship of God alone without partners while still in the cradle, as a miracle from God, and when he is a man, by God's revelation to him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And he will be one of the righteous" — in his statements and actions, for he will possess pure knowledge and righteous works. **[Saʿdī]** And of the completion of this glad tidings is that "he will speak to people in the cradle" — so his speaking will be a sign among the signs of God and a mercy from Him to his mother and to creation; and likewise he will speak to them "in his manhood" — in the state of his middle age — and this is the speech of prophethood, calling and guidance. **[Saʿdī]** His speech in the cradle contains signs and proofs of his truthfulness and prophethood, and of his mother's innocence of the evil thoughts held about her; and his speech in his middle age contains great benefit for creation, and his being an intermediary between them and their Lord in His revelation and the delivery of His religion and law; and with all that he is "of the righteous" — those whose hearts God has made sound by knowing Him and loving Him, whose tongues by praise of Him and remembrance of Him, and whose limbs by obeying Him and serving Him. **[Maʿārif]** One of the qualities of Jesus mentioned in this verse is that "he shall speak in the cradle", which means that he will speak while still a child, although no child has the ability to do so; when, soon after the birth of the infant Jesus, people chided Mary on the basis of a false accusation, this new-born infant spoke up, saying "I am a servant of Allah". **[Maʿārif]** The question arises: speaking in infancy was certainly a sign and a miracle, and it is quite appropriate to mention it here — but speaking to people during one's middle age is very normal, something everyone does, whether Muslim or non-Muslim, learned or illiterate; why then has it been mentioned as something special? **[Maʿārif]** One answer, given in *Bayān al-Qurʾān* by Maulana Ashraf Thanavi, is that the real purpose here is to focus particularly on the miraculous nature of meaningful infant speech: the reference to speech in middle age indicates that his childhood speech too would not be the usual baby-talk, but would be rational, even learned and eloquent, as a man's is in his advanced years. **[Maʿārif]** A further point: the reference to his speaking to people in his middle age provides a great standing argument in favour of the Islamic position, according to the Qurʾānic belief, that Jesus has been raised alive onto the heavens; ḥadīth narrations prove that when he was raised he was in the approximate age bracket of thirty to thirty-five years, which is youth at its best, and middle age — *kahl* in Arabic — was something he never reached during his life in this world; so his talking to people in his middle age can happen only when he graces this world once again with his presence. Therefore, just as his childhood speech was a miracle, his speech in middle age could be nothing else but another miracle. **[Study Quran]** On Jesus' speaking in the cradle, see the commentary on 19:29–31.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Yukallimu* is nominative in form, being free of governing operators, but it is in the position of the accusative, like the poet's line "I spent the night giving her supper with a sharp cutting blade, striking straight and crooked in its shanks" — where the second verb is in the position of an adjective on the first noun. *(digression: the grammatical note and its poetic witness)* **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-mahd* is the child's bed during his suckling — transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās through Ibn Jurayj. **[Ṭabarī]** *Kahl* is one past boyhood and before old age; one says "a man *kahl*" and "a woman *kahlah*", and he cites the *rajaz* verse "I shall not return after it as a camel-driver, dealing with *al-kahlah* and the boy". **[Qurṭubī]** "And he will speak to the people" is conjoined to *wajīhan*, according to al-Akhfash; *al-mahd* is the child's bed in his suckling — and "I prepared the affair" means I made it ready and smoothed it, as in 30:44, "for themselves they prepare"; and *imtahada* a thing means it rose, as the camel's hump rises. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-kahl* is between the state of boyhood and the state of old age, and a woman is *kahlah*; and *iktahallat al-rawḍah* is said when the light covers it all. **[Qurṭubī]** The meaning is: he will speak to people in the cradle as a sign, and he will speak to them as a *kahl* by revelation and messengership. **[Qurṭubī]** Abū al-ʿAbbās said: he spoke to them in the cradle when he vindicated his mother, saying "I am the servant of God" (19:30); as for his speaking while *kahl*, that is when God sends him down from heaven in the form of a son of thirty-three years — and that is the *kahl* — and he will say to them "I am the servant of God", as he said in the cradle; so these are two signs and two proofs. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Mahdawī said: the benefit of the verse is that He informed them that Jesus would speak to them in the cradle and would live until he spoke to them as a *kahl*, since the customary way is that one who speaks in the cradle does not live. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj said *wa-kahlan* means "and he will speak to people as a *kahl*"; al-Farrāʾ and al-Akhfash said it is conjoined to *wajīhan*; and it is said the meaning is "he will speak to people as a child and as a *kahl*". **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Jurayj related from Mujāhid: *al-kahl* is the forbearing one — but al-Naḥḥās said this is not known in the language, and *al-kahl* with the philologists is one who approaches forty; some say: one is called *ḥadath* up to sixteen, then *shābb* up to thirty-two, then he becomes *kahl* at thirty-three, according to al-Akhfash. **[Qurṭubī]** "And of the righteous" is conjoined to *wajīhan*, meaning: and he is of the worshipping righteous.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** Muḥammad b. Isḥāq recorded that Abū Hurayrah said that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "No infant spoke in the cradle except ʿĪsā and the companion of Jurayj." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded that Abū Hurayrah said that the Prophet ﷺ said: "No infant spoke in the cradle except three: ʿĪsā, the boy during the time of Jurayj, and another boy." **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: *al-mahd* is the child's bed during his suckling. **[Qurṭubī]** Abū Bakr b. Abī Shaybah recorded, from ʿAbdallāh b. Idrīs, from Ḥuṣayn, from Hilāl b. Yasāf, that "none spoke..." *(digression: the reports of the cradle-speakers, truncated in the source)*

**Belief.** **[Maʿārif]** The mention of speech in middle age is, for *Bayān al-Qurʾān*, a standing argument that Jesus has been raised alive to the heavens and will speak again on his return. **[Saʿdī]** Both speeches are signs: the cradle speech vindicating his mother and proving his prophethood, the later speech bringing benefit and guidance to creation. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He calls to the worship of God alone, in the cradle by miracle and in manhood by revelation. **[Qurṭubī]** Abū al-ʿAbbās and al-Mahdawī read the two speeches as paired signs — the miracle of infant speech and the survival that makes the later speech possible.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 19:29–31 for the cradle speech. **[Qurṭubī]** 19:30, "I am the servant of God"; 30:44 for the verb *mahhada*. **[Maʿārif]** 19:29–31 for the vindication of Mary. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:45, to whose *wajīhan* the phrase is conjoined. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:45 for the annunciation to which this verse belongs.

*Source note:* Qurṭubī and Maʿārif each treat 3:45–46 as a single unit; the lexicography of *al-Masīḥ* and the grammar of *wajīhan* are cited at 3:45, and the material on the cradle speech, on *kahl* and on the two speeches as paired signs is cited here.

**Reflection.** The verse makes a pointed choice: of all the things to say about the coming child, it says he will *speak* — twice, at the two ends of a life **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The cradle speech is the one the tradition dwells on, and its purpose is defensive: a newborn answers the accusation that is already being made against his mother **[Maʿārif]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The second speech is the puzzle the commentators cannot leave alone — since speaking at forty is unremarkable, it must either be saying that the infant's words were as mature as a grown man's, or pointing to a second speaking still to come **[Maʿārif]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The philologists tie the word *kahl* to a precise age — thirty-three — which is where the theological reading gets its leverage **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. And the closing phrase refuses to rest the matter on wonder: he is "of the righteous", a man of sound knowledge and sound works **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Jalālayn]**.

## 3:47 — قَالَتْ رَبِّ أَنَّىٰ يَكُونُ لِى وَلَدٌۭ وَلَمْ يَمْسَسْنِى بَشَرٌۭ ۖ قَالَ كَذَٰلِكِ ٱللَّهُ يَخْلُقُ مَا يَشَآءُ ۚ إِذَا قَضَىٰٓ أَمْرًۭا فَإِنَّمَا يَقُولُ لَهُۥ كُن فَيَكُونُ

*She said: "My Lord, how shall I have a child when no human being has touched me?" He said: "Thus does God create what He wills. When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, 'Be', and it is."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** Mary said, when the angels said to her that God gives her glad tidings of a word from Him: "My Lord, how shall I have a child — from which way shall I have a child? From a husband whom I marry and a spouse I wed, or will You originate his creation in me without spouse or male, and without any human touching me?" **[Ṭabarī]** God said to her: "Thus does God create what He wills" — meaning, thus does God create from you a child for you without any human touching you, making him a sign for the people and a lesson; for He creates what He wills and does what He intends, giving the child to whom He wills without a male and with a male, and withholding it from whom He wills even if she has a husband, because no creation of anything He wills to create is difficult for Him — He only commands, when He wills a thing that He wills, and says to it "Be", and it is, whatever He wills and however He wills. **[Jalālayn]** "She said 'Lord, how shall I have a child, when no mortal has touched me?' — neither through conjugality or otherwise. He said, the command: 'It is such that God will create from you a child without a father. God creates what He will. When He decrees a thing, willing its creation, He says to it only "Be" and it is' — that is, and 'he is'." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Mary said: "How can I have a son while I did not marry, nor intend to marry, nor am I an indecent woman, may God forbid?" **[Ibn Kathīr]** The angel conveyed to Mary God's answer: "So it will be, for God creates what He wills"; He is mighty in power and nothing escapes His ability. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God used the word "creates" here instead of the word "does", as in the story of Zachariah (3:40), in order to eradicate any evil thought concerning Jesus. **[Saʿdī]** "She said: 'My Lord, how shall I have a child when no human being has touched me?'" — and this is among the things that are found strange. **[Saʿdī]** "He said: 'Thus God creates what He wills'" — so that the servants may know that He has power over everything and that there is nothing to prevent His will. **[Maʿārif]** The glad tidings given by the angel regarding the birth of Jesus without a father surprised Mary, as she was a virgin; she wondered how it would happen — would that be a Divine miracle, or would she be asked to marry a man? **[Maʿārif]** The Divine answer eliminates the latter possibility: not restricted by it, "Allah creates what He wills" — that is, His will is all sufficient to bring something into existence; He needs no medium or cause; it is only His command "be" which makes things come into existence. **[Study Quran]** For Gabriel's announcement of Jesus to Mary, see 19:16–21.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "She said: 'My Lord'" — meaning "O my master", addressing Gabriel, for when he took form before her he said to her, "I am only the messenger of your Lord, to give you a pure boy"; and when she heard that from him she asked about the way of the child: "how shall I have a child when no human has touched me?" — that is, by marriage. **[Qurṭubī]** In her sūrah she adds, "and I was not unchaste" (19:20), mentioning this for emphasis, because her words "no human has touched me" cover both the lawful and the unlawful. **[Qurṭubī]** One says: the customary course God has established among His creatures is that a child comes only through marriage or fornication. **[Qurṭubī]** It is also said: she did not consider anything impossible for God's power, but wanted to know how this child would be — from a husband in the future, or would God create him at once? **[Ṭabarī]** *Anā* means "from which way", as he has explained in his treatment of the word elsewhere.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "'Thus God creates what He wills' — He does what He intends and creates what He wills, from a human or from other than a human; 'when He decrees a thing, He only says to it, "Be"' — whatever He wills and however He wills — 'and it is' what He intended." **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that Gabriel, when he said to her "thus God creates what He wills" — and "Thus, said your Lord: it is easy for Me" (19:9) — breathed into the opening of her garment and into her sleeve; Ibn Jurayj said this. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: Gabriel took the wrist-end of her shirt with his finger and breathed into it, and she conceived Jesus at that very hour. **[Qurṭubī]** Some said the blowing of Gabriel took place into her womb and she conceived thereby; and some said it is not admissible that the creation be from Gabriel's blowing, for then the child would be partly of the angels and partly of mankind — rather, the explanation is that when God created Adam and took the covenant from his offspring He placed some of the water in the loins of fathers and some of it in the wombs of mothers, and when the two waters meet they become a child; so God placed both waters in Mary, some in her womb and some in her loins, and Gabriel breathed into her in order to arouse her desire, since a woman does not conceive unless her desire is aroused; when her desire was aroused by Gabriel's breath, the water that was in her loins fell into her womb, the two waters mixed, and she conceived thereby — and that is His saying, "when He decrees a thing, He only says to it 'Be', and it is". *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the accounts of the manner of the conception)*

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The change of verb from "does" (3:40) to "creates" (3:47) removes any suspicion about Jesus' origin: he is created, not generated. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "When He has decreed something, He says to it only: 'Be!' — and it is": what God wills comes into existence instantly and without delay, as in 54:50, "And Our commandment is but one as the twinkling of the eye" — We only issue the command once, and it comes into existence instantly, as fast as, and faster than, a blink of the eye. **[Maʿārif]** God's will is all sufficient and needs no medium or cause. **[Saʿdī]** The answer is given so that the servants may know that He has power over everything and that nothing prevents His will. **[Ṭabarī]** He gives the child to whom He wills without a male and with a male, and withholds it from whom He wills even from a married woman.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 19:16–21 for Gabriel's announcement; 2:117c for "He only says to it, 'Be!'"; 6:73, 16:40, 19:35, 36:82 and 40:68 for the same formula; 3:59, where it is connected explicitly with Adam and Jesus. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:40, where the verb is "does" rather than "creates"; 54:50. **[Qurṭubī]** 19:20, "and I was not unchaste"; 19:9, "it is easy for Me"; and his treatment of "Be and it is" in al-Baqarah. **[Maʿārif]** 3:48, whose commentary frames this as the angel's glad tidings.

**Reflection.** Mary's question is the same question Zachariah asked, and the two are set side by side on purpose: an old man and a barren woman, then a woman no man has touched **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The commentators insist that hers is enquiry rather than refusal — she wants to know which door, not whether there is one — and they catch the precision of her phrasing, "no human being has touched me", which rules out both the lawful and the unlawful at once **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The answer is a single sentence about agency: God creates what He wills, and when He decrees He says "Be" **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Ibn Kathīr notices that the verb has been upgraded from "does" to "creates", and says why: the harder case needs the stronger word **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The reports about *how* the breath reached her are where the tradition becomes speculative, and al-Qurṭubī records even the awkward objections to them rather than smoothing them away **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:48 — وَيُعَلِّمُهُ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ وَٱلْحِكْمَةَ وَٱلْتَّوْرَىٰةَ وَٱلْإِنجِيلَ

*"And He will teach him the Book, and Wisdom, and the Torah, and the Gospel."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** This is the beginning of a report from God to Mary about what He will do for the child He has given her glad tidings of, of honour, elevation of rank and merit: "thus does God create from you a child, without male and without spouse, and He will teach him the Book" — which is the writing he writes with his own hand — "and the Wisdom" — which is the Sunnah He reveals to him in a book other than the Qurʾānic sense, i.e. without a book — "and the Torah" — the Torah which was sent down to Moses and was among them from the time of Moses — "and the Gospel" — the Gospel of Jesus, which did not exist before him, but God informed Mary before creating Jesus that He would reveal it to him. **[Ṭabarī]** He informed her of it and named it to her because she had already known, from what had been sent down of the books, that God would raise up a prophet to whom a book called the Gospel would be revealed; so God told her that that prophet — whose description she had heard, and whom He had promised His prophets beforehand, and upon whom He would send down the book called the Gospel — was the child He had granted her and given her glad tidings of. **[Jalālayn]** "And We will teach him" — read *nuʿallimuhu* or *yuʿallimuhu*, "He will teach him" — "the Book", that is, script, "wisdom and the Torah and the Gospel." **[Ibn Kathīr]** The good news brought to Mary about Jesus was even better because God would teach him "the Book and al-Ḥikmah"; it appears that the "Book" mentioned here refers to writing. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The Torah is the Book God sent down to Moses son of ʿImrān, while the Injīl is what God sent down to Jesus son of Mary, and Jesus memorised both books. **[Saʿdī]** "And He will teach him the Book" — that is, the genus of the previous scriptures — "and wisdom", judging between people — and He will give him prophethood and make him "a messenger to the Children of Israel", and support him with clear signs and overwhelming proofs. **[Maʿārif]** This verse recounts the august virtues of Jesus, who would be born blessed with the honour of learning from Allah: scriptures, wisdom, and specially the Torah and the Injīl; and that he will be sent as a Messenger of Allah to all of the Children of Israel. **[Study Quran]** Regarding the Qurʾānic understanding of the Torah and the Gospel, see the commentary on 3:3–4. **[Study Quran]** The Book and Wisdom appear together in many verses describing what was given to various prophets (2:151; 3:164; 4:54); they are used in connection with Jesus also in 5:110, and with the Children of Israel in 45:16.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Jurayj said: the Book is writing and script; and it is said that it is a book other than the Torah and the Gospel which God taught Jesus. **[Qurṭubī]** "And a messenger" means "and We will make him a messenger"; or "he will speak to them as a messenger"; and it is said that it is conjoined to *wajīhan*. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Akhfash said: you may also treat the *wāw* in "and a messenger" as inserted, with *messenger* as a circumstantial qualifier of the pronoun — the sense being "and He will teach him the Book [as] a messenger". **[Study Quran]** That Jesus was taught the Book and the Torah and the Gospel raises the question of what "the Book" refers to: some solve this by saying that the Book (*kitāb*) must mean the ability to write (*kitābah*); however, in Arabic the conjunction *wa* can in certain cases denote two nouns in apposition, as in 2:98, where Gabriel and Michael, who are archangels, are mentioned alongside the other angels — that is, in this verse the Book and the Torah would not necessarily refer to different things. **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-ḥikmah* is the Sunnah that God reveals to him without a book — transmitted from Qatādah.

**Readings.** **[Ṭabarī]** The readers differed: the generality of the readers of the Ḥijāz, Madīnah and some of the Kūfans read *yuʿallimuhu* with *yāʾ*, attaching it to "thus does God create what He wills", so that the report in "and He will teach him" follows the parallel report in "He creates what He wills" and "He only says to it 'Be' and it is". **[Ṭabarī]** The generality of the readers of Kūfah and some of the Baṣrans read *nuʿallimuhu* with *nūn*, conjoining it to "We reveal it to you", as though He said: "that is from the tidings of the Unseen which We reveal to you, and We will teach him the Book"; and they say that what follows "We reveal it to you" is in its relative clause up to "Be, and it is", and then "and We will teach him" is conjoined to it. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī's judgement: both are two variant readings that do not differ in meaning, so whoever reads either is correct, since the meaning of the two readings agrees — that it is a report from God that He will teach Jesus the Book and the rest of what He mentions teaching him. **[Jalālayn]** "And We will teach him" — *nuʿallimuhu*, or *yuʿallimuhu*, "He will teach him".

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn Jurayj said: "the Book" means with his own hand. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: "the Wisdom" is the Sunnah. **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said, on "and the Torah and the Gospel": Jesus used to read the Torah and the Gospel. **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: He informed her — meaning God informed Mary — of what He intended by him, and said "and He will teach him the Book and the Wisdom and the Torah", which had been among them from the time of Moses, "and the Gospel" — another book He originated for him, of which they had no knowledge beyond the mention that it would be from the prophets before him. **[Qurṭubī]** In the long ḥadīth of Abū Dharr: "the first of the prophets of the Children of Israel was Moses, and the last of them was Jesus."

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** God told Mary in advance the name of the book He would reveal to her son, so that she would know he was the promised prophet of whom she had read. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Jesus memorised both the Torah and the Gospel. **[Saʿdī]** God supported him with the knowledge of the previous scriptures and with wisdom in judging between people, along with prophethood and messengership. **[Study Quran]** The variant proposals about "the Book" all aim to say that Jesus was given a genuine revelation, not merely a skill — though the reading that makes it literacy is also ancient.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 3:3–4c on the Torah and the Gospel; 2:151, 3:164 and 4:54 for "the Book and Wisdom"; 5:110 for the same pair used of Jesus; 45:16 for the Children of Israel; 2:98 for the appositional *wa*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** His treatment of *al-ḥikmah* in Sūrah al-Baqarah; 5:110 for the parallel list. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:44, "We reveal it to you", to which the *nūn* reading is attached. **[Qurṭubī]** 5:110 for the parallel passage.

**Reflection.** The verse answers the wonder of 3:47 with a curriculum: the child will be taught — not merely born, but educated by God **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The list is precise and slightly surprising, because the Gospel did not yet exist: God names for Mary the very book He will one day send down to the child she is carrying, which makes the annunciation a promise with a title attached **[Ṭabarī]**. The variant readings — "He will teach him" or "We will teach him" — are settled by al-Ṭabarī with a shrug: two readings, one meaning **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. Whether "the Book" means literacy or scripture is the other old quarrel, and the Study Quran's note that the *wa* may simply be appositive keeps both alive **[Study Quran]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And the whole verse insists that revelation is learned, not only received: Jesus reads the Torah and the Gospel, and has them taught to him **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

## 3:49 — وَرَسُولًا إِلَىٰ بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ أَنِّى قَدْ جِئْتُكُم بِـَٔايَةٍۢ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ ۖ أَنِّىٓ أَخْلُقُ لَكُم مِّنَ ٱلطِّينِ كَهَيْـَٔةِ ٱلطَّيْرِ فَأَنفُخُ فِيهِ فَيَكُونُ طَيْرًۢا بِإِذْنِ ٱللَّهِ ۖ وَأُبْرِئُ ٱلْأَكْمَهَ وَٱلْأَبْرَصَ وَأُحْىِ ٱلْمَوْتَىٰ بِإِذْنِ ٱللَّهِ ۖ وَأُنَبِّئُكُم بِمَا تَأْكُلُونَ وَمَا تَدَّخِرُونَ فِى بُيُوتِكُمْ ۚ إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَءَايَةًۭ لَّكُمْ إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ

*"And [He will make him] a messenger to the Children of Israel: 'I have come to you with a sign from your Lord. I create for you out of clay the likeness of a bird, then I breathe into it, and it becomes a bird by God's leave. And I heal the blind and the leper, and I give life to the dead by God's leave, and I inform you of what you eat and what you store in your houses. Surely in that is a sign for you, if you are believers.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And a messenger to the Children of Israel" means: and We will make him a messenger, the words "We will make him" being omitted because the speech indicates them, as the poet said, "you saw your husband in the battle wearing a sword and a spear". **[Ṭabarī]** "I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" means: with a mark from your Lord that verifies my saying and confirms my report that I am a messenger from your Lord to you. **[Ṭabarī]** Then God explained what the sign is: "I create for you", so the sense is — and We will make him a messenger to the Children of Israel, on the basis that I have come to you with a sign from your Lord, namely that I create for you out of clay the likeness of a bird. **[Jalālayn]** "And He will make him to be a messenger to the Children of Israel", during his tender years or after puberty — Gabriel breathed into the opening of her garment and she became pregnant; what happened to her after this is mentioned later in Sūrah Maryam. **[Jalālayn]** "Thus when God sent him to the Children of Israel he said to them, 'I am God's Messenger to you' and 'I have come to you with a sign' — an indication of my truthfulness — 'from your Lord, and it is that I will create for you out of clay like the shape of a bird... then I will breathe into it', and it will be a bird, by the leave — the will — of God." **[Ibn Kathīr]** These are the miracles Jesus performed: he used to make the shape of a bird from clay and blow into it, and it became a bird by God's leave, and God made this a miracle for him to testify that He had sent him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I heal him who is *akmah*" — meaning a person who was born blind, which perfects this miracle and makes the challenge more daring; "and the leper", a known disease; "and I bring the dead to life by God's leave". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I inform you of what you eat and what you store in your houses" means: I tell you about what one of you has just eaten and what he is keeping in his house for tomorrow. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Surely, therein" — in all these miracles — "is a sign for you", testifying to the truth of what I was sent to you with, "if you believe". **[Saʿdī]** "I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" — indicating to you that I am the messenger of God in truth — "and that I create for you out of clay like the form of a bird and breathe into it and it becomes a bird by God's leave; and I heal the one born blind" — the one whose eyes are wiped and who has lost his sight — "and the leper, and I give life to the dead by God's leave, and I inform you of what you eat and what you store in your houses". **[Saʿdī]** "Surely in that which has been mentioned is a sign for you, if you are believers" — so God supported him with two kinds of signs: the extraordinary, astonishing proofs which none but the prophets can produce, and the message, the call and the religion he brought, and the fact that it is the religion of the Torah and of the prophets before him; and this is the greatest proof of the truthfulness of the truthful, for had he been one of the liars he would have opposed what the messengers brought and contradicted them in their fundamentals and their branches. **[Maʿārif]** The message he will carry to them will be his argument in favour of his prophethood; and in order that they believe, there are four signs or miracles enumerated in this verse that he would perform, being enough for willing believers. **[Study Quran]** For a discussion of the miracles mentioned in this verse, see the commentary on 5:110. **[Study Quran]** Jesus' giving life to the dead is accepted in both the Qurʾān and the Bible, but the Qurʾān emphasises that both his giving life to the clay bird — a miracle not found in the New Testament — and his raising of the dead happened by God's Leave.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-ṭayr* is the plural of *ṭāʾir*. **[Ṭabarī]** To the question how one can say "then I breathe into *it*" (*fīhi*, masculine) when what precedes is "the likeness of a bird", the answer is that the sense is "I breathe into the bird"; and "into *it*" (*fīhā*, feminine) would also be sound and admissible, as in 5:110, "then you breathe into it" — meaning into the form. **[Ṭabarī]** The Arabs do the same thing, saying "many a night I have spent *in* it" and "I have spent *it*". *(digression: the grammatical question and its poetic witness)* **[Ṭabarī]** "And I heal" means "and I cure": one says "God healed the sick man" when He cured him, "He heals him with a healing", and "the sick man recovered" — two well-known dialects. **[Ṭabarī]** The exegetes differed over *al-akmah*: Mujāhid said he is the one who sees by day and does not see by night; Qatādah said, "we used to be told that *al-akmah* is the one who is born blind, with the eyes covered"; Ibn ʿAbbās — through al-Ḍaḥḥāk and through Ibn Jurayj — said "the one born blind"; al-Suddī said "the blind man"; al-Ḥasan said "the blind"; ʿIkrimah said "the bleary-eyed". **[Ṭabarī]** The known meaning of *kamah* among the Arabs is blindness: one says "his eye became blind, it is becoming blind, with a blinding, and I blinded it" when one made it blind, as Suwayd said: "it blinded his two eyes until they turned white, and he blames himself now that he has desisted". **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-baras* is well known, a whiteness that affects the skin; *al-abraṣ* is the moon, and *sāmm abraṣ* is a known creature, the plural being *al-abāriṣ*; these two are singled out for mention because they are two incurable ailments, and since medicine was dominant in the time of Jesus, God showed them a miracle of that very kind.

**Readings.** **[Ṭabarī]** Some of the people of the Ḥijāz read "the likeness of *al-ṭāʾir*" and "it becomes a *ṭāʾir*" in the singular; others read "the likeness of *al-ṭayr*" and "it becomes *ṭayran*" in the plural in both places. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī prefers the reading with the plural in both, because that was a description of Jesus that he did this by God's leave, and because it agrees with the script of the muṣḥaf; following the script of the muṣḥaf, with soundness of meaning and the wide diffusion of the reading, is preferable to opposing the muṣḥaf. **[Jalālayn]** A variant reading for *annī*, "that I", has *innī*, "truly I", indicating a new independent sentence; *tayran* is also read *ṭāʾiran*. **[Qurṭubī]** Mujāhid, al-Zuhrī and al-Sakhtiyānī read "and what you *tadkhirūna*" with an unpointed *dhāl*, lightened.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq: Jesus sat one day with boys of the school, took some clay, and said, "Shall I make for you a bird out of this clay?" They said: "And you can do that?" He said: "Yes, by the leave of my Lord." He shaped it, and when he had made it in the form of a bird he blew into it and said, "Be a bird, by the leave of God", and it went out flying between his hands. The boys went out with the report and mentioned it to their teacher, and he spread it among the people; and when Jesus grew up, the Children of Israel were suspicious of him, and his mother feared for him, and she carried him on a small donkey and fled with him. **(Isrāʾīliyyāt)** **[Ṭabarī]** It is mentioned that when he wanted to create the bird from clay he asked them, "which bird is strongest in creation?" and they said: the bat, for it is only flesh; so he did it — transmitted from Ibn Jurayj. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that he created for them the bat, because it is the most complete of birds in creation, so as to be more eloquent as a display of power: it has a breast, teeth and an ear, menstruates, becomes pure and gives birth; and it is said they asked him for a bat because it is more wondrous than the rest of creation — among its wonders that it is flesh and blood yet flies without feathers, gives birth as animals give birth rather than laying eggs like the rest of birds, has a udder from which milk comes, cannot see in the light of day or the darkness of night but sees only in two hours, an hour after sunset and an hour after the break of dawn before it grows fully light, laughs as a human laughs, and menstruates as a woman menstruates. **[Qurṭubī]** Wahb said: it would fly as long as the people were looking at it, and when it went out of their sight it fell dead, so that the act of the creature might be distinguished from the act of God. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that he gave life to four persons: al-ʿĀdhir, who was a friend of his; the son of the old woman; the daughter of the tithe-collector; and Sām the son of Noah. Al-ʿĀdhir had died some days before, and he supplicated God and he rose by God's leave with his body-fluid dripping, and lived and had children. The son of the old woman: Jesus passed by him as he was being carried on his bier, supplicated God, and he rose, put on his clothes, carried the bier on his neck and went back to his family. The daughter of the tithe-collector: a night had passed since her death, and he supplicated God and she lived afterwards and had children. When they saw that, they said: you only revive those whose death was recent — perhaps they had not died but suffered a swoon; so revive for us Sām the son of Noah. He said: show me his grave — and he went out with the people to his grave and supplicated God, and he came out of his grave with his head gone white. Jesus said to him: how did your head go white when there was no white hair in your time? He said: O Spirit of God, you called me and I heard a voice saying "answer the Spirit of God", and I thought the Resurrection had come, and from the terror of it my head went white. He asked him about the agony of death and he said: O Spirit of God, the bitterness of the agony of death has not left my throat — and more than four thousand years had passed since his death. He said to the people: believe him, for he is a prophet; and some of them believed him and some denied him and said, "this is magic". *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the named raisings of the dead)* **[Qurṭubī]** It is related through Ismāʿīl b. ʿAyyāsh, from Muḥammad b. Ṭalḥah, that when Jesus son of Mary wanted to give life to the dead he would pray two units of prayer, reciting in the first "Blessed is He in Whose hand is the dominion" (Sūrah al-Mulk) and in the second "Tanzīl al-Sajdah", and when he finished he would praise God and extol Him, then supplicate with seven names: O Eternal, O Hidden, O Enduring, O Singular, O Odd, O One, O Sufficient — related by al-Bayhaqī, who said its chain is not strong. *(weak)* **[Qurṭubī]** When he had given life to the dead for them, they asked for another sign, saying: tell us what we eat in our houses and what we store for tomorrow; and he told them: so-and-so, you ate such-and-such, and you ate such-and-such and stored such-and-such — and that is His saying, "and I inform you". **[Qurṭubī]** Saʿīd b.
