Jubayr and others said: he used to tell the boys in the school what they were storing, until their fathers forbade them to sit with him; Qatādah said: he told them what they had eaten of the table spread (*al-māʾidah*) and what they had stored of it secretly. **[Study Quran]** Some relate that as a child Jesus would tell his playmates or his classmates about what their parents were doing or what they kept in their houses; others say that this was in relation to a time when the people were forbidden to store up food in their houses; others say it refers to the table onto which food descended in 5:112–15, adding that they were to partake of the feast but not store any of it — they did, and were chastised for it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Many scholars have stated that God sent every prophet with a miracle suitable to his time: Moses came in an age of magic, and God gave him a miracle that captured the eyes and bewildered every magician, so that the magicians embraced Islam when they recognised it came from the Almighty; Jesus was sent in a time when medicine and the knowledge of physics were advancing, and he brought the kinds of miracle no physician could perform — how can any physician bring life to clay, cure blindness and leprosy, and bring back those held in the grave? — while Muḥammad ﷺ was sent in a time of eloquent people and proficient poets, and brought them a Book which mankind and jinn could not imitate in ten chapters, or even one, even working together, because it is the Word of God and nothing like the speech of creatures.

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** Making the shape of a bird was the making of a picture, something permitted in that Sharīʿah; in our Sharīʿah its permissibility was abrogated.

**Belief.** **[Saʿdī]** God supported him with two kinds of proof — the wonder-working signs no one but a prophet can produce, and the religion itself, which agrees with what the earlier messengers brought. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The miracles suit the age and are a testimony from God that He sent him. **[Study Quran]** The Qurʾān stresses that the miracles happened *by God's Leave*, so that no divinity may be misattributed to Jesus. **[Qurṭubī]** The bird fell dead once out of sight, so that the act of the creature might be distinguished from the act of the Creator.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 5:110c for the miracles; 5:112–15 for the table. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 43:63, "and in order to make clear to you some of that wherein you differ". **[Qurṭubī]** Sūrah al-Mulk and Sūrah al-Sajdah for the reported prayer; 5:110 for the parallel passage. **[Ṭabarī]** 5:110 for the feminine pronoun in "then you breathe into it". **[Saʿdī]** 3:50, where the confirmation of the Torah is added to the signs.

*Source note:* Qurṭubī treats 3:48–49 as a single unit, Maʿārif and Ibn Kathīr treat 3:48–50 as one; the material on the teaching of the Book is cited at 3:48, and the material on the signs and miracles here.

**Reflection.** The signs are chosen to match the audience: an age of physicians is answered by cures no physician can perform **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Yet each sign is hedged with "by God's leave", twice, and the tradition notices even the mechanics: the clay bird flies only while it is watched and drops dead out of sight, so that no one confuses the maker of the shape with the giver of life **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. The reports of *who* was raised — a friend, a widow's son, a tax-collector's daughter, and then Sām son of Noah, whose hair turns white at the sound of the call — are told with a storyteller's relish and sit at the edge of what the verse warrants, which is why they are flagged here as Isrāʾīliyyāt **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Ibn Kathīr's comparison of the three prophets is the theological summary of the whole passage: Moses in the age of magic, Jesus in the age of medicine, Muḥammad in the age of poetry, each given the unanswerable thing **[Ibn Kathīr]**. And the last sign is the least spectacular and the most domestic — what you ate, what you kept back — which means no one in the crowd could shrug it off **[Saʿdī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:50 — وَمُصَدِّقًۭا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَىَّ مِنَ ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ وَلِأُحِلَّ لَكُم بَعْضَ ٱلَّذِى حُرِّمَ عَلَيْكُمْ ۚ وَجِئْتُكُم بِـَٔايَةٍۢ مِّن رَّبِّكُمْ فَٱتَّقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَأَطِيعُونِ

*"And [I come] confirming what was before me of the Torah, and to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you. And I have come to you with a sign from your Lord; so fear God and obey me."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is: and I have come to you with a sign from your Lord, and I have come to you confirming what was before me of the Torah — which is why *muṣaddiqan* is in the accusative as a circumstantial qualifier from "I have come to you", and not conjoined to *wajīhan*; what shows this is the phrase "what is before *me*", for were it conjoined to *wajīhan* the wording would have been "confirming what is before *him*" and "and to make lawful" in the third person. **[Ṭabarī]** Jesus used to believe in the Torah and affirm it, and that it is from God — as do all the prophets, who affirm every book and messenger of God before them, even though some of the rulings of their laws differ, because God made them differ in that. **[Ṭabarī]** And Jesus — as we have been told — acted by the Torah and did not depart from any of its rulings, except what God lightened for its people in the Gospel of what had been made hard for them in it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I have come confirming that which was before me of the Torah" — affirming the Torah and upholding it; "and to make lawful to you part of what was forbidden to you". **[Ibn Kathīr]** This part of the verse indicates that Jesus abrogated some of the laws of the Torah and informed the Jews of the truth regarding some issues that they used to dispute about, as in 43:63. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I have come to you with a proof from your Lord" — containing affirmation and evidence of the truth of what I am conveying. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "So revere God and obey me. Truly, God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him" — for I and you are equal in our servitude, submission and humbleness to Him; "this is the straight path". **[Jalālayn]** "Likewise I have come to you confirming that which was before me of the Torah and to make lawful for you some of that which was forbidden to you in it" — thus he made lawful for them fish and birds which had no spikes; and it is also said that he made it all lawful for them, so that "some" means "all" — "I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" (repeated for emphasis and to expand upon it), "so fear God and obey me" in what I command you of affirming God's oneness and being obedient to Him. **[Saʿdī]** "And confirming what is before me of the Torah" — so God supported him with two kinds of signs and overwhelming proofs; "and to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you" — that is, to lighten from you some of the burdens and shackles; "so fear God and obey me; God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him" — and this is what all the messengers call to: the worship of God alone without partner, and obedience to them; and this is the straight path which whoever travels is brought to the gardens of bliss. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 50 says that Jesus will declare that he has come to confirm the Torah which was revealed before his coming, and to make lawful what remained unlawful for them in the law of Moses — which means that the unlawfulness of some things in the earlier code would stand abrogated by the new one, that of Jesus, whose station of prophethood was the conclusive argument for that claim of abrogation; and the proof of his truth were the signs from their Lord. **[Maʿārif]** Once his prophethood is established, verse 51 states that Jesus will ask them to beware of any contravention of the Divine commandments, fear Allah and follow his teachings in matters of religion — which, in a nutshell, are that "Allah is my Lord and your Lord" (the ultimate in belief) and "Worship Him" (the ultimate in deeds); this, then, is the straight path which helps perfect the ideal combination of beliefs and deeds, leads to the way of salvation and is the source of communion with Allah. **[Study Quran]** Jesus is also said to confirm the Torah in 5:46 and 61:6; the language of confirmation echoes the manner in which the Qurʾān is said to confirm earlier revelations (2:41, 91, 97; 3:3; 4:47; 5:46; 35:31; 46:30; 61:6).

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Muṣaddiqan* is conjoined to "and a messenger"; and it is also said that the meaning is "and I have come to you confirming". **[Qurṭubī]** "What is before me" means what was before me. **[Qurṭubī]** "And to make lawful for you" contains an ellipsis — the sense being "and to make lawful for you [I have] come to you". **[Qurṭubī]** Abū ʿUbaydah allowed "some" to mean "all", citing the verse of Labīd; but this is an error according to the people of reasoning among the philologists, because "some" and "part" cannot mean "all" in this place — Jesus only made lawful for them some of the things Moses had forbidden them, such as eating fats, and he did not make lawful killing, theft or indecency. **[Qurṭubī]** "Some" may, however, be placed in the sense of "all" when a contextual indicator is joined to it, as Ṭarafah said to ʿAmr b. Hind: "Abū Mundhir, you have destroyed, so spare some of us — your kindness! — some evil is lighter than some", meaning some evil is lighter than all of it. **[Qurṭubī]** "And I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" — the sign is made singular although they are signs, because they are one genus in indicating his message. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Nakhaʿī read "some of that which *ḥaruma* ʿalaykum", like "karuma", meaning "became forbidden to you".

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From Wahb b. Munabbih: Jesus was upon the law of Moses, and he used to keep the Sabbath and face Bayt al-Maqdis, and he said to the Children of Israel: "I have not called you to oppose a letter of what is in the Torah, except to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you and to lift from you some of the burdens." **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said, on "confirming what was before me of the Torah and to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you": what Jesus brought was gentler than what Moses brought, and there had been forbidden to them in what Moses brought the flesh of camels and the caul fat, and some birds and fish. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said the same, adding: and God had forbidden them the fats and permitted them in what Jesus brought, and some fish, and some birds that have no spur, and things He had forbidden them and made hard for them — so Jesus came to them with alleviation from it in the Gospel, and what Jesus brought was gentler than what Moses brought. **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn Jurayj said: the flesh of camels and the fats; when Jesus was sent he made them lawful, and he was sent to the Jews and they differed and split. **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "confirming what was before me of the Torah" — what preceded me of it — "and to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you" — I inform you that it was forbidden to you and you left it, then He made it lawful. **[Qurṭubī]** It is related from Qatādah that Jesus came to them with something gentler than what Moses came with, because Moses came to them with the prohibition of camels and some of the fats, and Jesus came to them making some of them lawful. **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that Jesus only made lawful for them what had been forbidden to them because of their own sins and was not in the Torah, such as eating fats and every clawed animal; and it is said that he only made lawful things which the rabbis had forbidden them and which were not forbidden to them in the Torah.

**Rulings.** **[Study Quran]** To make lawful part of what was forbidden is understood to pertain to certain dietary restrictions, but not to fundamental moral injunctions such as those against murder and adultery. **[Study Quran]** What was previously forbidden and is now made lawful was either some of the rules in the Torah, or restrictions the Israelites imposed upon themselves in succeeding generations; this latter interpretation — that Jesus did not change any aspect of Moses' original revelation — is supported by Jesus' words in 43:63, "I have come unto you with wisdom and to make clear to you some of that wherein you differ". **[Qurṭubī]** "Some" cannot here mean "all", since Jesus did not make lawful killing, theft or indecency; what he made lawful was foods, such as the flesh of camels and the fats. **[Jalālayn]** He made lawful for them fish and birds that had no spikes; and it is said he made all of it lawful, so that "some" means "all".

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** "So revere God and obey me. Truly, God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him" — for I and you are equal in our servitude, submission and humbleness to Him; and "this is the straight path". **[Maʿārif]** The religion Jesus calls to is summed up in two sentences: "Allah is my Lord and your Lord" — the ultimate in belief — and "Worship Him" — the ultimate in deeds; and this is the straight path which perfects the ideal combination of beliefs and deeds. **[Saʿdī]** This is what all the messengers call to: the worship of God alone without partner, and obedience to them. **[Study Quran]** "Reverence God and obey me" is spoken also by the prophets Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot and Shuʿayb; and the statement that "God is my Lord and your Lord" is spoken by Jesus in 5:72, 5:117, 19:36 and 43:64, by Hūd in 11:56, and by Moses in 40:27 and 44:20.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 5:46 and 61:6 for Jesus' confirmation of the Torah; 2:41, 2:91, 2:97, 3:3, 4:47, 5:46, 35:31, 46:30, 61:6 for the language of confirmation; 4:160 and 6:146 for the dietary prohibitions in the Torah; 3:93 for restrictions the Israelites imposed on themselves; 43:63; 26:108, 126, 144, 163, 179 for the other prophets' "reverence God and obey me"; 5:72, 5:117, 19:36, 43:64, 11:56, 40:27, 44:20 for "God is my Lord and your Lord". **[Ibn Kathīr]** 43:63, "I have come unto you with wisdom and to make clear to you some of that wherein you differ". **[Qurṭubī]** His treatment of *bayna yadayhi* earlier in this sūrah. **[Maʿārif]** 3:51, where the call is completed.

*Source note:* Maʿārif and Ibn Kathīr treat 3:48–50 as a single unit; their material on the signs and on the teaching of the Book is cited at 3:48–49, and their material on the confirmation of the Torah, on the lifting of prohibitions and on the closing call here.

**Reflection.** Jesus is presented here doing exactly what the Qurʾān says of itself: confirming what came before while adjusting some of what it bound **[Study Quran]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. The adjustment is narrowed with care — dietary restrictions, not murder and adultery; some, not all — and a grammarian's quarrel about whether "some" can mean "all" is settled by pointing at what nobody claims he permitted **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. There are two opposed traditions underneath: that he lifted genuine Torah prohibitions, and that he only lifted burdens the rabbis had invented, or that their own sins had brought upon them — and the second lets Jesus say, with Wahb, that he has not contradicted a single letter of Moses **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. What he does not do is the thing the sūrah is arguing against: he places himself on the far side of the line, "God is my Lord and your Lord", and tells them to worship Him **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The phrase "reverence God and obey me" is the same formula the earlier Arabian prophets used, which quietly folds Jesus into a single company of callers rather than setting him apart **[Study Quran]**.

## 3:51 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ رَبِّى وَرَبُّكُمْ فَٱعْبُدُوهُ ۗ هَٰذَا صِرَٰطٌۭ مُّسْتَقِيمٌ

*"God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him. This is a straight path."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** The sense is: and I have come to you with a sign from your Lord by which you know with certainty my truthfulness in what I say — "so fear God", O assembly of the Children of Israel, in what He commanded you and forbade you in His book which He sent down to Moses, and fulfil His covenant which you covenanted in it; "and obey me", in what I call you to of affirming me in what my Lord and your Lord has sent me with — "so worship Him", for it is with that He sent me to you, and with making lawful some of what was forbidden to you in your book; and that is the upright path and the firm guidance in which there is no crookedness. **[Ṭabarī]** He transmits from Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr, on "so fear God and obey me; God is my Lord and your Lord": a disavowal of what they say about him — meaning what the Christians say about him — and an argument for his Lord against them; and on "so worship Him, this is a straight path": this is what I have laid upon you and brought you. **[Jalālayn]** "Surely God is my Lord and your Lord so worship Him. This that which I enjoin upon you is a straight path" — but they rejected him and did not believe in him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "So revere God and obey me. Truly, God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him" — for I and you are equal in our servitude, submission and humbleness to Him; and "this is the straight path". **[Saʿdī]** "And to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you" — that is, to lighten from you some of the burdens and shackles; "so fear God and obey me; God is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him" — and this is what all the messengers call to: the worship of God alone without partner, and obedience to them. **[Saʿdī]** This is the straight path which whoever travels is brought to the gardens of bliss. **[Maʿārif]** Once his prophethood is established, this verse states that Jesus will ask them to beware of any contravention of the Divine commandments, fear Allah, and follow his teachings in matters of religion — which, in a nutshell, are that "Allah is my Lord and your Lord" (the ultimate in belief) and "Worship Him" (the ultimate in deeds). **[Maʿārif]** This, then, is the straight path which helps perfect the ideal combination of beliefs and deeds, leads to the way of salvation and is the source of communion with Allah.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" means: and I have come to you with a proof (*ḥujjah*) and a lesson (*ʿibrah*) from your Lord, by which you know the truth of what I say to you. **[Ṭabarī]** Mujāhid said: "I have come to you with a sign from your Lord" means all the things Jesus made clear to them, and what his Lord gave him — transmitted through two chains. **[Ṭabarī]** "From your Lord" means: from your Lord. **[Ṭabarī]** He cross-refers his earlier treatment of *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*.

**Readings.** **[Ṭabarī]** The readers differed over "God is my Lord and your Lord": the generality of the readers of the cities read *inna Allāha rabbī wa-rabbukum* with *kasrah* on the *alif* of *inna*, as the beginning of a new report. **[Ṭabarī]** Some read *anna Allāha rabbī wa-rabbukum* with *fatḥah* on the *alif* of *anna*, taking it as: "and I have come to you with a sign from your Lord — that God is my Lord and your Lord", attaching *an* to "the sign" as a substitution from it. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ṭabarī's judgement: the correct reading is what the readers of the cities are upon, namely *kasrah* on the *alif* of *inna* as the beginning of a report, because of the consensus of the authoritative body of readers upon its soundness; what they have agreed upon is a proof, and what an isolated reader holds alone is mere opinion, and opinion is not set against proof.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** This verse, though it comes out in the form of a report, contains the conclusive argument (*al-ḥujjah al-bālighah*) of God for His messenger Muḥammad ﷺ against the delegation of the people of Najrān who disputed with him about Jesus: God informs them that Jesus was free of what those who attributed him to other than what he described himself as attributed to him — namely that he is a servant of God like the rest of His servants among the people of the earth, except for what God singled him out for of prophethood, and the proofs He gave him as evidence of his truthfulness, just as He gave the rest of the messengers other than him signs and evidences of their truthfulness, and as a proof of his prophethood. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Jesus and his people are equal in servitude, submission and humbleness to God — which is the point of placing this sentence in his mouth. **[Saʿdī]** The verse is the summary of what every messenger has called to. **[Maʿārif]** Belief and deeds are each summed up in one half-verse: "Allah is my Lord and your Lord" and "Worship Him".

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** 3:50, to which the *fatḥah* reading attaches "that God is my Lord"; and his earlier treatment of *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 43:63, "I have come unto you with wisdom and to make clear to you some of that wherein you differ". **[Study Quran]** 5:72, 5:117, 19:36 and 43:64 for the same statement spoken by Jesus; 11:56 by Hūd; 40:27 and 44:20 by Moses. **[Saʿdī]** 24:55 for the promise of succession.

**Reflection.** The sentence is placed in Jesus' mouth rather than God's, which is the whole argument: the one they call divine says "my Lord and your Lord" **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. Al-Ṭabarī sees the verse as God's proof put into Jesus' own speech for the benefit of the Najrān delegation, and the proof is that he describes himself as a servant **[Ṭabarī]**. The variant reading — *inna* or *anna* — is not a curiosity: the second would make the whole thing the content of the sign rather than a fresh declaration, and al-Ṭabarī dismisses it on the ground that an isolated reading cannot override the agreed text **[Ṭabarī]**. The closing words "fear God and obey me" bind the two together, creed and command, so that neither can be kept without the other **[Saʿdī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. And the final line is as plain as the sūrah ever gets: this is the straight path **[Jalālayn]**.

*Source note:* The Study Quran has no separate block for 3:51 (its note on 3:50–51 was cited at 3:50), and Qurṭubī's block for 3:50–51 comments only on the phrases of 3:50; his material is therefore cited there and there is nothing distinct from him to cite here.

## 3:52 — فَلَمَّآ أَحَسَّ عِيسَىٰ مِنْهُمُ ٱلْكُفْرَ قَالَ مَنْ أَنصَارِىٓ إِلَى ٱللَّهِ ۖ قَالَ ٱلْحَوَارِيُّونَ نَحْنُ أَنصَارُ ٱللَّهِ ءَامَنَّا بِٱللَّهِ وَٱشْهَدْ بِأَنَّا مُسْلِمُونَ

*Then when Jesus sensed disbelief in them, he said: "Who will be my helpers unto God?" The disciples said: "We are the helpers of God; we believe in God, and bear witness that we are Muslims."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "Then when Jesus sensed (*aḥassa*) disbelief in them" means: then when Jesus found (*wajada*) disbelief from them. **[Ṭabarī]** The sense of the whole is: when Jesus found, from the Children of Israel to whom God sent him, denial of his prophethood, rejection of his saying, and turning away from what he called them to of the command of God, he said: "Who will be my helpers unto God?" — meaning: who will be my aides against those who reject the proof of God, those who turn away from His religion, and those who deny the prophethood of His prophet — "unto God"? **[Ṭabarī]** "Unto God" (*ilā Allāh*) means "with God" (*maʿa Allāh*); it is fitting for the Arabs, when they join a thing to another and then report about the two by joining one with the other that is joined to it, to put *ilā* in place of *maʿa* at times — as in the saying "a few camels *to* a few camels are a herd", meaning: when you join a few to a few they become a herd. But when a thing is simply *with* another, they do not express it with *ilā*, and it is not permissible to say "so-and-so arrived and *to* him is wealth" meaning "and with him is wealth". **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Suddī said: "who will be my helpers unto God" means "with God". **[Jalālayn]** "And when Jesus sensed" — when he became aware of their disbelief, and they plotted to kill him — "he said 'Who will be my helpers, departing unto God?'" — to help His religion. **[Jalālayn]** "The disciples said 'We will be helpers of God'" — those who assist His religion; they were Jesus' intimates and the first to believe in him, twelve men who were of pure white complexion (*hawar*); but some say they were called *ḥawāriyyūn* because they were bleachers of clothes (*qaṣṣārūn*) — "we believe in", we accept the truth of, "God; witness, O Jesus, that we have submitted". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Then when ʿĪsā came to know" means: ʿĪsā felt that they were adamant in disbelief and continuing in misguidance. **[Ibn Kathīr]** On "who will be my helper in God's cause": Mujāhid commented, "meaning, who would follow me to God?" — however, it appears that ʿĪsā was asking, "Who would help me convey the Message of God?" **[Ibn Kathīr]** *Ḥawārī* in Arabic means "support"; and the Two Ṣaḥīḥs recorded that the Prophet ﷺ said, "Every prophet has a *ḥawārī*, and al-Zubayr is my *ḥawārī*". **[Saʿdī]** At that point the factions of the Children of Israel differed concerning Jesus: some of them believed in him and followed him, and some disbelieved in him, denied him and accused his mother of indecency — the Jews. **[Saʿdī]** "Then when Jesus sensed disbelief from them" — and their agreement to reject his call — "he said", calling the Children of Israel to support him: "Who will be my helpers unto God?" The disciples — that is, the helpers — said: "We are the helpers of God; we believe in God, and bear witness that we are Muslims" — and this is among God's favours to them and to Jesus, that He inspired these disciples to believe in him, to submit to obeying him and to help His messenger. **[Maʿārif]** Following the blessed predictions mentioned earlier, Jesus was born with all the promised signs; as a messenger sent to the Children of Israel he presented his call to them supported by miracles to prove his prophethood — but they were adamant and kept rejecting him as a prophet, even to the point of physical hostility. **[Study Quran]** The disbelief is understood to mean actual hostility beyond simple rejection, and commentators contextualise this verse in relation to Jesus' being in exile or fleeing from those wishing to kill him. **[Study Quran]** *Helpers unto God* is understood to mean "helpers [along] with God", so that the question means more specifically "Who will add their help to that of God?" or "Who will be my helpers [in my fleeing] unto God?"

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-iḥsās* is finding, as in 19:98, "do you perceive (*tuḥissu*) any of them?"; *al-ḥass* without the *alif* is destroying and killing, as in 3:152, "when you were slaying them (*taḥussūnahum*) by His leave"; and *al-ḥass* is also tenderness and pity, as in the line of al-Kumayt: "is there one who wept at the abode hoping that it would feel pity (*an taḥissa*) for him, or would the water of his copious tears make the abode weep?" — meaning "that it would soften towards him". *(digression: the lexicography of ḥ-s-s)* **[Qurṭubī]** "Then when Jesus sensed disbelief in them" — from the Children of Israel; *aḥassa* means "he knew and found", according to al-Zajjāj; Abū ʿUbaydah said it means "he recognised", the root being the finding of a thing by sense-perception. **[Qurṭubī]** "Disbelief in them" means disbelief in God; and it is said that he heard from them the word of disbelief; and al-Farrāʾ said: they intended to kill him. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Suddī, al-Thawrī and others said the meaning of "unto God" is "with God", *ilā* having the sense of *maʿa*, as in 4:2, "do not consume their wealth *with* your wealth"; al-Ḥasan said the meaning is "who will be my helpers in the way *to* God", since he was calling them to God; and it is said the meaning is "who will join his help to the help of God" — and on these two views *ilā* is in its own proper sense, and that is the good reading. **[Qurṭubī]** The disciples (*al-ḥawāriyyūn*) are the companions of Jesus, and they were twelve men — said by al-Kalbī and Abū Rawq. **[Qurṭubī]** They differed over why they were named that: Ibn ʿAbbās said they were named for the whiteness of their clothes, and they were fishermen; Ibn Abī Najīḥ and Ibn Arṭāh said they were bleachers (*qaṣṣārūn*) and were named for their whitening of clothes. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt: the story of the single dye-bath and the many-coloured cloths)* **[Jalālayn]** They were twelve men, called *ḥawāriyyūn* either for their pure white complexion (*hawar*) or because they were bleachers of clothes. **[Maʿārif]** The word *ḥawāriyy* is derived from *hawar*, which lexically means whiteness; in religious terminology the title has been given to the sincere disciples or companions of Jesus either because of their sincerity and the purity of their hearts, or because of their white dress. **[Maʿārif]** Some commentators give the number of the disciples as twelve; and the word is also used in the absolute sense of "helper" or "supporter", which is the sense of the ḥadīth, "Every prophet has a *ḥawāriyy*, that is, a sincere companion; Zubayr is my *ḥawāriyy*". **[Study Quran]** *Apostles* translates *ḥawāriyyūn*, a word that may be derived from the Ethiopic *ḥawārya*.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The Prophet ﷺ said during the Hajj season, before the Hijrah: "Who will give me asylum so that I can convey the Speech of my Lord, for the Quraysh have prevented me from conveying the Speech of my Lord?" — until he found the Anṣār; they helped him, gave him refuge, he migrated to them, they comforted him and protected him from all his enemies. **[Ibn Kathīr]** This is similar to what happened with ʿĪsā, for some of the Children of Israel believed in him, gave him their aid and support, and followed the light that was sent with him. **[Ibn Kathīr]** In the Two Ṣaḥīḥs: when the Prophet ﷺ encouraged the people to fight during the battle of al-Aḥzāb, al-Zubayr came forward, and again when he asked a second time — and the Prophet ﷺ said, "Every prophet has a *ḥawārī*, and al-Zubayr is my *ḥawārī*". **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan and Mujāhid said: he sought help so as to be shielded by it from his people and to make the call manifest; and this is God's way with His prophets and His friends — as Lot said, "would that I had against you some power, or that I could take refuge in a strong support" (11:80), meaning a clan and companions to help him. **[Qurṭubī]** ʿAṭāʾ said: Mary entrusted Jesus to various trades, and the last she handed him over to was the disciples, who were bleachers and dyers; when Jesus' teacher wanted to travel he said to Jesus, "I have many cloths of different colours, and I have taught you the dye — so dye them"; and Jesus cooked a single vat, put all the cloths into it and said, "Be, by God's leave, as I intend you to be" — and when the disciple arrived, all the cloths... *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)*

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** Verse 52 shows that Jesus looked for helpers in the way of Allah only when he sensed hostility from the disbelievers, and not at the initial stage of his call. **[Maʿārif]** He had risen to the task all alone, obeying the command of his Lord without worrying about setting up a "party" in advance; when the need came, he found a group around him — and every worthy mission demands such determination and courage.

**Belief.** **[Study Quran]** *Submitters* renders *muslimūn*, and this is one of several places in the Qurʾān in which *islām* and *muslim* convey a universal meaning beyond indicating followers of Muḥammad specifically. **[Saʿdī]** The disciples' faith was a favour from God to them and to Jesus, in that He inspired them to believe and to help His messenger. **[Jalālayn]** They were the first to believe in him.

**Cross-references.** **[Qurṭubī]** 4:2 for *ilā* in the sense of *maʿa*; 11:80 for Lot's "strong support". **[Ṭabarī]** 19:98 and 3:152 for the root *ḥ-s-s*; and his earlier treatment of *al-anṣār*. **[Study Quran]** 5:111 for *muslimūn* used of the disciples; 2:128, 2:132, 3:19c for the universal sense of *islām*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 61:14, "a party of the Children of Israel believed and a party disbelieved".

**Reflection.** The verse records the moment a prophet asks for help, and the tradition is at pains to say what kind of moment it was: not at the outset, when the call was new, but after hostility had become plain **[Maʿārif]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The question is "who will be my helpers *unto* God", and the grammarians argue over the preposition — with God, or towards God — the second reading making the flight itself part of the piety **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Study Quran]**. The answer comes in the plural, from twelve men, and what they say is *we are the helpers of God* before it is *we believe*, as though help were the first form the faith took **[Jalālayn]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The word *muslimūn* on their lips is the sūrah's quiet insistence that this religion is not named after a later community but after the posture itself **[Study Quran]**. And Ibn Kathīr cannot tell it without putting Muḥammad beside it: the same question asked at the pilgrimage season, the same answer given by the Anṣār **[Ibn Kathīr]**.

## 3:53 — رَبَّنَآ ءَامَنَّا بِمَآ أَنزَلْتَ وَٱتَّبَعْنَا ٱلرَّسُولَ فَٱكْتُبْنَا مَعَ ٱلشَّٰهِدِينَ

*"Our Lord, we believe in what You have sent down, and we follow the Messenger, so write us down among the witnesses."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** This is a report from God about the disciples, that they said: "Our Lord, we believe" — that is, we affirm the truth of — "in what You have sent down", meaning what You sent down to Your prophet Jesus of Your book; "and we follow the Messenger", meaning: we have become followers of Jesus upon Your religion with which You sent him, and his helpers for the truth with which You sent him to Your servants; and "so write us down among the witnesses" means: so establish our names with the names of those who bore witness to the truth, and affirmed Your oneness, and affirmed Your messengers as true, and followed Your command and Your prohibition — and place us in their number and with them in the honour You honour them with, and lodge us in their place, and do not make us of those who disbelieved in You, turned away from Your path and opposed Your command and prohibition. **[Ṭabarī]** By this God makes His creatures know the way of those whose sayings and deeds He is pleased with, so that they may follow their road and tread their path and arrive at the like of what they arrived at of the degrees of His honour; and by it He gives the lie to those who have adopted religions other than the ḥanīfiyyah of submission, in their claim about the prophets of God that they were upon something else. **[Ṭabarī]** And by it He argues against the delegation of the people of Najrān who disputed with the Messenger of God ﷺ: that the saying of those followers of Jesus with whom God was pleased was the opposite of their saying, and their road was not their road. **[Jalālayn]** "Lord we believe in what You have revealed", of the Gospel, "and we follow the Messenger", Jesus; "inscribe us therefore with those who bear witness" to Your Oneness and to the truthfulness of Your Messenger. **[Qurṭubī]** "Our Lord, we believe in what You have sent down" — that is, they say: our Lord, we believe; "in what You have sent down" means in Your book and in what You have made manifest of Your judgement; "and we follow the Messenger" — meaning Jesus. **[Qurṭubī]** "So write us down among the witnesses" means the community of Muḥammad ﷺ, according to Ibn ʿAbbās; and the meaning is: establish our names with their names and make us of their number; and it is said the meaning is: write us down with those who bore witness to Your prophets' truthfulness. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded that Ibn ʿAbbās said about "so write us down among those who bear witness": "Meaning among the Ummah of Muhammad" — a ḥadīth with a good chain of narration. **[Saʿdī]** "Our Lord, we believe in what You have sent down and we follow the Messenger" — and this is a total commitment to belief in everything God has sent down and to obedience to His messenger; "so write us down among the witnesses" — to You of Your oneness, to Your prophet of his message, and to Your religion of its truth and truthfulness. **[Study Quran]** The idea of witnesses reflects an important theme in the Qurʾān regarding testifying and bearing witness, carried out by human beings, angels and God — as in 3:18, where God bears witness that there is no god but He, as do the angels and the possessors of knowledge, upholding justice. **[Study Quran]** In 3:64 the Muslim community is commanded to say, "Bear witness that we are submitters", if others turn away from them; and *witness* (*shāhid*) can have the dual sense of someone who knows as well as someone who upholds and confirms.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** "We believe" is glossed as "we affirm the truth of"; "what You have sent down" is specified as the book sent down to Jesus; and "we follow the Messenger" is spelt out as becoming followers of Jesus upon God's religion and helpers for His truth. **[Qurṭubī]** "What You have sent down" is widened to include both the book and what God has made manifest of His judgement.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The disciples' prayer is a model God sets before His creatures so that they may follow their road and arrive at the like of what they arrived at — and simultaneously a refutation of those who claim the prophets were upon a religion other than submission. **[Saʿdī]** The prayer is a total commitment: belief in everything sent down and obedience to the messenger in everything. **[Study Quran]** *Witness* carries both the sense of knowing and the sense of upholding and confirming. **[Qurṭubī]** To be "written among the witnesses" is, on the report from Ibn ʿAbbās, to be numbered with the community of Muḥammad ﷺ.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 3:18 for God's bearing witness alongside the angels and the possessors of knowledge; 3:64 for the command to say "bear witness that we are submitters"; 2:143c for the community as witnesses. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:52 for the disciples' declaration that precedes this prayer; and the Najrān delegation disputation threaded through the sūrah. **[Saʿdī]** 3:52, where the disciples' faith is described as God's inspiration to them. **[Qurṭubī]** He cross-refers the reports on the identity of "the witnesses".

**Reflection.** The disciples' second sentence is a prayer rather than a declaration, which is the difference between believing something and asking to be counted **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Saʿdī]**. Two things are bound together in it — "what You sent down" and "the Messenger" — so that revelation and the one who brings it cannot be separated **[Jalālayn]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The strangest and most beautiful note is Ibn ʿAbbās's: that what they asked for was to be written among the community of Muḥammad, a prayer made centuries before that community existed **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Al-Ṭabarī makes the verse do double work — a model for believers and an argument against Najrān, since these are the people who actually followed Jesus and they did not say what the delegation says **[Ṭabarī]**. And the word they reach for is *witnesses*, a word that means both knowing and standing up for what you know **[Study Quran]**.

*Source note:* Maʿārif treats 3:52–53 as a single unit; its lexicography of *ḥawāriyyūn* and its note on when Jesus sought helpers are cited at 3:52, and it has no separate material on 3:53.

## 3:54 — وَمَكَرُوا۟ وَمَكَرَ ٱللَّهُ ۖ وَٱللَّهُ خَيْرُ ٱلْمَٰكِرِينَ

*"And they plotted, and God plotted. And God is the best of plotters."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: and the disbelievers among the Children of Israel plotted — those of whom God mentioned that Jesus sensed disbelief from them. **[Ṭabarī]** Their plotting, with which God described them, was their conspiring together to assassinate Jesus and kill him; that was because Jesus, after his people had expelled him and his mother from among them, returned to them. **[Jalālayn]** "God says: And they — the disbelievers among the Children of Israel — schemed against Jesus by assigning someone to assassinate him; and God schemed, by casting the likeness of Jesus onto the person who intended to kill him, and so they killed him while Jesus was raised up into heaven; and God is the best of schemers, most knowledgeable of him", Jesus. **[Saʿdī]** As for those from whom Jesus sensed disbelief — and they were the generality of the Children of Israel — "they plotted" against Jesus, "and God plotted" against them, "and God is the best of plotters": so they agreed to kill him and crucify him, and the likeness of Jesus was cast upon another, and they seized the one upon whom the likeness had been cast thinking he was Jesus — and God said to Jesus, "I am taking you and raising you to Me and purifying you of those who disbelieved" (3:55); so God raised him to Him and purified him of those who disbelieved, and they crucified the one they killed thinking he was Jesus, and they incurred a tremendous sin. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God states that the Children of Israel tried to kill ʿĪsā by conspiring to defame him and crucify him; they complained about him to the king, who was a disbeliever, claiming that ʿĪsā was a man who misguided people, discouraged them from obeying the king, caused division and separated a man from his own son, and they said other lies about him which they will carry on their necks, including accusing him of being an illegitimate son. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The king became furious and sent his men to capture ʿĪsā in order to torture and crucify him; when they surrounded his home and he thought they would surely capture him, God saved him from them, raising him up from the house to heaven. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God put the image of ʿĪsā on a man who was in the house, and when the unjust people went in while it was still dark, they thought he was ʿĪsā; they captured that man, humiliated and crucified him, and placed thorns on his head — but God deceived these people, saved and raised His prophet, and left them in disarray in the darkness of their transgression, thinking they had achieved their goal. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God made their hearts hard and defiant of the truth, disgracing them with a disgrace that will remain with them until the Day of Resurrection. **[Study Quran]** "They plotted" refers to Jesus' enemies; other verses mention God's "scheming" against the schemes of disbelieving people. **[Study Quran]** Some understand God's "scheming" to refer to His taking and raising Jesus unto Himself. **[Maʿārif]** In these verses the miraculous event of the Ascension of Jesus has been mentioned: "and they made a move" refers to the evil designs of the Jews who planned to arrest him and get him crucified, and "and Allah made a move" refers to the plan designed by God to save His prophet from their ill designs.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "And they plotted" — meaning the disbelievers among the Children of Israel from whom he sensed disbelief, that is, his killing; and that was because when his people and his mother had expelled him from among them, he returned to them with the disciples and cried out the call among them, and they resolved to kill him and conspired to assassinate him — and that is their plotting. **[Qurṭubī]** God's plotting (*makr*) is His granting respite to His servants from where they do not know, according to al-Farrāʾ and others. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: every time they committed a fresh sin, We renewed a favour to them. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj said: God's plotting is His requiting them for their plotting, so the requital is named by the name of the initiating act — as in 2:15, "God mocks them", and 4:142, "and He deceives them". **[Qurṭubī]** The root meaning of *makr* in the language is strategy and deception; *al-makr* is also the fullness of the shank, and a woman with full shanks is *mamkūrat al-sāqayn*; *al-makr* is a kind of cloth; and it is said that it is red ochre, as Ibn Fāris relates. **[Qurṭubī]** "And God is the best of plotters" — an active participle from *makara yamkuru makran*; some scholars have counted it among the names of God, so that one supplicates with it: "O best of plotters, plot for me"; and the Prophet ﷺ used to say in his supplication, "O God, plot for me and do not plot against me". *(digression: whether al-Mākir is one of the divine names)* **[Maʿārif]** The word *makr* in Arabic denotes a subtle and secret move or plan: if it is for a good purpose it is good, and if for a bad purpose it is bad — which is why the qualifier "evil" was attached to it in 35:43, "and evil plotting besets none but its own people". **[Maʿārif]** In the everyday idiom of Urdu, in which this commentary was originally written, the word *makr* is used exclusively for conspiracy and evil strategy, and it should therefore not be equated with the Arabic usage — and this is why God has been called *khayr al-mākirīn* here.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From al-Suddī: Jesus travelled with them — meaning the disciples, who used to fish, and who believed in him and followed him when he called them — until he came to the Children of Israel by night and cried out among them, and that is His saying, "a party of the Children of Israel believed and a party disbelieved" (61:14). **[Ṭabarī]** As for God's plotting against them, it was — according to what al-Suddī mentioned — His casting the likeness of Jesus upon one of his followers, so that those plotting against Jesus killed him while supposing him to be Jesus, God having raised Jesus before that. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Suddī said: then the Children of Israel besieged Jesus and nineteen men of the disciples in a house, and Jesus said to his companions, "Who will take my form and be killed, and Paradise is his?" A man among them took it, and Jesus was raised to heaven — and that is His saying, "and they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of plotters". When the disciples came out they saw them to be nineteen, and told them that Jesus had been raised to heaven; so they began counting the people and found them one man short of the number, while they saw the form of Jesus among them, and so they doubted about him; and on that basis they killed the man and crucified him, supposing him to be Jesus — and that is the saying of God, "they did not kill him and did not crucify him, but it was made to appear so to them" (4:157). *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* **[Qurṭubī]** It is said that "God plotted" means His casting the likeness of Jesus upon another and raising Jesus to Himself: when the Jews gathered to kill Jesus, he entered the house fleeing from them and Gabriel raised him through the opening to heaven; their king said to a certain wicked man among them called Yahuwdhā (Judas), "Go in to him and kill him" — and he entered by the wicket and did not find Jesus there, and God cast upon him the likeness of Jesus. When he came out they saw him in the likeness of Jesus, so they seized him, killed him and crucified him. Then they said: his face resembles the face of Jesus, but his body resembles the body of our companion — if this is our companion, where is Jesus? And if this is Jesus, where is our companion? And fighting broke out among them and they killed one another — and that is His saying, "and they plotted, and God plotted". *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* **[Ṭabarī]** It is also possible that the meaning of "God plotted" is His granting them respite so that the term might reach its appointed end, as he has explained under 2:15, "God mocks them".

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The alternative reading of God's "plotting" is respite — letting the plotters run so that the appointed term arrives. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj's reading is that the requital is named after the initiating act, exactly as with "God mocks them" and "He deceives them". **[Maʿārif]** *Makr* is morally neutral in Arabic — good when the purpose is good — which is why the phrase can be predicated of God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God saved and raised His prophet and left the plotters in disarray, thinking they had succeeded.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 8:30, 13:42 and 27:50 for the parallel "scheming" verses; 3:55 and 4:157–58 for the taking and raising. **[Qurṭubī]** 2:15 and 4:142 for the naming of the requital after the act; 3:152 for *al-ḥass*; and his treatment of the same in al-Baqarah. **[Ṭabarī]** 61:14 for the party that believed and the party that disbelieved; 4:157 for the likeness. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:55 for the raising.

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:52–54 as a single unit; his material on the disciples and on the *ḥawārī* ḥadīth is cited at 3:52, and his account of the plot, the raising and the substitution here.

**Reflection.** The sentence is deliberately offensive to pious ears, and the commentators spend most of their energy making it safe: *makr* is a secret plan, good or bad by its object, so the moral weight falls entirely on who is planning **[Maʿārif]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Al-Zajjāj offers the cleanest solution — the response is named after the provocation, as with "God mocks them" — and al-Farrāʾ offers another, that God's plotting is simply respite, letting evildoers run until the term arrives **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. The narrative underneath it is one the Qurʾān does not spell out, and the reports diverge wildly: a disciple who volunteers to take the form, or Judas sent in through the wicket and transformed, with the Jews then fighting each other over whether the corpse is their man **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]** — all flagged here as Isrāʾīliyyāt. What the verse itself insists on is only the asymmetry: they plotted, God plotted, and one of the two plotters is better at it **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Jalālayn]**.

## 3:55 — إِذْ قَالَ ٱللَّهُ يَٰعِيسَىٰٓ إِنِّى مُتَوَفِّيكَ وَرَافِعُكَ إِلَىَّ وَمُطَهِّرُكَ مِنَ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَجَاعِلُ ٱلَّذِينَ ٱتَّبَعُوكَ فَوْقَ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰ يَوْمِ ٱلْقِيَٰمَةِ ۖ ثُمَّ إِلَىَّ مَرْجِعُكُمْ فَأَحْكُمُ بَيْنَكُمْ فِيمَا كُنتُمْ فِيهِ تَخْتَلِفُونَ

*When God said: "O Jesus, I will take you and raise you to Me and purify you of those who disbelieved, and I will make those who follow you above those who disbelieve until the Day of Resurrection. Then to Me is your return, and I will judge between you in what you used to differ."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: and God plotted against the people who tried to kill Jesus — along with their disbelief in God and their rejection of Jesus in what he brought them from their Lord — when God said to Jesus, "I will take you": so the particle *idh* is connected to "and God plotted", meaning: and God plotted against them when God said to Jesus, "I will take you and raise you to Me", and He took him and raised Him to Himself. **[Jalālayn]** "And mention when God said 'O Jesus I am gathering you', seizing you, 'and raising you to Me', away from the world without death, 'and I am cleansing you of', removing you far away from, 'those who disbelieved, and I am setting those who follow you' — those Christians and Muslims who believed in your prophethood — 'above those who disbelieved in you', namely the Jews, becoming above them through definitive argument and the sword, 'until the Day of Resurrection. Then to Me shall be your return and I will decide between you as to what you were at variance about', as regards religion." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "I will take you and raise you to Myself" — while you are asleep; and he cites 6:60, "It is He Who takes your souls by night", and 39:42, "It is God Who takes away the souls at the time of their death, and those that die not during their sleep". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And purify you from those who disbelieve" — by raising you to heaven. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And I will make those who follow you superior to those who disbelieve, till the Day of Resurrection" — and this is what happened: when God raised ʿĪsā to heaven his followers divided into sects and groups; some of them believed in what God sent ʿĪsā as — a servant of God, His messenger, and the son of His female-servant — but some went to the extreme over him, believing he was the son of God, some saying he was God himself, others that he was one of a Trinity. **[Saʿdī]** "And when God said, 'O Jesus, I am taking you and raising you to Me and purifying you of those who disbelieved'" — so God raised him to Him and purified him of those who disbelieved. **[Saʿdī]** "And I will make those who follow you above those who disbelieve until the Day of Resurrection" — what is meant by those who followed him is the party that believed in him, and God helped them against those who deviated from his religion; then, when the community of Muḥammad ﷺ came, they were his followers in truth, and He aided them and gave them victory over all the disbelievers and made them prevail by the religion Muḥammad ﷺ brought them. **[Study Quran]** *Take* translates *mutawaffī*, which is usually understood to mean that God takes one unto Himself, that is, in death; in the context of this verse most assert, however, that it does not refer to being taken in death but rather to being taken from the world, although others believe this does refer to God causing Jesus to die, and others say it refers to sleep. **[Study Quran]** *Purify thee* can mean to free him from his enemies or to absolve him of their doings; and "place those who followed thee above those who disbelieved" is understood variously as the historical relationship of Christians to Jews, as the Muslims who believe in him in the proper way rather than the Christians, or as the superiority of proof and demonstration.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** The operator of *idh* is "they plotted", or an implied verb. **[Qurṭubī]** A group of the people of meanings, among them al-Ḍaḥḥāk and al-Farrāʾ, take "I will take you and raise you to Me" as involving advancement and delay, because the *wāw* does not require order: the meaning being "I am raising you to Me and purifying you of those who disbelieved, and I will cause you to die after you descend from heaven" — as in 20:129, "and were it not for a word that preceded from your Lord, it would have been an inevitable consequence, and a stated term", the underlying order being "and were it not for a word that preceded from your Lord and a stated term, it would have been inevitable"; and he cites the poet: "ah, palm tree of Dhāt ʿIrq — upon you be peace and the mercy of God", meaning "upon you be peace" and then "the mercy of God". **[Maʿārif]** The word *mutawaffīka* takes *tawaffī* as its verbal noun, from the root *wafy*; lexically the word means "to take in full", and its derivations *wafā*, *īfāʾ* and *istīfāʾ* convey that sense — a meaning universally confirmed by all the lexicons of the Arabic language. **[Maʿārif]** Since a man completes his appointed time at the hour of death, and the soul given by God is taken back fully and conclusively, the word is also used figuratively in the sense of death. **[Maʿārif]** Ibn Taymiyyah says in *al-Jawāb al-Ṣaḥīḥ*: "*al-tawaffī*, in the Arabic language, means to exact fully or take in full, and it takes three forms — the first: taking in sleep; the second: taking in death; and the third: taking the soul and the body all together." **[Maʿārif]** In *Kulliyyāt Abī al-Baqāʾ* it is said: "*al-tawaffī* is putting to death and the exacting of the soul in common usage, while in classical usage it is taking in full and the exacting of the due right." **[Maʿārif]** The majority of scholars therefore translate *mutawaffīka* in this verse in the sense of "taking in full" — meaning that God will not leave Jesus in the hands of the Jews, but will take him away, in the form that he will be raised unto Him in the heavens.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said on "I will take you": it means the death of sleep — God raised him in his sleep; and al-Ḥasan reported that the Messenger of God ﷺ said to the Jews, "Jesus did not die, and he will return to you before the Day of Resurrection" *(weak: al-Ṭabarī's chain here is mursal)*. **[Ṭabarī]** Others said: the meaning is "I am seizing you from the earth and raising you to Me", the sense of "taking" being "seizing", as one says "I took in full (*tawaffaytu*) what was owed me from so-and-so" meaning "I seized it and took it in full"; so the meaning of "I will take you and raise you" is: I am seizing you from the earth alive to My neighbourhood, and taking you to what is with Me without death, and raising you from among the idolaters and the people who disbelieve in you. **[Ṭabarī]** Muṭarrif al-Warrāq said: "I will take you from the world, and it is not the death of death"; al-Ḥasan said: "I will take you from the earth"; and Ibn Jurayj said: His raising him to Him is His taking him and His purifying him of those who disbelieved. **[Ṭabarī]** Kaʿb al-Aḥbār said: God would not cause Jesus son of Mary to die; He only sent him as a caller and a bearer of good news, calling to Him alone — and when Jesus saw how few followed him and how many denied him, he complained of that to God, and God revealed to him, "I will take you and raise you to Me", and "he whom I have raised to Me is not dead, and I will send you against the one-eyed Dajjāl and you will kill him". *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḥasan and Ibn Jurayj said: *mutawaffīka* means "I am seizing you and raising you to heaven without death", like "I took in full my wealth from so-and-so" meaning "I took it". **[Qurṭubī]** Wahb b. Munabbih said: God took Jesus for three hours of the day, then raised him to heaven — and this is far-fetched, since it is established in the reports from the Prophet ﷺ that he will descend and kill the Dajjāl. *(weak: al-Qurṭubī rejects it)* **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Zayd said: "I will take you" and "raising you" are one, and he has not died yet; and Ibn Ṭalḥah related from Ibn ʿAbbās the meaning "I will cause you to die". **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said: it is the death of sleep, citing 6:60, "He it is Who takes your souls by night" — that is, He makes you sleep, for sleep is the brother of death, as he ﷺ said when asked "is there sleep in Paradise?" — "No; sleep is the brother of death, and in Paradise there is no death" — reported by al-Dāraquṭnī. **[Qurṭubī]** The sound view is that God raised him to heaven without death and without sleep, as al-Ḥasan and Ibn Zayd said, and it is the choice of al-Ṭabarī and what is sound from Ibn ʿAbbās, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk said it. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Ḍaḥḥāk said: when they wanted to kill Jesus, the disciples gathered in an upper room, twelve men, and the Messiah entered to them through the niche of the room; Iblīs informed the assembly of the Jews, and four thousand men of them rode out and seized the door of the room; and the Messiah said to the disciples, "Which of you will go out and be killed and be with me in Paradise?" *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Abī Ḥātim recorded that al-Ḥasan said that "I will take you" refers to sleep, for God raised ʿĪsā while he was asleep. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The Messenger of God ﷺ used to say when he awoke: "All thanks are due to God Who brought us back to life after He had caused us to die (sleep), and the Return is to Him". **[Study Quran]** In a ḥadīth, the Prophet is reported to have said, "No man has greater claim to Jesus than I do, because there was no prophet between us, and he will be my vicegerent over my community. He will descend, and when you see him you will know him." **[Maʿārif]** Some authentic commentators have interpreted *mutawaffīka* in the sense of giving death, but they do not mean that Jesus' death will occur at the hands of his enemies: the true meaning, according to them, is that God said two things to comfort Jesus when the Jews were bent on killing him — one, that his death will come not at their hands in the form of killing but as a natural death; and two, that in order to rescue him from their evil designs God will, at that time, raise Jesus towards Him. **[Maʿārif]** This explanation is exactly what has been reported from Ibn ʿAbbās, as quoted by al-Suyūṭī in his *al-Durr al-Manthūr* on the authority of several narrators.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** When God raised Jesus, his followers split into those who held him to be a servant and messenger of God, and those who exceeded — some saying he was the son of God, some that he was God, some that he was one of a Trinity; God mentioned these false creeds in the Qurʾān and refuted them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The Muslims are the true followers of Jesus, for they believed in him correctly; God abrogated all the laws sent down to the prophets with the law He sent Muḥammad ﷺ with, and so his religion shall always be dominant and victorious over all others. **[Saʿdī]** Those who follow Jesus truly are the party that believed in him, and after them the community of Muḥammad ﷺ; yet God's just wisdom requires that whoever holds to the religion is given clear victory, and whoever abandons His command, throws His law behind him and dares to disobey Him is punished and has enemies set over him. **[Study Quran]** Many commentators mention the belief that Jesus will return before the end of time, fight the Dajjāl and rule according to the law of Muḥammad until the world comes to an end. **[Qurṭubī]** The sound position is that God raised him without death and without sleep — the choice of al-Ṭabarī and what is established from Ibn ʿAbbās.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 4:156–159; 6:60 and 39:42 for *tawaffī* as sleep; 24:55 for the promise of succession; 19:34–36, "Such is ʿĪsā, son of Maryam: a statement of truth". **[Qurṭubī]** 20:129 for the advancement-and-delay argument; 6:60; and his *al-Tadhkirah* for the descent and the killing of the Dajjāl. **[Study Quran]** 4:158 and 4:157c for the crucifixion and the end of Jesus' earthly life; 2:113c for God's judgement in matters of religious disagreement. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:54, to which *idh* is attached; 61:14. **[Saʿdī]** 24:55 for the promise to the believers.

**Reflection.** Everything in this verse turns on one verb, *mutawaffīka*, and the commentaries are unusually candid about how many ways it can go: death, sleep, or simply taking **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Maʿārif goes to the lexicons and to Ibn Taymiyyah to show that the root means "taking in full", and that death is a derived and figurative use — which lets the majority read the verse as rescue rather than as dying **[Maʿārif]**. Ibn Kathīr opts for sleep, and cites the ordinary Qurʾānic usage of *tawaffā* for the nightly taking of souls, which is a way of keeping every occurrence of the word in the family **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The reading al-Qurṭubī calls sound — raised without death and without sleep, al-Ṭabarī's own choice — is also the one that makes sense of the promise attached to it, since the raising is what defeats the plot of 3:54 **[Qurṭubī]**. The second half of the verse has proved harder to live with: "those who follow you above those who disbelieve" has been claimed for Christians over Jews, for Muslims over both, and for argument over force, and the sūrah leaves the question open for history to answer **[Study Quran]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**.

## 3:56 — فَأَمَّا ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ فَأُعَذِّبُهُمْ عَذَابًۭا شَدِيدًۭا فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ وَمَا لَهُم مِّن نَّٰصِرِينَ

*"As for those who disbelieve, I will punish them with a severe punishment in this world and in the Hereafter, and they will have no helpers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: as for those who denied your prophethood, O Jesus, opposed your religion, and rejected the truth you brought them, and spoke falsehood about you, and attributed you to other than what it is proper to attribute you to — of the Jews and the Christians and all the other categories of religions — "I will punish them with a severe punishment": as for in this world, by killing, captivity, humiliation and misery; and as for in the Hereafter, by the fire of hell, abiding therein forever. **[Ṭabarī]** "And they will have no helpers" means: they have none to prevent them from the punishment of God, and none to push away His painful chastisement from them, by strength or by intercession, for He is the Mighty, Possessor of vengeance. **[Jalālayn]** "As for the disbelievers I will chastise them with a terrible chastisement in this world", through being killed, taken captive and made to pay the *jizya*, "and the Hereafter", in the Fire; "they shall have no helpers", none to protect them from it. **[Qurṭubī]** "As for those who disbelieve, I will punish them with a severe punishment in this world and the Hereafter" — meaning by killing, crucifying, captivity and the *jizya*; and in the Hereafter, by the Fire. **[Ibn Kathīr]** This is what God did to the Jews who disbelieved in ʿĪsā and to the Christians who went to the extreme over him: God tormented them in this life — they were killed, captured, and lost their wealth and their kingdoms — and their torment in the Hereafter is worse and more severe, as in 13:34, "and they have no defender against God". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And they will have no helpers." **[Saʿdī]** This recompense is general for everyone characterised by these attributes, among all the people of the previous religions. **[Saʿdī]** Then, when the master of the messengers and seal of the prophets was sent, and his message abrogated all messages and his religion abrogated all religions, whoever holds fast to other than this religion is among the ruined. **[Study Quran]** Punishment and reward are meted out in both this life and the Hereafter, as in 2:114, 3:22 and 16:30.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** The punishment named for this world is spelt out as killing, captivity, humiliation and misery; that for the Hereafter as the fire, abiding forever. **[Qurṭubī]** He adds crucifixion and the *jizya* to the forms the punishment takes in this world. **[Jalālayn]** The *jizya* is listed among the worldly forms of the chastisement.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** Though the verse comes out in the form of a report, it is a threat (*waʿīd*) from God to those who disbelieve in Him and His messengers and a promise to those who believe in Him and His messengers — for He informed both parties that He will neither defraud the believer of his due nor wrong his honour by placing it in one who disbelieved and opposed His command and prohibition, which would make Him a wrongdoer by placing it with other than its people. **[Ṭabarī]** "He says: I do not love the wrongdoers, so how should I wrong My creation?" **[Maʿārif]** The words "I shall punish them in this world and in the Hereafter" raise a difficulty: since this statement relates to the judgement to be pronounced on the Last Day, how can punishment "in this world" be spoken of, when this world will not exist then — and even though it exists now, the judgement is to be given on the Day of Resurrection? **[Maʿārif]** The difficulty is resolved by an analogy: the saying is like a judge saying to a culprit, "Right now I am sending you to jail for a year; if you misbehave there, I shall make it two years" — which simply means that the two years will be counted from the day the punishment is awarded. **[Maʿārif]** So the punishment in the world has already been given; now the punishment of the Hereafter will be added on, and the sum total will finally be executed on the Last Day — which means that having been punished in the world will not serve as an expiation for the punishment of the Hereafter. **[Maʿārif]** This is contrary to the condition of the believers who, when struck by suffering in this mortal world, have their sins forgiven and find the punishment due in the Hereafter lightened or warded off; and it is for this reason that the hint "God does not love wrongdoers" (3:57) has been made: believers are dear because of their belief, and the beloved are always treated in such a manner, while the disbelievers are detested because of their disbelief, and those detested do not receive such treatment. *(Bayān al-Qurʾān)* **[Saʿdī]** After the sending of Muḥammad ﷺ, holding to any religion other than his is among the things that ruin a person.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 2:114, 3:22 and 16:30 for punishment in both lives; 16:122 and 39:10 as parallels. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 13:34, "and they have no defender against God". **[Ṭabarī]** 3:55, where the judgement between the parties is first announced. **[Qurṭubī]** 9:29 for the *jizya*.

**Reflection.** The verse is small and relentless: the same people are punished twice, and the second punishment is not reduced by the first **[Ṭabarī]**. The commentators list what the worldly punishment actually looked like — killing, captivity, the *jizya* — which is to say that this is not a threat held in reserve but a thing that has already happened **[Jalālayn]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Maʿārif raises the awkward question of how a judge on the Last Day can still speak of "this world", and answers it with the image of a sentence extended for misbehaviour inside the sentence — the point being that suffering here does not pay off suffering there for the disbeliever, whereas for the believer it does **[Maʿārif]**. That asymmetry is exactly what the next verse's closing clause is for: God does not love the wrongdoers, and the beloved are treated differently **[Maʿārif]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. And al-Ṭabarī's last move is the one that matters most — a verse that reads like a report is in fact a threat to one party and a promise to the other **[Ṭabarī]**.

*Source note:* Saʿdī and Maʿārif each treat 3:56–57 (Maʿārif 3:56–58) as a single unit; their material on the two verses is divided between them by content, and Maʿārif has nothing separate for 3:58.

## 3:57 — وَأَمَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ فَيُوَفِّيهِمْ أُجُورَهُمْ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ ٱلظَّٰلِمِينَ

*"And as for those who believe and do righteous deeds, He will pay them their rewards in full. And God does not love the wrongdoers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And as for those who believed and did righteous deeds" — those who believed in you, O Jesus, meaning: affirmed you as true, and affirmed your prophethood and the truth you brought them from Me, and professed the submission (*islām*) with which I sent you, and acted upon what I made obligatory for you of My obligations, and what I legislated of My laws, and what I instituted of My practices. **[Ṭabarī]** "He will pay them their rewards in full" — He will give them the recompense of their righteous deeds complete, not defrauded of anything of it and not diminished. **[Ṭabarī]** "And God does not love the wrongdoers" — God does not love one who wrongs another of a right belonging to him, or who places a thing in other than its place. **[Ṭabarī]** By this God removes from Himself that He wrongs His servants — repaying the evildoer who disbelieved with the reward of the good-doer who believed, or repaying the good-doer who believed, followed His command, kept from His prohibition and obeyed Him, with the reward of the evildoer who disbelieved in Him, denied His messengers and opposed His command and prohibition. He says: I do not love the wrongdoers, so how should I wrong My creation? **[Jalālayn]** "But as for the believers who do righteous deeds He will pay them in full their wages. God loves not the evildoers" — that is, He will chastise them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And as for those who believe and do righteous deeds, God will pay them their reward in full" — in this life, with victory and domination, and in the Hereafter, with Paradise and high grades; "and God does not like the wrongdoers". **[Maʿārif]** A hint to the effect that believers are treated differently has been made in "God does not love wrongdoers": believers are dear because of their belief, and the beloved are always treated in such a manner; the disbelievers are detested because of their disbelief, and those detested do not receive such treatment. *(Bayān al-Qurʾān)* **[Saʿdī]** This recompense, too, is general for everyone characterised by these attributes among all the people of the previous religions, until the sending of the seal of the prophets abrogated all messages and all religions.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** "Those who believed" is glossed with an entire creed: affirming Jesus, affirming his prophethood, professing the submission he was sent with, and acting on the obligations, laws and practices God instituted through him. **[Ṭabarī]** "He will pay them in full" excludes any defrauding or diminishing. **[Jalālayn]** *Yuwaffīhim* is also read *nuwaffīhim*, "We will pay them in full".

**Readings.** **[Jalālayn]** *Yuwaffīhim* — "He will pay them in full" — is also read *nuwaffīhim*, "We will pay them in full".

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn ʿAbbās: "and did righteous deeds" means "they performed My obligations". **[Jalālayn]** It is reported that God sent Jesus a cloud which raised him up, but his mother clutched at him in tears; he then said to her, "Verily the Resurrection shall bring us together again". **[Jalālayn]** This took place on the Night of Ordainment (*laylat al-qadr*) in the Holy House of Jerusalem, when he was thirty-three years old; his mother lived on after him for six years. *(Isrāʾīliyyāt)* **[Jalālayn]** The Two Shaykhs, al-Bukhārī and Muslim, narrate a ḥadīth stating that Jesus will descend when the Hour is nigh and will rule according to the law of our Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ, and that he will slay the false messiah and the swine, break the cross and impose the *jizya*. **[Jalālayn]** In a ḥadīth recorded by Muslim he will remain for seven years; according to Abū Dāwūd al-Ṭayālisī he will remain for forty years, and he will die and have prayers performed over him; and it is possible that what is meant by the forty years is the total time he will have spent on earth before he was raised and afterwards.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The sentence, though in the form of a report, is at once a threat to the deniers and a promise to the believers: God will not defraud the believer of his due, nor wrong his honour by placing it in one who disbelieved. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The reward is paid twice over — victory and domination in this life, Paradise and high ranks in the next. **[Maʿārif]** The closing clause carries the reason the two parties are treated differently: the believers are beloved and the disbelievers detested.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:56 for the corresponding punishment; 13:34. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:55, where the judgement is announced; his treatment of the duties in his commentary on the obligations. **[Saʿdī]** 3:56, with which this verse is paired. **[Jalālayn]** Sūrah Maryam for the raising.

**Reflection.** The verse is the mirror of the one before it, and the pairing is exact: the same God, the same judgement, two outcomes **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. What earns the reward is named twice — belief and deed — and al-Ṭabarī unpacks both until "belief" includes professing the very religion Jesus was sent with **[Ṭabarī]**. Then comes the strange closing sentence, which on its face has nothing to do with either party: God does not love the wrongdoers **[Jalālayn]**. Al-Ṭabarī explains the placement by saying that God is clearing Himself of the only wrong that would be possible here — treating the disbeliever as the believer, or the believer as the disbeliever — and that is why the clause belongs in a verse about wages **[Ṭabarī]**. Maʿārif reads it as the reason the two are handled differently in the first place: suffering wipes out sins for the believer and not for the disbeliever, because the beloved are treated as beloved and the detested are not **[Maʿārif]**.

*Source note:* The Study Quran has no separate block for 3:57 (its note on 3:56–57 was cited at 3:56), and Qurṭubī's block for 3:56–58 comments only on 3:56 and 3:58, so there is nothing distinct from him to cite here.

## 3:58 — ذَٰلِكَ نَتْلُوهُ عَلَيْكَ مِنَ ٱلْءَايَٰتِ وَٱلذِّكْرِ ٱلْحَكِيمِ

*"That is what We recite to you of the verses and the Wise Reminder."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "That" refers to these tidings with which God informed His prophet about Jesus and his mother Mary, and her mother Ḥannah, and Zachariah and his son John, and what He narrated of the affair of the disciples and of the Jews among the Children of Israel. **[Ṭabarī]** "We recite it to you", O Muḥammad — meaning: We read it to you, O Muḥammad, upon the tongue of Gabriel, by Our revealing it to you. **[Ṭabarī]** "Of the verses" — meaning: of the lessons and proofs against those who dispute with you, of the delegation of the Christians of Najrān, and of the Jews of the Children of Israel who denied you and denied the truth you brought them from Me. **[Ṭabarī]** "And the Reminder" — meaning: and the Qurʾān; "the Wise" — meaning: possessed of the wisdom that separates between truth and falsehood, and between you and those who attribute the Messiah to other than his lineage. **[Jalālayn]** "This what is mentioned of the matter of Jesus We recite to you, narrate to you, O Muhammad, of verses and wise, clear, remembrance, namely the Qurʾān"; "of verses" is a circumstantial qualifier referring to the suffixed pronoun of *natlūhu*, and its operator is the demonstrative force of *dhālika*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "This is what We recite to you of the verses and the Wise Reminder" — meaning: what We narrated to you, O Muḥammad, regarding ʿĪsā, his birth and his life, is what God conveyed and revealed to you, sent down from the Preserved Tablet; so there is no doubt in it. **[Saʿdī]** That is, this great Qurʾān, in which is the news of the first and the last, and of the prophets and the messengers — it is the clear verses of God, and it is that which reminds the servants of everything they need, and it is the wise, the firmly ordered, true in its reports, excellent in its rulings. **[Study Quran]** The Wise Reminder is another name for the Qurʾān, though some understand this to refer to the Preserved Tablet, from which all revelation comes down to earth. **[Study Quran]** This verse ends the passage in which God is addressing Jesus and begins to address the Prophet Muḥammad directly regarding belief in Jesus.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "That" is in the nominative as the subject, its predicate being "We recite it"; and it is also admissible to read "the matter is that", with an implied subject. **[Jalālayn]** "Of verses" functions as a circumstantial qualifier whose operator is the demonstrative force of *dhālika*. **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-ḥakīm*, "the Wise", is the one possessed of the wisdom that decides between truth and falsehood — and here, specifically, between the Prophet and those who ascribe the Messiah to a lineage not his.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "that We recite to you of the verses and the Wise Reminder" — the decisive, separating truth in which falsehood has not been mixed, of the report about Jesus and of what they differed over concerning him; so accept no report other than it. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Ḍaḥḥāk said: "the Reminder" is the Qurʾān. **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: "the Reminder" is the Qurʾān, and "the Wise" is that which is complete in its wisdom.
