**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** What is recited is not one story among many but the decisive account, unmixed with falsehood, and no other report about Jesus is to be accepted alongside it. **[Saʿdī]** The Qurʾān is the reminder that contains everything the servants need, true in what it reports and excellent in what it rules. **[Study Quran]** The address now turns from Jesus to Muḥammad: from here the sūrah speaks to the Prophet directly about belief in Jesus.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 85:22 for the Preserved Tablet. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 19:34–36, "Such is ʿĪsā, son of Maryam: a statement of truth, about which they doubt. It befits not God that He should beget a son. Glorified be He. When He decrees a thing, He only says to it: 'Be!' and it is." **[Ṭabarī]** His earlier treatment of *al-dhikr* and of *al-ḥakīm*. **[Jalālayn]** 3:57 for the report that precedes.

*Source note:* Ibn Kathīr treats 3:55–58 as a single unit; his material on *mutawaffīka*, on the sects after the raising and on the descent is cited at 3:55, and his comment on the recitation here.

**Reflection.** This is the hinge of the whole passage: the discourse that has been addressed to Jesus turns and speaks to Muḥammad, and the sūrah names what it has just done — recited **[Study Quran]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. The two nouns it uses for itself are doing different work: "verses" because what has just been told is aimed *at* someone — the Najrān delegation and the Jews who denied — and "the Wise Reminder" because it decides **[Ṭabarī]**. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss of "the Wise" is the sharpest: wisdom here is what separates truth from falsehood, and specifically what separates the Prophet from those who ascribe the Messiah to the wrong lineage **[Ṭabarī]**. The report he chooses to end on says the same thing in a harder voice — this is the decisive account, and accept no other **[Ṭabarī]**.

*Source note:* Maʿārif treats 3:56–58 as a single unit; its discussion of the difficulty in "I shall punish them in this world and the Hereafter" is cited at 3:56, and it has nothing separate for 3:58.

## 3:59 — إِنَّ مَثَلَ عِيسَىٰ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ كَمَثَلِ ءَادَمَ ۖ خَلَقَهُۥ مِن تُرَابٍۢ ثُمَّ قَالَ لَهُۥ كُن فَيَكُونُ

*The likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam: He created him from dust, then said to him, "Be", and he was.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: the likeness of Jesus, in My creating him without a male — so inform of it, O Muḥammad, the delegation of the Christians of Najrān — is, with Me, as the likeness of Adam, whom I created from dust, then I said to him "Be", and he was, without male and without female. **[Ṭabarī]** He means: My creating Jesus from his mother without a male is not more wondrous than My creating Adam without male or female, and than My command when I commanded him to be — and he was flesh. He says: so likewise My creating Jesus — I commanded him to be, and he was. **[Jalālayn]** "Truly the likeness of Jesus" — his remarkable case — "in God's sight is as Adam's likeness" — as the case of Adam, whom God created without father or mother; this is a comparison of one remarkable thing with another more remarkable, so that it convinces the disputer and establishes itself in the mind more effectively. **[Jalālayn]** "He created him" — Adam, that is, his form — "of dust, then said He to him 'Be' a human being, and he was; similarly He said to Jesus 'Be' — without a father — and he was." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "The likeness of ʿĪsā before God", regarding God's ability, since He created him without a father, "is the likeness of Adam", for God created Adam without a father or a mother; rather, "He created him from dust, then said to him 'Be', and he was". **[Ibn Kathīr]** Therefore, He Who created Adam without a father or a mother is able to create ʿĪsā, as well, without a father; and if the claim is made that ʿĪsā is God's son because he was created without a father, then the same claim befits Adam even more — however, since such a claim regarding Adam is obviously false, then making the same claim about ʿĪsā is even more false. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Furthermore, by mentioning these facts God emphasises His ability: creating Adam without male or female, Eve from a male without a female, and ʿĪsā from a mother without a father, compared with His creating the rest of creation from male and female — and this is why He said in Sūrah Maryam, "and that We may make him a sign for mankind" (19:21). **[Saʿdī]** When God mentioned the story of Mary and Jesus and their true report, and that he is a servant whom God favoured, and that whoever claims there is something of divinity in him has lied against God and denied all His prophets and denied Jesus — for the doubt that occurred to those who took him as a god is a false doubt: if it had any sound basis, Adam would have more right to it, since he was created without mother or father, and yet all mankind agree that he is one of the servants of God. **[Saʿdī]** So the claim of Jesus' divinity, on the basis that he was created from a mother without a father, is the most false of claims; and this is the truth in which there is no doubt — that Jesus, as he said of himself, "I said nothing to them except what You commanded me: worship God, my Lord and your Lord" (5:117). **[Study Quran]** It is reported that these verses were revealed while the Christian delegation from Najrān was in Madinah; these Christians reportedly argued that since Jesus had no human father, he was truly God's son. **[Study Quran]** This verse constitutes one of the central arguments in the Qurʾān against the divinity of Christ: it acknowledges the miraculous nature of his birth but rejects the implication that this makes him divine — if God could create Adam, who had neither earthly father nor mother, from dust, He could also create Jesus from the "blood" of Mary. **[Maʿārif]** The background of these verses is the Prophet's ultimatum to the Christians of Najrān, which offered three options in order: accept Islam; pay the *jizya*; or prepare for war. **[Maʿārif]** After mutual consultation the Christians sent a delegation consisting of Shuraḥbīl ʿAbdullāh b. Shuraḥbīl and Jabbār ibn Fayd; they came and began to talk on religious matters, and when, in their effort to prove the godhood of Jesus, they took the debate to the extremes of dispute, this verse of *mubāhala* was revealed. **[Maʿārif]** Thereupon the Prophet ﷺ invited the Christians to the *mubāhala*, and quite readily he himself came out with Fāṭimah, ʿAlī and their sons Ḥasan and Ḥusayn, all ready for it. **[Maʿārif]** Looking at this, Shuraḥbīl said to his two comrades: "You already know that he is a prophet of Allah, and getting into a *mubāhala* with a prophet means certain death and destruction for us; so find some other way out." They asked him what he thought it could be, and he said: "I think the best solution is to opt for a truce, as the prophet has advised." So everybody agreed, and as a result the Prophet ﷺ made peace by fixing a levy on them, which they accepted.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** This verse is a proof of the validity of *qiyās*, analogical reasoning. **[Qurṭubī]** The comparison bears on the fact that Jesus was created without a father, like Adam — not on his being created from dust; a thing may be compared with a thing even when there is a great difference between them, provided they share a single description. **[Qurṭubī]** Adam was created from dust and Jesus was not, so in this respect there is a difference between them; but the likeness between them is that each was created without a father, and also that the origin of each of their creations was from dust — for Adam was not created from dust itself, but God made the dust clay, then made it sounding clay, then created him from it; and likewise Jesus was transformed from state to state and then made a human being without a father. **[Ṭabarī]** "The likeness" is the likeness in God's creating, and the whole point is the comparison of two wonders, the second greater than the first. **[Jalālayn]** The comparison is of one remarkable thing with another more remarkable, so that it convinces the disputer and fixes itself in the mind more effectively. **[Study Quran]** The word *likeness* means that certain attributes of Adam and Jesus are alike.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Shaʿbī said: the people of Najrān were the most extravagant of the Christians in their claims about Jesus and used to argue with the Prophet ﷺ, so God sent down this verse in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān, "the likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam...", up to "then we invoke the curse of God upon the liars". **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: a group of the people of Najrān came to Muḥammad ﷺ, among them al-Sayyid and al-ʿĀqib, and said to him, "What is your business mentioning our companion?" He said: "Who is he?" They said: "Jesus — you claim he is the servant of God!" He said: "Yes, indeed; he is the servant of God." They said: "Have you seen the like of Jesus, or been told of it?" Then they left him, and Gabriel came to him with the command of our Lord, the Hearing, the Knowing, and said: "Say to them, when they come to you: 'the likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam'..." **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said: it has been mentioned to us that the two chiefs of the people of Najrān and their two bishops, al-Sayyid and al-ʿĀqib, met the Prophet of God ﷺ and asked him about Jesus, saying: "Every human being has a father, so what is the matter with Jesus that he has no father?" — and God sent down concerning him this verse. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Suddī said: when the Messenger of God ﷺ was sent and the people of Najrān heard of him, some of them came to him. *(digression: the several accounts of the Najrān exchange)* **[Qurṭubī]** This verse was revealed on account of the delegation of Najrān when they objected to the Prophet's saying that "Jesus is the servant of God and His word" and said: "Show us a servant created without a father" — so the Prophet said to them: "Adam — who was his father? Do you marvel that Jesus has no father? Adam had neither father nor mother." **[Qurṭubī]** And that is His saying, "and they do not bring you a similitude" — about Jesus — "but We bring you the truth" — about Adam — "and a better explanation" (25:33). **[Qurṭubī]** It is related that when he called them to Islam they said: "We were Muslims before you." He said: "You lie; three things prevent you from Islam — your saying God has taken a son, your eating swine, and your prostrating to the cross." They said: "Who is the father of Jesus?" — so God sent down "the likeness of Jesus with God is as the likeness of Adam..." up to "then we invoke the curse of God upon the liars" (3:61). **[Qurṭubī]** The Prophet called them, and some of them said to others: "If you do it, the valley will blaze with fire against you." They said: "Will you not offer us something other than this?" He said: "Islam, or the *jizya*, or war" — and they agreed to the *jizya*. **[Saʿdī]** The delegation of the Christians of Najrān came to the Prophet ﷺ and persisted in their falsehood after he had established the proofs against them that Jesus is the servant of God and His messenger, while they claimed his divinity; the matter reached the point that God commanded him to engage in *mubāhala* with them, since the truth had become clear to them but obstinacy and partisanship prevented them from it. **[Saʿdī]** He called them to bring their families and their sons while he brought his, then to pray to God to send down His punishment and curse upon the liars; they consulted whether to accept, and agreed not to, because they knew he was in truth the prophet of God and that if they did it they, their children and their families would perish — so they made peace with him and offered the *jizya*, and asked for a truce, and he granted it and did not press them, because the purpose, the clarity of the truth, had been achieved, and their obstinacy in refusing the *mubāhala* proved that they were wrongdoers.

**Belief.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The argument is *a fortiori*: if fatherlessness makes Jesus the son of God, it makes Adam so more fittingly — and nobody claims that about Adam, so the claim about Jesus is even more false. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God's power is displayed in the four modes of origination: Adam without male or female, Eve from a male without a female, Jesus from a mother without a father, and everything else from both. **[Saʿdī]** The claim of divinity on the ground of virginal conception is the most false of claims, since Adam would have a stronger title to it and is universally agreed to be a servant. **[Study Quran]** The verse acknowledges the miracle and denies the inference. **[Qurṭubī]** The verse is at once a proof of the validity of analogical reasoning in law and theology, and a proof that the comparison is by a shared attribute rather than by an identical process.

**Cross-references.** **[Qurṭubī]** 25:33, "and they do not bring you a similitude but We bring you the truth and a better explanation"; 3:61 for the *mubāhala* verse; 9:29 for the *jizya*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 19:21, "and that We may make him a sign for mankind". **[Study Quran]** 2:117c for the Divine fiat "Be!"; 6:73, 16:40, 36:82, 40:68 for the same formula. **[Saʿdī]** 5:117, "I said nothing to them except what You commanded me: worship God, my Lord and your Lord"; 3:62 for "this is the true narrative". **[Ṭabarī]** 3:61–63 for the continuation of the Najrān passage.

**Reflection.** This is the verse the whole sūrah has been walking toward since 3:33, and it is an argument rather than a declaration **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Study Quran]**. Its form is *a fortiori* and its target is an inference: fatherlessness does not make a son of God, because a man with neither parent is the stronger case and no one claims it for him **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**. The comparison is not between two identical processes — Adam from dust, Jesus from a woman — and al-Qurṭubī says precisely what is being compared: one shared attribute, which is also why he can cite the verse as a proof that analogical reasoning is valid at all **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. Setting the verse in the Najrān dispute gives it its edge: a delegation had made a specific claim, the Prophet had answered it with a question — "who was Adam's father?" — and the verse came down as the answer in its final form **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. And it is followed immediately by the instruction not to doubt, which tells you what kind of claim this is **[Ṭabarī]**.

## 3:60 — ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّكَ فَلَا تَكُن مِّنَ ٱلْمُمْتَرِينَ

*The truth is from your Lord, so do not be of those who doubt.*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: that which I have told you of the report about Jesus, and that his likeness is as the likeness of Adam, whom He created from dust and then his Lord said to him "Be" — that is the truth from your Lord; "so do not be of those who doubt" — meaning: do not be of those who doubt that it is so. **[Jalālayn]** "The truth is from your Lord" — *al-ḥaqqu min rabbik*, the predicate of a missing subject, which is implied to be "the matter concerning Jesus" — "be not of those who waver", those who are uncertain about it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "The truth from your Lord, so be not of those who doubt" — meaning: this is the only true story about ʿĪsā, and what is beyond truth save falsehood? **[Study Quran]** This verse says not to doubt the description of Jesus in the previous verse.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Farrāʾ said: "the truth" is in the nominative by an implied pronoun — that is, "it is the truth from your Lord". **[Qurṭubī]** Abū ʿUbaydah said: it is the resumption of speech, and its predicate lies in "from your Lord". **[Qurṭubī]** And it is said that it is the subject — that is, "the truth has come to you". **[Jalālayn]** The subject is suppressed and understood as "the matter concerning Jesus". **[Ṭabarī]** *Al-miryah*, *al-shakk* and *al-rayb* are all one and the same, like "give me", "hand me" and "come now" — different in wording but one in meaning.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** Qatādah said, on "the truth from your Lord, so do not be of those who doubt": do not be in doubt about Jesus — that he is as the likeness of Adam, the servant of God and His messenger, and His word and a spirit from Him. **[Ṭabarī]** Al-Rabīʿ b. Anas said: do not be in doubt concerning what We have related to you — that Jesus is the servant of God and His messenger, and a word from Him and a spirit, and that his likeness with God is as the likeness of Adam, whom He created from dust and then said to him "Be", and he was. **[Ṭabarī]** From Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "the truth from your Lord" is what has come to you of the report about Jesus; and "so do not be of those who doubt" means: the truth has come to you from your Lord, so do not doubt it. **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn Zayd said: the doubters are those who doubt.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** The verse is placed immediately after the argument of 3:59 and immediately before the challenge to mutual cursing at 3:61, so that the whole Najrān passage is bracketed by a claim and a warning not to step back from it. **[Study Quran]** Addressed to the Prophet, the prohibition is taken as bearing on the description of Jesus given in the preceding verse rather than on anything the Prophet himself had questioned. **[Jalālayn]** The address to the Prophet carries the community with it, since the doubt the verse forbids is the one the Najrān dispute was designed to plant.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** What is not to be doubted is stated positively by the commentators: that Jesus is the servant of God and His messenger, and a word from Him and a spirit, and that his likeness with God is as the likeness of Adam. **[Ibn Kathīr]** There is no third option beside the true story and falsehood. **[Saʿdī]** This is the truth in which there is no doubt — that Jesus is as he said of himself: "I said nothing to them except what You commanded me: worship God, my Lord and your Lord". **[Jalālayn]** The prohibition of doubting is addressed to the Prophet, and through him to the community.

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** 3:59, whose account is the thing not to be doubted; and his earlier treatment of *al-imtirāʾ* and of the parallel verse. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:61–63, the *mubāhala* verses that follow. **[Saʿdī]** 5:117 and 3:62. **[Qurṭubī]** 3:61 for the continuation of the passage. **[Study Quran]** 3:59 for the description referred to.

**Reflection.** A single line, addressed to the Prophet, telling him not to doubt — which is exactly the kind of line that has to be explained, since the Prophet is not in doubt **[Study Quran]**, **[Jalālayn]**. The commentators handle it by turning the prohibition into a definition: the thing not to be doubted is that Jesus is God's servant and messenger, and a word from Him and a spirit, and that his case is Adam's case **[Ṭabarī]**. There is a quiet grammar quarrel underneath — subject suppressed, or resumption, or a fresh sentence — and each reading shifts where the emphasis falls **[Qurṭubī]**. And the sentence does its real work on the reader rather than the addressee: having built the argument in 3:59, the sūrah immediately closes the door on hesitation, as though it knew how hard this particular claim would be to hold **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Saʿdī]**.

*Source note:* Saʿdī and Maʿārif each treat 3:59–60 as a single unit; their material on the Najrān background, the *mubāhala* and "this is the true narrative" is cited at 3:59, and there is nothing separate from them to cite here beyond what is given above.

## 3:61 — فَمَنْ حَاجَّكَ فِيهِ مِن بَعْدِ مَا جَاءَكَ مِنَ ٱلْعِلْمِ فَقُلْ تَعَالَوْا۟ نَدْعُ أَبْنَاءَنَا وَأَبْنَاءَكُمْ وَنِسَاءَنَا وَنِسَاءَكُمْ وَأَنفُسَنَا وَأَنفُسَكُمْ ثُمَّ نَبْتَهِلْ فَنَجْعَل لَّعْنَتَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَى ٱلْكَٰذِبِينَ

*"Then whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you, say: 'Come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves; then let us pray earnestly and invoke the curse of God upon the liars.'"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by "whoever disputes with you concerning him": whoever argues with you, O Muḥammad, about the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary. **[Ṭabarī]** The pronoun in "concerning him" goes back to the mention of Jesus; and it is admissible that it goes back to "the truth" which He said in "the truth is from your Lord". **[Ṭabarī]** "After the knowledge that has come to you" — after the knowledge has come to you which I have made clear to you concerning Jesus, that he is the servant of God. **[Ṭabarī]** "Then say: 'Come!'" — come here — "let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves, then let us pray earnestly" — then let us curse one another. **[Ṭabarī]** In speech one says "What is wrong with him? *Bahalahu Allāh*" — meaning "may God curse him" — and "What is wrong with him? Upon him be the *buhlat* of God", meaning the curse. **[Ṭabarī]** And Labīd said, mentioning a people who perished: "Time looked at them and *ibtahala*" — meaning he called down destruction upon them. **[Qurṭubī]** "Whoever disputes with you" means argues with you and contends with you, O Muḥammad; "concerning him" — concerning Jesus; "after what has come to you of knowledge" — that he is the servant of God and His messenger. **[Qurṭubī]** "Then say: 'Come!'", meaning come forward; the word was coined for one who has majesty and loftiness, then in usage it came to apply to everyone calling another to approach. **[Qurṭubī]** "Then let us pray earnestly" (*nabtahil*) — we humble ourselves in supplication, according to Ibn ʿAbbās; Abū ʿUbaydah and al-Kisāʾī said: we invoke curses on one another; and the root of *ibtihāl* is exertion in supplication, whether by cursing or otherwise. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Then whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you, say: 'Come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves'" — for the *mubāhala*; "then we pray" — supplicate — "and we invoke Allāh's curse upon the liars", among the two of us. **[Jalālayn]** "And whoever from among the Christians disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge of his affair that has come to you, say to them: 'Come! Let us call our sons and your sons, our wives and your wives, ourselves and yourselves, and gather them together, then let us humbly pray and invoke God's curse upon those who lie'" — by saying "Lord, curse the one that tells lies concerning the affair of Jesus". **[Study Quran]** There are varying accounts of the details of the encounter, but the elements common to the story are that when the Christians refused to embrace Islam and continued to hold to their beliefs about Jesus, they were challenged to place the curse of God upon those who lie, and the two sides agreed to meet the next day. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 61 instructed the Holy Prophet ﷺ to invite his opponents, the Christians, to *mubāhala*, which literally means "mutual prayer", but in the terminology of Islamic Sharīʿah refers to a particular form of resolving religious disputes: when the argumentation from both sides fails to resolve a religious issue, the parties jointly pray to God to cast His curse on whichever of the two parties is false. **[Maʿārif]** Since curse means moving someone far away from the divine mercy — and moving far away from mercy is being close to divine wrath — therefore the essence of the meaning is: wrath be on the liar; as such, whoever is the liar shall face the evil consequences, whereupon the truth will become evident before the disbelievers as well.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** One says in speech *bahalahu Allāh* — God cursed him — and *al-bahl* is the curse; and Labīd's line uses *ibtahala* of time destroying a people. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-bahl* is the curse; *al-bahl* is also a small amount of water; *abhaltuhu* means "I left him and his desire", and *bahaltuhu* likewise; Abū ʿUbaydah reported *bahalahu Allāhu yubhiluhu bahlatan*, meaning He cursed him. **[Qurṭubī]** *Taʿālaw* was originally said of one possessed of majesty and loftiness, then passed into general use for every call to approach. **[Qurṭubī]** *Nadʿu* is in the jussive position. **[Qurṭubī]** "Our sons" is a proof that the sons of daughters are called sons — and that is because the Prophet ﷺ brought al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn, with Fāṭimah walking behind him and ʿAlī behind her, and he was saying to them: "When I supplicate, say *āmīn*." **[Maʿārif]** The words "our sons" in verse 61 do not mean sons of the loins only; the sense is general, and they may be sons or grandsons, because this is how the word is used in common usage. **[Maʿārif]** Therefore the grandsons of the Holy Prophet ﷺ, Ḥasan and Ḥusayn, as well as ʿAlī, the son-in-law of the Holy Prophet ﷺ, are all included in the words "our sons". **[Maʿārif]** ʿAlī has an additional reason for being included among the "sons" of the Holy Prophet ﷺ, because he had grown up under the patronage of the Holy Prophet ﷺ, who had treated him like his own son.

**Context.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** The reason for the call to the *mubāhala* and for the revelation of the verses from the beginning of this sūrah up to here is that a delegation from the Christians of Najrān came to Madinah to argue about ʿĪsā, claiming that he was divine and the son of God; God sent down the beginning of this sūrah up to here to refute their claims, as Imām Muḥammad b. Isḥāq b. Yasār and other scholars stated. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Muḥammad b. Isḥāq said in his famous *Sīrah*: "The delegation of Christians from Najrān came to the Messenger of God ﷺ; the delegation consisted of sixty horsemen, including fourteen of their chiefs who make decisions" — among them al-ʿĀqib, also known as ʿAbdul-Masīḥ, al-Sayyid, also known as al-Ayham, and Abū Ḥārithah b. ʿAlqamah, their patriarch, priest and religious leader. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Abū Ḥārithah knew the description of the Messenger of God ﷺ from what he read in the earlier divine Books; however, his ignorance led him to insist on remaining a Christian, because he was honoured and held a high position with the Christians. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Three of them were the chiefs of the delegation: al-ʿĀqib, their leader, to whom they referred for advice and decision; al-Sayyid, their scholar and their leader in journeys and social gatherings; and Abū Ḥārithah, their patriarch. **[Ibn Kathīr]** They disagreed among themselves about ʿĪsā: some said "He is God", some said "He is the son of God", and others said "He is one of a trinity"; God is far above what they attribute to Him. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: they are the people of Najrān — al-Sayyid, al-ʿĀqib and Ibn al-Ḥārith, their chiefs. **[Ṭabarī]** The word "liars" covers the liars among us and among you concerning that Jesus is the servant of God and His messenger — not that he is "God", exalted is God far above what they say. *(editorial note in the printed Tabarī)* **[Study Quran]** The Christians consulted among themselves and concluded that, since they wished to remain Christian, no further good could come from entering into this challenge: either they feared the curse of God upon themselves if Muḥammad was in fact a true prophet, or they feared him politically and did not desire to become his enemy.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7171 — Qatādah, on "whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you": concerning Jesus — that he is the servant of God and His messenger, from the word of God and His spirit. **[Ṭabarī]** 7172 — from Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "after what I have recounted to you of his report and how his affair was". **[Ṭabarī]** 7173 — al-Rabīʿ b. Anas: "whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you" — whoever disputes with you about Jesus after the knowledge that has come to you about him. **[Ṭabarī]** 7174 — Ibn Zayd, on "then let us pray earnestly and invoke the curse of God upon the liars": from us and from you. **[Ṭabarī]** 7175 — from ʿAbdullāh b. al-Ḥārith b. Jazʾ al-Zubaydī, that he heard the Prophet ﷺ say: "Would that there were a screen between me and the people of Najrān, so that I should not see them and they should not see me!" — because of how bitterly they used to wrangle with the Prophet ﷺ. **[Jalālayn]** The Prophet ﷺ called upon the Najrān delegation to do this when they disputed with him about Jesus; they said, "Let us think about it and we will come back to you". **[Jalālayn]** The judicious one among them said: "You know that he is a prophet, and that every people that has ever challenged a prophet to a mutual imprecation has been destroyed." **[Jalālayn]** When they came to the Prophet ﷺ, who had set out with al-Ḥasan, al-Ḥusayn, Fāṭimah and ʿAlī, he said to them: "When I supplicate, say 'Āmīn'"; but they refrained from this mutual imprecation and made peace with the Prophet on the condition that they pay the *jizya*, as reported by Abū Nuʿaym. **[Jalālayn]** According to Ibn ʿAbbās the Prophet said: "Had they set out and performed the mutual cursing, they would have gone home and found neither possessions nor family"; and it is also reported that had they set out with this intention they would have been consumed by fire. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Bukhārī recorded that Ḥudhayfah said: "Al-ʿĀqib and al-Sayyid, two leaders from Najrān, came to the Messenger of God ﷺ seeking to invoke God for curses, and one of them said to the other, 'Let us not do that. By God, if he were truly a Prophet and we invoke God for curses, we and our offspring shall never succeed afterwards.'" **[Ibn Kathīr]** So they said: "We will give you what you asked, and send a trusted man with us — just a trusted man" — and the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "I will send a trusted man with you, a truly trustworthy man"; the Companions all felt eager to be that man, and he said, "O Abū ʿUbaydah b. al-Jarrāḥ! Stand up" — and when he stood, the Messenger of God ﷺ said, "This is the trustee of this Ummah". **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Bukhārī also recorded that Anas said that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Every Ummah has a trustee, and the trustee of this Ummah is Abū ʿUbaydah b. al-Jarrāḥ." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Imām Aḥmad recorded that Ibn ʿAbbās said that Abū Jahl said, "If I see Muḥammad praying next to the Kaʿbah, I will step on his neck" — and the Prophet ﷺ later said: "Had he tried to do it, the angels would have taken him publicly. Had the Jews wished for death, they would have perished and would have seen their seats in the Fire. Had those who sought *mubāhala* with the Messenger of God ﷺ gone ahead with it, they would not have found estates or families when they returned home." **[Qurṭubī]** This verse is among the signs of the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ, because he called them to the *mubāhala* and they refused it and accepted the *jizya*, after their elder al-ʿĀqib had informed them that if they performed the *mubāhala* with him the valley would blaze with fire against them — "for Muḥammad is a sent prophet, and you know that he has brought you the decisive word about the affair of Jesus". **[Qurṭubī]** So they abandoned the *mubāhala* and returned to their lands on the basis that they would pay every year a thousand garments in Ṣafar and a thousand garments in Rajab, and the Messenger of God ﷺ made peace with them on that in place of Islam. **[Ṭabarī]** 7180 — from Mughīrah, from ʿĀmir (al-Shaʿbī): he — the Prophet ﷺ — was commanded to imprecate them, meaning the people of Najrān, by "whoever disputes with you concerning him after the knowledge that has come to you"; they appointed a time to imprecate him, and he appointed them the morrow. **[Ṭabarī]** They went to al-Sayyid and al-ʿĀqib, who were the most sensible of them, and followed their lead; they went on to a sensible man among them and mentioned what they had parted from the Messenger of God ﷺ upon, and he said: "What have you done!!" and reproached them, saying: "If he is a prophet and he prays against you, God will never fail to be angry with you on his account; and if he is a king and he prevails over you, he will never spare you." **[Study Quran]** They requested that the Prophet send back with them a trustworthy Companion, which he did — according to one account, in order that he might act as a kind of arbiter among them regarding some pre-existing financial issues.

**Rulings.** **[Maʿārif]** Originally the *mubāhala* can be carried out by the disputants only; they can simply get together and pray, and it is not necessary that all their family members be assembled — however, if all the family members participate in the *mubāhala*, it becomes more effective, and that is why the Holy Prophet ﷺ was instructed to invite the family members of both sides. **[Study Quran]** The Prophet and the Najrān delegation came to an agreement that the Najrānīs would become a treaty-holding people (*dhimmī*), remaining Christian and running their own affairs, but paying a yearly indemnity (*jizyah*; see 9:29) to the Muslim community. **[Qurṭubī]** The settlement the Prophet accepted from them was a fixed annual payment in place of Islam.

**Belief.** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse is numbered among the signs (*aʿlām*) of Muḥammad's prophethood, since the challenge was issued and declined. **[Maʿārif]** The *mubāhala* is the last resort when argument has been exhausted, and its point is that the liar is exposed before both parties. **[Ṭabarī]** What is staked on the outcome is precisely the claim that Jesus is the servant of God and His messenger and not God. *(editorial note in the printed Tabarī)* **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Allāh is far above what they attribute to Him" — the three Christian claims recorded here are all refuted by the Qurʾān.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 9:29 for the *jizyah*. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:59–60 for the argument that precedes; 19:21 for the sign of mankind. **[Maʿārif]** 3:59 for the claim about Jesus that is at issue. **[Qurṭubī]** 6, p. 130 for a fuller treatment of *taʿālaw*. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:120–121 for his earlier treatment of the root *ḥ-j-j*.

**Reflection.** The movement of the passage is worth pausing on: argument first, and only then, when argument has been exhausted, a curse **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**. The Prophet is told to stake his own household on the outcome — his sons, his women, himself — and the commentators note that "sons" here means grandsons, which is how the two boys came to be standing there **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Maʿārif]**. Then the other side declines. Qurṭubī treats the refusal as one of the proofs of the prophethood: the challenge was real, and it was not taken up **[Qurṭubī]**. What Ṭabarī adds is the human detail — a sensible man of Najrān hearing what they had agreed to and saying, in effect, that they had walked into a trap with no good exit, since a curse from a prophet and a victory by a king are both fatal **[Ṭabarī]**. And there is the odd, tired line of the Prophet himself, wishing for a screen between him and them because of how they wrangled **[Ṭabarī]** — which is what a dispute looks like from the inside once the arguments have all been made.

*Source note:* Saʿdī treats 3:59–62 as a single block; his account of the Najrān delegation, the summons to *mubāhala* and the settlement is cited at 3:59, and the remainder of that block at 3:62.

## 3:62 — إِنَّ هَٰذَا لَهُوَ ٱلْقَصَصُ ٱلْحَقُّ ۚ وَمَا مِنْ إِلَٰهٍ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَهُوَ ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلْحَكِيمُ

*"This is indeed the true narrative. And there is no god but God. And indeed, God is the Mighty, the Wise."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: this that I have told you, O Muḥammad, of the affair of Jesus, and that I have recounted to you of his story — and that he is My servant and My messenger, and My word which I cast to Mary, and a spirit from Me — this is the true narrative and report, so know it. **[Ṭabarī]** And know that creation has no object of worship that deserves their worship by His owning them, other than the One whom you worship, and He is God, the Mighty, the Wise. **[Ṭabarī]** "The Mighty" — mighty in His vengeance against whoever disobeys Him and opposes His command, and claims a god alongside Him or worships a lord other than Him. **[Ṭabarī]** "The Wise" — wise in His governance; no weakness enters what He ordains and no flaw attaches to it. **[Jalālayn]** This mentioned above is the true story: the report free of any doubt. **[Jalālayn]** There is no god but God, and assuredly God is Mighty in His Kingdom, Wise in His actions. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Verily, this is the true narrative" — meaning, what We narrated to you, O Muḥammad, about ʿĪsā is the plain truth that cannot be avoided. **[Qurṭubī]** The reference in "this is" is to the Qurʾān and the narratives it contains; they are called *qaṣaṣ* because the meanings follow one upon another in them, from their saying "so-and-so follows (*yaquṣṣu*) the track of so-and-so" — that is, he follows it. **[Qurṭubī]** "And there is no god but God" — *min* is extra, for emphasis, so the meaning is "and there is no god but God"; "the Mighty" is the One who is not overcome; "the Wise" is the possessor of wisdom. **[Saʿdī]** And therefore He said, exalted is He: "this is indeed the true narrative" — meaning that in which there is no doubt; "and God is the Mighty" who has subdued by His power and strength all existents, and to whom the inhabitants of the earth and the heavens have submitted — and with all that He is "the Wise", who places things in their places and assigns them their stations. **[Study Quran]** The true account is understood to refer to the preceding verses, meaning that they are a true account of Jesus' reality and of what led to the prayer challenge, or it refers to the Qurʾān as a whole and the accounts it relates.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** *Qaṣaṣ* is so named because the meanings follow one another in it, from *qaṣṣa al-athar*, "he followed the track"; and *min* in *mā min ilāhin* is redundant, added for emphasis. **[Study Quran]** It is noteworthy that here "there is no god but God" translates *mā min ilāhin illā 'Llāh*, not the usual formula *lā ilāha illā 'Llāh*; the latter is the standard Islamic testimony of faith in Arabic, while the former is understood by some as a kind of intensification of the negation of other gods. **[Ṭabarī]** "The Mighty" is glossed by vengeance against the disobedient and "the Wise" by governance in which no weakness or flaw enters.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7176 — from Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr: "this is the true narrative" — meaning, this that you have brought of the report about Jesus is the true narrative of his affair. **[Ṭabarī]** 7177 — from Ibn Jurayj: "this that We have said about Jesus is the true narrative". **[Ṭabarī]** 7178 — Ibn Zayd said: this is the true narrative about Jesus — it is not fitting for Jesus to go beyond this or to pass it: that he should go beyond being the word of God cast to Mary, and a spirit from Him, and the servant of God and His messenger. **[Ṭabarī]** 7179 — from Ibn ʿAbbās: "this that We have said about Jesus is the truth", and "there is no god but God", the verse. **[Ṭabarī]** When God had separated between His prophet Muḥammad ﷺ and the delegation of the Christians of Najrān with the decisive judgement and the just ruling, He commanded him — if they turned away from what he had called them to, of affirming the oneness of God, that He has no child and no consort, and that Jesus is His servant and His messenger, and insisted on nothing but contention and dispute — to call them to the imprecation; and the Messenger of God ﷺ did that. **[Ṭabarī]** When he did it, they drew back and refused the imprecation, and called for a settlement.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The truth asserted is a defined set of propositions about Jesus: servant, messenger, word cast to Mary, spirit from God — and the two names close the matter, since the Mighty cannot be overcome and the Wise ordains nothing with a flaw in it. **[Saʿdī]** God's might and wisdom are held together: He subdues everything by His power, and with all of that He places everything in its proper place. **[Ibn Kathīr]** What has been narrated is the plain truth that cannot be avoided. **[Qurṭubī]** The negation of divinity is absolute and the *min* merely emphasises it.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 3:59–61 for the account referred to. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:88 and 6:165, 168, 271 for his treatment of "the Mighty"; 3:59–63 for the whole Najrān passage. **[Qurṭubī]** His earlier treatment of "the Mighty" and "the Wise". **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:63, which follows.

**Reflection.** The verse is a verdict, and the verdict is narrower than it first looks: what is "the true narrative" is not a general claim about honesty but the specific set of propositions about Jesus that the sūrah has just built **[Ṭabarī]**. Ibn Zayd's formulation is the sharpest — that it is not fitting for Jesus to be carried past being the word of God, a spirit from Him, and His servant and messenger **[Ṭabarī]**. Then come the two names, and Saʿdī reads them as a deliberate pairing: absolute power that could crush, and wisdom that places everything where it belongs **[Saʿdī]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. There is also a small textual point worth keeping — the negation of other gods here is not phrased in the formula Muslims repeat, and the commentators who notice it read that as an intensification **[Study Quran]**. And Ṭabarī situates the whole verse as the moment the judgement closes: the disputation has ended, the offer of the curse follows, and it is declined **[Ṭabarī]**.

*Source note:* Maʿārif treats 3:61–63 as a single unit; its discussion of the meaning of *mubāhala* and of "our sons" is cited at 3:61, and it has nothing separate for 3:62 or 3:63.

## 3:63 — فَإِن تَوَلَّوْا۟ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ عَلِيمٌۢ بِٱلْمُفْسِدِينَ

*"And if they turn away, then God knows well the workers of corruption."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** "And if they turn away" — meaning: if these who disputed with you about Jesus turn away from the truth that has come to you from your Lord concerning Jesus, and from all besides it of the guidance and clarification God gave you, and turn from it and do not accept it. **[Ṭabarī]** "Then God knows well the workers of corruption" — meaning: God has knowledge of those who disobey their Lord and do in His earth and His lands what He forbade them, and that is their corruption (*ifṣād*). **[Ṭabarī]** He says, exalted is His mention: so He is knowing of them and of their works, counting them against them and preserving them, until He recompenses them for them with their recompense. **[Jalālayn]** "And if they turn their backs", rejecting faith, "assuredly God knows the agents of corruption" — here the third-person pronominalisation has been replaced with the overt noun *al-mufsidūn*, "the agents of corruption". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And if they turn away" — by abandoning this truth — "then surely, Allāh is All-Aware of those who do mischief" — for those who abandon the truth for falsehood commit mischief, and Allāh has full knowledge of them and will subject them to the worst punishment. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Verily, Allāh is able to control everything; all praise and thanks are due to Him, and we seek refuge with Him from His revenge. **[Saʿdī]** The matter with them reached the point that God commanded the Prophet ﷺ to engage in *mubāhala* with them, since the truth had become clear to them but obstinacy and partisanship prevented them from it; and their obstinacy in refusing the *mubāhala*, when he had granted them a truce and not pressed them, proves that they were wrongdoers.

**Context.** **[Ṭabarī]** The "turning away" is what the Najrān delegation actually did, and the reports describe the morning it happened. **[Ṭabarī]** Their adviser told them: when you go to him in the morning and he offers you what you parted from yesterday, say "We seek refuge in God"; and if he calls you again, say it again — perhaps he will release you from it. **[Ṭabarī]** So when they came in the morning, the Prophet ﷺ came carrying Ḥasan, holding the hand of Ḥusayn, with Fāṭimah walking behind him, and he called them to what they had parted from the day before — and they said "We seek refuge in God!" **[Ṭabarī]** He called them again and they repeated it; then he said: "If you refuse, then embrace Islam, and you shall have what the Muslims have and owe what the Muslims owe, as God has said; and if you refuse, then pay the *jizya* by hand, being humbled, as God has said." **[Ṭabarī]** They said: "We own nothing but ourselves." He said: "Then if you refuse, I announce to you a state of equal footing, as God has said." **[Ṭabarī]** They said: "We have no capacity for a war with the Arabs, but we will pay the *jizya*" — and he imposed upon them two thousand garments a year, a thousand in Rajab and a thousand in Ṣafar. **[Ṭabarī]** And the Prophet ﷺ said: "The bearer of good tidings came to me of the destruction of the people of Najrān — even the birds on the trees" — or, in one version, "the sparrows on the trees" — "had they gone through with the imprecation."

**Language.** **[Jalālayn]** The expected pronoun is replaced by the overt noun *al-mufsidūn*, "the agents of corruption". **[Ṭabarī]** "Turn away" (*tawallaw*) is glossed as turning one's back and refusing to accept, and "corruption" (*ifsād*) as doing in God's earth what He forbade. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The sentence ends with the consequence drawn out: those who exchange truth for falsehood are the workers of corruption.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The threat is not immediate punishment but exact knowledge preserved for the reckoning: God counts and keeps their works until He recompenses them. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Abandoning the truth for falsehood is itself the corruption. **[Saʿdī]** Refusing the challenge after the truth had been made clear is what establishes that they, not the Prophet, were the wrongdoers.

**Cross-references.** **[Ṭabarī]** 2:162–164, 3:131 and 4:237 for his treatment of *tawallā*; 1:287, 416 and 4:238, 243, 244 for *fasād*; 3:59–62 for the passage that precedes. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:62 for the truth that is abandoned. **[Study Quran]** 3:62, with which this verse is paired in the printed text. **[Jalālayn]** 3:61–62 for the refusal.

*Source note:* Qurṭubī's block for 3:62–63 comments only on 3:62, and the Study Quran's combined note for 3:62–63 is likewise exhausted there; Saʿdī's block for 3:59–62 ends at 3:62; and Maʿārif treats 3:61–63 as a single unit whose material belongs entirely to 3:61 — so there is nothing distinct from these four to cite here.


**Reflection.** Where 3:62 states the truth, 3:63 states what happens when it is refused, and the two are one sentence in the Arabic **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The word used for the refusal is "turn away", which al-Ṭabarī unpacks carefully — it is not ignorance but a turning from guidance that has arrived **[Ṭabarī]**. And the closing name is not "the Punisher" but "the one who knows the corrupters", which the same commentator reads as a delay rather than a remission: the works are counted and kept until the recompense **[Ṭabarī]**. Jalālayn notes the small grammatical oddity — the pronoun the sentence seems to be reaching for is swapped for the plain noun "the agents of corruption", and the naming is the point **[Jalālayn]**. Within the Najrān story the verse lands as an explanation of what the refusal actually was: not a failure of evidence but obstinacy, and the refusal itself became the proof **[Saʿdī]**.


## 3:64 — قُلْ يَٰٓأَهْلَ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ تَعَالَوْا۟ إِلَىٰ كَلِمَةٍۢ سَوَاءٍۭ بَيْنَنَا وَبَيْنَكُمْ أَلَّا نَعْبُدَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهَ وَلَا نُشْرِكَ بِهِۦ شَيْـًۭٔا وَلَا يَتَّخِذَ بَعْضُنَا بَعْضًا أَرْبَابًۭا مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ ۚ فَإِن تَوَلَّوْا۟ فَقُولُوا۟ ٱشْهَدُوا۟ بِأَنَّا مُسْلِمُونَ

*Say: "O People of the Scripture, come to a word common between us and you: that we worship none but God, associate nothing with Him, and take not one another as lords apart from God." And if they turn away, say: "Bear witness that we are submitters."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means: "Say", O Muḥammad, to the People of the Scripture — that is, the people of the Torah and the Gospel — "'Come'", come here — "to a word common (*sawāʾ*) between us and you", meaning: to a word of justice between us and you; and the just word is that we declare God to be one, so that we worship none other than Him and disown every object of worship besides Him, associating nothing with Him. **[Ṭabarī]** "And let not some of us take others as lords apart from God" — meaning: and let none of us profess obedience to another in what he commands of disobedience to God, and let him not venerate him by prostrating to him as one prostrates to his Lord. **[Ṭabarī]** "And if they turn away" — if they turn from what you called them to, of the common word which I commanded you to call them to, and do not answer you to it — "then say", O believers, to those who turn away from that: "Bear witness that we are submitters". **[Jalālayn]** "Say: 'O People of the Scripture! Jews and Christians, come now to a word agreed upon'" — *sawāʾ* is the verbal noun meaning *mustawin amruhā*, "a word regarding which the matter is upright" — "between us and you, and it is that we worship none but God" — *allā* is made up of *an-lā*, "that…not" — "and that we do not associate anything with Him and do not take each other for lords beside God", as you have taken rabbis and monks; "and if they turn their backs" in rejection of God's oneness, "say you to them: 'Bear witness that we have submitted'", that we are of those who affirm the oneness of God. **[Ibn Kathīr]** This verse includes the People of the Book, the Jews and the Christians, and those who follow their ways. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Come to a word" — "word" in Arabic also means a complete sentence, as is evident from this verse; God described this word as being one "that is the same between us and you", an honest and righteous word that is fair to both parties. **[Ibn Kathīr]** "That we worship none but Allāh, and that we associate no partners with Him" — we worship neither a statue, cross, idol, *ṭāghūt*, fire, nor anything else; rather, we worship Allāh alone without partners, and this is the message of all of Allāh's Messengers. **[Qurṭubī]** The address, in the opinion of al-Ḥasan, Ibn Zayd and al-Suddī, is to the people of Najrān; and in the opinion of Qatādah, Ibn Jurayj and others, to the Jews of Madinah, who were addressed thus because they made their rabbis like lords in the obedience they gave them; and it is said that it is to the Jews and the Christians together. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-sawāʾ* is justice and fairness — said Qatādah; and al-Farrāʾ said the meaning of "the just, straight word" is that in which there is no swerving from the truth, and He explained it by "that we worship none but God". **[Maʿārif]** This verse unfolds an important principle of *tablīgh* and *daʿwah*: the principle requires that a person who desires to carry his call to a group holding beliefs and ideas different from his own should follow a particular method — that method being to induce that group to unite only on what they both can agree to. **[Maʿārif]** For instance, when the Holy Prophet ﷺ gave the Byzantine ruler, Hiraql (Heraclius), the call to Islam, he picked out a particular point on which there was mutual agreement, namely the Oneness of God Almighty. **[Study Quran]** Interpreting this verse hinges on the understanding of "common" (*sawāʾ*), a word sometimes understood as meaning "just" or "fair". **[Study Quran]** *Sawāʾ* comes from a root meaning "level [with]", "same", "equal", "straight", "sound", "well-proportioned" or "middle [of]", as in *sawāʾ al-sabīl*, which literally means "the middle of the road" but idiomatically means "the right way". **[Saʿdī]** This noble verse — the Prophet ﷺ used to write it to the kings of the People of the Scripture. **[Saʿdī]** It comprises the *tawḥīd* of divinity built upon the worship of God alone with no partner, and that one believe that human beings and all creation are entirely at the level of humanity, and that none of them deserves anything of the attributes of lordship or of the descriptions of divinity. **[Saʿdī]** So if the People of the Scripture and others submit to this, they are guided; and "if they turn away, then say: bear witness that we are submitters", like His saying "Say: O disbelievers…" to the end of it.

**Language.** **[Jalālayn]** *Sawāʾ* is a verbal noun meaning "a word regarding which the matter is upright"; and *allā* is composed of *an* plus *lā*. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-sawāʾ* is justice and fairness, per Qatādah; and Zuhayr said: "Show me a plan in which there is no wrongdoing, in which fairness makes things equal between us." **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Farrāʾ said: one says *siwan* and *suwan* in the sense of justice; if you open the *sīn* you lengthen it, and if you vowellise it with *kasrah* or *ḍammah* you shorten it, as in "a level place" (20:58). **[Qurṭubī]** The position of *an* is either genitive, as a substitute for *kalimah*, or nominative with an implied subject — the estimate being "it is that we worship none but God" — or it is explicative with no position at all; and with that, both the nominative and the jussive are admissible in *naʿbuda* and what is conjoined to it, the jussive being on the basis that *an* is explicative in the sense of *ay*. **[Ṭabarī]** The "just word" is defined by its content: declaring God one, worshipping none other, and disowning every object of worship besides Him. **[Study Quran]** The word *likeness* has, in this verse, the sense of a just and fair word rather than of what the religions hold in common.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** In the reading of ʿAbdullāh: "to a word of justice (*ʿadl*) between us and you". **[Qurṭubī]** And Qaʿnab read *kilmatin* with the *lām* made quiescent, transferring the vowel of the *lām* to the *kāf*, just as one says *kibd*.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7191 — Qatādah said: it has been mentioned to us that the prophet of God ﷺ called the Jews of Madinah to the common word, and they are the ones who disputed about Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** 7192 — al-Rabīʿ said: it has been mentioned to us that the prophet of God ﷺ called the Jews to the common word. **[Ṭabarī]** 7193 — Ibn Jurayj said: we have been told that the prophet of God ﷺ called the Jews of Madinah to that and they refused him, so he fought them — he called them to the saying of God, "Say: O People of the Scripture, come to a word common between us and you", the verse. **[Ṭabarī]** 7194 — from Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Jaʿfar b. al-Zubayr, on "Say: O People of the Scripture, come to a word common between us and you" up to "then say: bear witness that we are submitters": he called them to fairness and cut off their argument — meaning the delegation of Najrān. **[Qurṭubī]** And in the letter of the Prophet ﷺ to Heraclius: "In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Very-Merciful. From Muḥammad, the Messenger of God, to Heraclius, the great one of the Romans. Peace upon him who follows the guidance. To proceed: I invite you with the call of Islam. Embrace Islam and you will be safe; embrace Islam and God will give you your reward twice over; but if you turn away, then upon you is the sin of the *arīsiyyīn* [the tillers and peasants] — and: 'O People of the Scripture, come to a word common between us and you, that we worship none but God…' up to 'then say: bear witness that we are submitters'" — the wording of Muslim. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Ibn Jurayj commented on "and that none of us shall take others as lords besides Allāh": "We do not obey each other in disobedience to Allāh". **[Ibn Kathīr]** Muḥammad b. Isḥāq and other scholars said that the beginning of Sūrah Āl ʿImrān, and more than eighty verses thereafter, were revealed about the delegation of Najrān. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Al-Zuhrī stated that the people of Najrān were the first people to pay the *jizyah*; however, there is no disagreement that the verse that ordained the *jizyah* [9:29] was revealed after the conquest of Makkah — so how could this verse be contained in the Prophet's letter to Heraclius before the conquest? **[Ibn Kathīr]** The answer is that the delegation of Najrān came before al-Ḥudaybiyyah, and what they paid was in lieu of the *mubāhala*, not as *jizyah*; the verse about the *jizyah* was revealed later and its ruling supported what had occurred with the people of Najrān. **[Ibn Kathīr]** It is also possible that the Prophet ﷺ wrote this statement in his letter to Heraclius before the verse was revealed, and that the Qurʾān later agreed with his statement word by word — just as the Qurʾān was revealed in agreement with what ʿUmar said regarding the captives of Badr, the ḥijāb, refraining from the funeral prayer over the hypocrites, "And take you the station of Abraham as a place of prayer" (2:125) and "It may be, if he divorced you all, that his Lord will give him instead of you wives better than you" (66:5). **[Study Quran]** For some, the command in this verse was meant to address the Jews in and around Madinah, while for others it was directed at both Jews and Christians, but some restricted it to the Christians of Najrān in particular. **[Study Quran]** Some understand "take one another as lords" to mean that one prostrates before them; for others it means that one is obedient to them to the detriment of the truth. **[Maʿārif]** The statement "you be witness…" in the last sentence of the verse teaches us a lesson: should someone refuse to accept the truth even after it has been proved clearly, then the proper course is to re-state one's own belief and conclude the conversation — entering into further debate and verbal altercation is not appropriate.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The common word has a defined content: singling out God in worship, associating nothing with Him, and refusing to give created beings the obedience or prostration due to the Lord. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Worshipping God alone without partners is the message of every messenger, as at 21:25 and 16:36. **[Saʿdī]** The verse summons to a religion upon which all the prophets and messengers agreed — the oneness of divinity — and to the recognition that no human being partakes in lordship or divinity at all. **[Study Quran]** Some take *sawāʾ* as what the religions share, since they all claim to worship God; others, as that concerning which the Qurʾān, the Torah and the Gospel do not differ; and al-Thaʿlabī quotes an opinion of Ibn Masʿūd that to call people to the *sawāʾ* of something is to call them to the middle of it.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 21:25, "None has the right to be worshipped but I, so worship Me"; 16:36, "Worship Allāh and avoid ṭāghūt"; 2:125 and 66:5 for the revelations that came in agreement with ʿUmar; 9:29 for the *jizyah*. **[Study Quran]** 28:22 for *sawāʾ al-sabīl*; 9:31, "They have taken their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God, as well as the Messiah, son of Mary". **[Jalālayn]** 9:31 for the taking of rabbis and monks as lords. **[Saʿdī]** Sūrat al-Kāfirūn, "Say: O disbelievers…", as the parallel for the concluding instruction. **[Maʿārif]** Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī for the letter to Heraclius.

**Reflection.** This is the verse that has travelled furthest outside its own setting, and the commentators keep it tied to that setting: a Prophet writing to kings, and a delegation who would not answer **[Saʿdī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. The hinge is one word, *sawāʾ* — and the debate about it is a real one about what is being proposed. Read as "just", the verse is a demand: come to a word that is fair to both of us, and here it is **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Study Quran]**. Read as "common", it is an appeal to what the parties already share. Al-Ṭabarī settles the question by ignoring the ambiguity and defining the word as justice — and then by spelling out the justice: one God, no partner, and no human being given the obedience or the prostration that belongs to the Lord **[Ṭabarī]**. The last clause is the one Maʿārif draws out as a rule for anyone who preaches: once the case has been made and refused, you restate your own position and stop **[Maʿārif]**. There is no third step.

## 3:65 — يَٰٓأَهْلَ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ لِمَ تُحَآجُّونَ فِىٓ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَمَآ أُنزِلَتِ ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةُ وَٱلْإِنجِيلُ إِلَّا مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦٓ ۖ أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ

*"O People of the Scripture, why do you argue about Abraham, when the Torah and the Gospel were not sent down except after him? Will you not reason?"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by "O People of the Scripture": O people of the Torah and the Gospel. **[Ṭabarī]** "Why do you argue" — why do you dispute — "about Abraham" and contend concerning him, meaning Abraham, the Friend of the Merciful, upon him be blessings. **[Ṭabarī]** Their arguing about him was each party's claim, of the people of these two scriptures, that he was one of them and professed the religion of their sect; so God censured them for that claim and pointed to the contradiction in their claim, saying: how can you claim that he was upon your religion (*millah*) and your creed, when your religion is only Judaism or Christianity, and the Jew among you asserts that his religion is the establishment of the Torah and acting by what is in it, and the Christian among you asserts that his religion is the establishment of the Gospel and what is in it — and these two books were not sent down except after a span of time from Abraham's death? **[Ṭabarī]** So how can he be one of you? What is the point of your contending about him and your claiming that he was of you, when the matter is as you yourselves know? **[Jalālayn]** When the Jews claimed that Abraham was Jewish and that they were following his religion, and the Christians made a similar claim, the following was revealed: "O People of the Scripture! Why do you argue about" — dispute over — "Abraham?", claiming that he belonged to one of your two religions, "when the Torah was not revealed, neither the Gospel, but a very long time after him, and it was only after these two were revealed that Jewry and Christianity came into being. What, do you not comprehend?" — the falsehood of what you say? **[Ibn Kathīr]** Allāh censures the Jews and Christians for their dispute with Muslims over Abraham al-Khalīl and for the claim each group made that he was one of them. **[Qurṭubī]** This verse was revealed because of the claim of each party, of the Jews and the Christians, that Abraham was upon his religion; so God belied them, in that Judaism and Christianity came into being only after him — and that is His saying, "and the Torah and the Gospel were not sent down except after him". **[Qurṭubī]** "Will you not reason?" — [do you not see] the refutation of your argument and the falsehood of what you say? **[Study Quran]** This verse and the next are usually read together, **[Maʿārif]** The argumentation referred to in this verse was to decide whether Abraham followed the way of the Jews or that of the Christians; the futility of their exercise already stood exposed, as both these ways in religion appeared long after the revelation of the Old and New Testaments and simply did not exist before that — so how could it be claimed that Abraham adhered to these ways? **[Saʿdī]** All the religions — the Jews, the Christians and the idolaters, and likewise the Muslims — all claimed to be upon the religion (*millah*) of Abraham.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** The original of *lima* is *li-mā*, and the *alif* was dropped to distinguish interrogation from statement. **[Ṭabarī]** "Argue" (*tuḥājjūn*) is glossed as disputing and contending, and the object of the contention is stated to be Abraham's religion. **[Jalālayn]** The clause "what, do you not comprehend?" is glossed as "the falsehood of what you say".

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7202 — from Ibn Isḥāq, through Muḥammad b. Abī Muḥammad the client of Zayd b. Thābit, from Saʿīd b. Jubayr or ʿIkrimah, from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Christians of Najrān and the rabbis of the Jews gathered before the Messenger of God ﷺ and contended in his presence; the rabbis said, "Abraham was nothing but a Jew", and the Christians said, "Abraham was nothing but a Christian" — so God sent down concerning them, "O People of the Scripture, why do you argue about Abraham, when the Torah and the Gospel were not sent down except after him? Will you not reason?" **[Ṭabarī]** Ibn ʿAbbās continued: the Christians said "He was a Christian!" and the Jews said "He was a Jew!" — so God informed them that the Torah and the Gospel were sent down only after him, and that Judaism and Christianity were after him. **[Ṭabarī]** 7203 — Qatādah, on "O People of the Scripture, why do you argue about Abraham": you claim that he was a Jew or a Christian, "and the Torah and the Gospel were not sent down except after him" — so Judaism came after the Torah and Christianity after the Gospel; "will you not reason?" **[Ibn Kathīr]** Muḥammad b. Isḥāq reported that Ibn ʿAbbās said: "The Christians of Najrān and Jewish rabbis gathered before the Messenger of Allāh ﷺ and disputed in front of him. The rabbis said, 'Ibrāhīm was certainly Jewish.' The Christians said, 'Certainly, Ibrāhīm was Christian.' So Allāh sent down [this verse]" — meaning: how is it that you, Jews, claim Ibrāhīm was a Jew, although he lived before Allāh sent down the Tawrāh to Mūsā? And how is it that you, Christians, claim Ibrāhīm was a Christian, although Christianity came after his time? **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj said: this verse is the clearest proof against the Jews and the Christians, since the Torah and the Gospel were sent down after him, and there is in them no name for either of the two religions, while the name of Islam is in every book. **[Qurṭubī]** And it is said: between Abraham and Moses was a thousand years, and between Moses and Jesus also a thousand years.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The argument is a chronological one: a religion defined by a book cannot be attributed to a man who died before the book existed. **[Qurṭubī]** Al-Zajjāj pushes it further — the two scriptures contain no name for either religion, whereas the name of Islam appears in every book. **[Maʿārif]** The two religions in dispute did not exist in Abraham's time at all, so the claim that he adhered to either of them cannot be made. **[Saʿdī]** The claim of the Jews and the Christians to Abraham's religion is known to be a lie and a fabrication, since the Judaism and Christianity they profess were founded only after the Friend. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Each party's claim would place Abraham after a revelation that postdates him, which is impossible.

**Cross-references.** **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:66–68 for the continuation; 2:135, "And they say, 'Be Jews or Christians and you shall be rightly guided'". **[Study Quran]** 7:157 for the foretellings of the Prophet in the earlier scriptures. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:120–121 for his treatment of the root *ḥ-j-j*; 3:64 for the preceding call. **[Jalālayn]** 3:66–68 for the verses that follow.

**Reflection.** The argument is almost embarrassingly simple, which is why the commentators keep repeating it in different forms: the Torah and the Gospel came after Abraham, so Abraham cannot have been a Jew or a Christian, since those are the religions *of* those books **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]****. The occasion makes it sharper — Jews and Christians, in front of the Prophet, each claiming him, and each claim collapsing on the same fact **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Al-Zajjāj's addition is the interesting one: the names themselves are absent from the earlier scriptures, whereas "Islam" is present in every book **[Qurṭubī]**. And al-Ṭabarī's framing sentence is worth keeping: this is God censuring a claim on the grounds that it contradicts what the claimants themselves already know **[Ṭabarī]** — the failure here is not of information but of reasoning, which is exactly what the closing question asks about **[Jalālayn]**.

## 3:66 — هَٰٓأَنتُمْ هَٰٓؤُلَآءِ حَٰجَجْتُمْ فِيمَا لَكُم بِهِۦ عِلْمٌۭ فَلِمَ تُحَآجُّونَ فِيمَا لَيْسَ لَكُم بِهِۦ عِلْمٌۭ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ

*"Here you are — those who argued about that of which you have knowledge; so why do you argue about that of which you have no knowledge? God knows, and you do not know."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by "here you are" (*hā-antum*): the people who said about Abraham what they said. **[Ṭabarī]** "You argued" — you contended and disputed. **[Ṭabarī]** "About that of which you have knowledge" — about the matter of your religion which you found in your books, and which the messengers of God brought you from Him, and other than that of what you were given and whose soundness is established with you. **[Ṭabarī]** "So why do you argue" — why do you dispute and contend — "about that of which you have no knowledge", meaning: about that of which you have no knowledge, of the affair of Abraham and his religion, which you did not find in the books of God and which your prophets did not bring you and which you did not witness so as to know it? **[Ṭabarī]** "And God knows and you do not know" — God knows what is hidden from you, which you did not witness and did not see and which His messengers did not bring you, of the affair of Abraham and other matters about which you dispute; for nothing is hidden from Him and the knowledge of nothing in the heavens or the earth escapes Him. **[Ṭabarī]** "And you do not know" — of that, except what you have observed and witnessed, or grasped its knowledge by report and by hearing. **[Jalālayn]** "Lo!" — *hā* is for calling attention to something — "you" — *antum* is the subject — "are those" — the predicate is what follows — "who dispute about what you know", concerning the affair of Moses and Jesus and your claim to be adhering to their religions; "why do you then dispute concerning that of which you have no knowledge?" — of Abraham's circumstances; "and God knows" his circumstances "and you know not". **[Ibn Kathīr]** This verse criticises those who argue and dispute without knowledge, just as the Jews and Christians did concerning Ibrāhīm. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Had they disputed about their religions, which they had knowledge of, and about the law that was legislated for them until Muḥammad ﷺ was sent, it would have been better for them; rather, they disputed about what they had no knowledge of, so Allāh criticised them for this behaviour. **[Qurṭubī]** "Here you are — those who argued" means: concerning the affair of Muḥammad ﷺ, because they used to know him from the description of him which they found in their book, and they disputed about him with falsehood. **[Qurṭubī]** "Why do you argue about that of which you have no knowledge" — meaning their claim about Abraham, that he was a Jew or a Christian. **[Saʿdī]** Grant that they dispute about what they have knowledge of — how can they dispute in this case? **[Saʿdī]** This, before one even looks at the falsehood contained in their statement, makes the corruption of their claim known. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 66 points out the hollowness of their approach when they indulged in their argumentation on the basis of incomplete knowledge; that they would venture to do so on the basis of no knowledge could hardly be explained. **[Maʿārif]** The truth is that only God knows the way of Abraham. **[Study Quran]** "That of which you have knowledge" refers to the People of the Book's knowledge of Muhammad and his religion, including the foretellings of the coming of the Prophet that Muslims maintain Jews and Christians find in their own scriptures; others say their dispute was over what was forbidden or lawful in their own religion; still others say they disputed whether the law of the Qurʾān differs from the law of the Torah and the Gospel.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** The root of *hā-antum* is *a-antum*, the first *hamzah* being replaced by *hāʾ* because it is its sister — from Abū ʿAmr b. al-ʿAlāʾ and al-Akhfash; al-Naḥḥās said: this is a good saying. **[Qurṭubī]** Qunbul read, from Ibn Kathīr, *ha-antum*, like *haʿantum*. **[Qurṭubī]** And better than that is that the *hāʾ* is a substitute for a *hamzah*, its root being *a-antum*; and it is also admissible that *hā* is for alerting, entering upon *antum*, the *alif* being dropped because of the frequency of use. **[Qurṭubī]** In *hāʾulāʾi* there are two dialects, lengthening and shortening, and some of the Arabs shorten it; and Abū Ḥātim recited: "By your life, we and the confederates — *hā-ulāʾ* — are in an ordeal whose claws have not been pared." **[Qurṭubī]** And *hāʾulāʾi* here stands in the position of the vocative, meaning "O you"; and it is admissible that it is the predicate of *antum*, with *ulāʾi* in the sense of "those who" and what follows as its relative clause; and it is admissible that the predicate of *antum* is *ḥājajtum*. **[Jalālayn]** *Hā* is for calling attention, *antum* is the subject, and the predicate is what follows it. **[Ṭabarī]** "That of which you have knowledge" is defined as what was found in the books and brought by the messengers, or whose soundness is otherwise established.

**Readings.** **[Qurṭubī]** Qunbul, from Ibn Kathīr, read *ha-antum* like *haʿantum*; and *hāʾulāʾi* is read with both lengthening and shortening.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7208 — al-Suddī: as for "that which they have knowledge of", it is what was forbidden them and what they were commanded; and as for "that which they have no knowledge of", it is the affair of Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** 7209 — Qatādah: "you argued about that of which you have knowledge" — about what you witnessed and saw and observed — "why do you argue about that of which you have no knowledge" — about what you did not witness, did not see and did not observe — "and God knows and you do not know". **[Ṭabarī]** 7210 — al-Rabīʿ said the like of it. **[Qurṭubī]** It is narrated from the Prophet ﷺ that a man came to him denying his child, and said: "O Messenger of God, my wife has given birth to a black boy." **[Qurṭubī]** The Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Do you have camels?" He said: "Yes." He said: "What are their colours?" He said: "Red." He said: "Is there a dusky one among them?" He said: "Yes." He said: "Then whence is that?" He said: "Perhaps a strain of ancestry drew it out."

**Rulings.** **[Qurṭubī]** In the verse is a proof of the prohibition of disputation for one who has no knowledge, and of the ban upon one who has no verification with him — for God said, "Here you are — those who argued about that of which you have knowledge; why then do you argue about that of which you have no knowledge?" **[Qurṭubī]** And the command to dispute has come for one who knows and is certain, as in 16:125, "and dispute with them with that which is better". **[Saʿdī]** In this verse there is a proof that it is not lawful for a person to speak or to argue about that of which he has no knowledge. **[Ibn Kathīr]** God commanded them to refer what they have no knowledge of to Him Who knows the seen and the unseen matters and Who knows the true reality of all things — and this is why He said, "It is Allāh Who knows, and you know not".

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** Human knowledge is bounded by what has been witnessed, or arrived by report and hearing; God's knowledge is bounded by nothing in the heavens or the earth. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The proper response to ignorance about a matter is to refer it to the Knower, not to dispute about it. **[Maʿārif]** Only God knows the religion of Abraham, and it is described in the verse that follows.

**Cross-references.** **[Qurṭubī]** 16:125, "and dispute with them with that which is better", as the counterpart command for the one who knows. **[Study Quran]** 7:157 for the foretellings; 3:65, with which this verse is paired. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 2:135 for the parallel claim about Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:120–121 for his treatment of the root *ḥ-j-j*; 3:65 for the dispute that occasions the verse.

*Source note:* The Study Quran has no separate block for 3:66; its combined note for 3:65–66 is cited at 3:65.

**Reflection.** The rebuke is careful: they are not blamed for arguing, but for arguing outside the range of what they can know **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Ṭabarī]**. Al-Ṭabarī draws the boundary precisely — knowledge is what you found in your books, what the messengers brought you, or what you witnessed yourself; everything else is guesswork dressed as certainty **[Ṭabarī]**. Al-Suddī and Qatādah name the two sides of the line for this particular dispute: the lawful and the forbidden, which they did know, against Abraham's religion, which they did not **[Ṭabarī]**. The jurist's conclusion drawn from the verse is the one Qurṭubī and al-Saʿdī both state: disputation is forbidden to the one who does not know and commanded to the one who does — and the two are held together by 16:125 **[Qurṭubī]****, **[Saʿdī]**. And the verse ends where all such arguments should end, by handing the matter back to the One who knows it **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Maʿārif]**.


## 3:67 — مَا كَانَ إِبْرَٰهِيمُ يَهُودِيًّۭا وَلَا نَصْرَانِيًّۭا وَلَٰكِن كَانَ حَنِيفًۭا مُّسْلِمًۭا وَمَا كَانَ مِنَ ٱلْمُشْرِكِينَ

*"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but he was a ḥanīf, a submitter, and he was not one of the idolaters."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** This is God's bellying of the claim of those who disputed about Abraham and his religion, from among the Jews and the Christians, and who claimed that he was upon their religion; and His declaring Abraham quit of them, and that they are opposed to his religion; and His judgement for the people of Islām and the community of Muḥammad ﷺ that they are the people of his religion and upon his way and his laws, to the exclusion of all other people of religions. **[Ṭabarī]** God says: Abraham was not a Jew, nor a Christian, nor was he of the idolaters who worship idols and images, or a creature rather than its Creator, Who is the God of creation and their Maker. **[Ṭabarī]** "But he was a ḥanīf" — meaning: one following the command of God and obedience to Him, upright upon the clear road of guidance which he was commanded to keep to. **[Ṭabarī]** "A submitter" — meaning: one humbling himself to God in his heart, abasing himself to Him with his limbs, submitting to what He ordained for him and imposed on him of His rulings. **[Jalālayn]** God, in order to dissociate Abraham from their claims, said: "No; Abraham in truth was not a Jew, neither a Christian, but he was a Muslim", professing the Oneness of God, "and a ḥanīf", who inclined away from all other religions towards the upright one; "and he was never of the idolaters". **[Ibn Kathīr]** "Ibrāhīm was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but he was a true Muslim, a ḥanīf" — shunning *shirk* and living in *īmān* — "and he was not of the idolaters". **[Qurṭubī]** God declared him far above their false claims, and made clear that he was upon the Islamic *ḥanīfiyyah* and was not an idolater. **[Qurṭubī]** The *ḥanīf* is the one who declares God to be one, performs the pilgrimage, offers sacrifice, circumcises and faces the qiblah;
