and "the Muslim" in the language is the one who abases himself to the command of God and complies with Him. **[Saʿdī]** As for the Jews, the Christians and the idolaters, Abraham is quit of them and of their allegiance, because his religion is the tolerant *ḥanīfiyyah*, in which is belief in all the messengers and all the books — and this is the distinguishing mark of the Muslims. **[Maʿārif]** The way of Abraham, which is referred to at the end of the previous verse as known to God alone, has been described in verse 67. **[Study Quran]** Related to this verse is 2:135, "And they say, 'Be Jews or Christians and you shall be rightly guided.' Say: 'Rather, ours is the creed of Abraham, the ḥanīf, and he was not of the idolaters.'"

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Ḥanīf* is one who follows God's command, upright upon the clear road of guidance; and "submitter" is one humble in heart and limb, compliant with what God has ordained. **[Qurṭubī]** *Al-ḥanīf* is the one who declares God one, performs the ḥajj, sacrifices, circumcises and faces the qiblah; and *al-muslim* in the language is the one who abases himself to the command of God. **[Jalālayn]** *Ḥanīf* is one who inclined away from every other religion towards the upright one. **[Ibn Kathīr]** *Ḥanīf* here is paired with *muslim* and glossed as shunning *shirk* and living in faith.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7211 — al-Shaʿbī said: the Jews said, "Abraham is upon our religion", and the Christians said, "He is upon our religion" — so God sent down "Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian", the verse; and God belied them and refuted their argument — meaning the Jews who claimed that Abraham died a Jew. **[Ṭabarī]** 7212 — al-Rabīʿ said the like of it. **[Ṭabarī]** 7213 — from Mūsā b. ʿUqbah, from Sālim b. ʿAbdullāh, from his father: Zayd b. ʿAmr b. Nufayl went out to Syria, asking about the religion and following it, and he met a learned man of the Jews and asked him about his religion, saying: "Perhaps I shall profess your religion, so tell me about it." **[Ṭabarī]** The Jew said to him: "You will not be upon our religion until you take your share of the wrath of God." **[Ṭabarī]** Zayd said: "I flee only from the wrath of God, and I will never bear anything of the wrath of God while I am able. Can you direct me to a religion in which there is none of this?" **[Ṭabarī]** He said: "I know of none except that it be *ḥanīf*." He said: "And what is the *ḥanīf*?" He said: "The religion of Abraham — he was neither a Jew nor a Christian, and he worshipped none but God." **[Ṭabarī]** Then Zayd went out from him and met a learned man of the Christians, and asked him about his religion. *(the report continues with the Christian's answer)*

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The verse does three things at once: it belies the claimants, declares Abraham quit of them, and assigns his religion to the community of Muḥammad ﷺ. **[Ibn Kathīr]** He was a *ḥanīf*, shunning idolatry, and a *muslim*, and explicitly not of the idolaters. **[Saʿdī]** The mark of Abraham's religion is belief in all the messengers and all the books, and that is the distinguishing characteristic of the Muslims. **[Qurṭubī]** *Ḥanīfiyyah* is a creed with visible practices attached — unity, pilgrimage, sacrifice, circumcision and facing the qiblah.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 2:135 for the parallel; 2:140, which adds Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the Tribes among those who were neither Jews nor Christians; 2:111–13 and 5:18 for the related claims of special status; 2:135c for the concept of *ḥanīf*; 3:19c for "submitter". **[Ibn Kathīr]** 2:135, "And they say, 'Be Jews or Christians and you shall be rightly guided'". **[Qurṭubī]** His treatment of *islām* in Sūrat al-Baqarah. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:104–108 for his discussion of the meaning of *ḥanīf*.

**Reflection.** Al-Ṭabarī's opening sentence is unusually dense and worth unpacking: the verse belies a claim, declares Abraham quit of the claimants, and *awards* his religion to someone **[Ṭabarī]**. It is that third function that turns a piece of chronology into a verdict, and the next verse will name the winners **[Ṭabarī]**. The two words used for Abraham are both defined by the commentators in the same direction — *ḥanīf* as upright on a road rather than merely negative, and *muslim* as humility of heart and limb before what God has ordained **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Qurṭubī's definition of *ḥanīf* is the one that surprises: a creed with practice attached — unity, pilgrimage, sacrifice, circumcision, qiblah **[Qurṭubī]**. And the report Ṭabarī places here is the loveliest thing in the passage: Zayd b. ʿAmr asking a Jewish scholar for a religion, being told it comes with a share of God's wrath, and answering that he flees *from* the wrath of God — then being told the only alternative is *ḥanīf*, which is "the religion of Abraham: he was neither a Jew nor a Christian, and he worshipped none but God" **[Ṭabarī]**.

## 3:68 — إِنَّ أَوْلَى ٱلنَّاسِ بِإِبْرَٰهِيمَ لَلَّذِينَ ٱتَّبَعُوهُ وَهَٰذَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ وَلِىُّ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*"Indeed, the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him, and this prophet, and those who believe. And God is the Protector of the believers."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by "the people most entitled to Abraham": the people most deserving of Abraham, and of his support and his allegiance. **[Ṭabarī]** "Are those who followed him" — those who travelled his road and his way, declaring God to be one, sincere to Him in the religion, establishing his *sunnah* and legislating his laws, and being *ḥanīfs* submitting to God, not associating anything with Him. **[Ṭabarī]** "And this prophet" — meaning Muḥammad ﷺ. **[Ṭabarī]** "And those who believe" — those who believed Muḥammad and what he brought them from God. **[Ṭabarī]** "And God is the Protector of the believers" — God is the helper of those who believe in Muḥammad, affirming him in his prophethood and in what he brought them from Him, against those who oppose them among the people of the religions. **[Jalālayn]** "Surely the people with the best claim" — most worthy — "of Abraham are those who followed him during his time, and this Prophet", Muḥammad ﷺ, "on account of his according with him as regards most of the rulings of his law, and those who believe" from among his community; "they are the ones that ought to say 'We follow his religion', and not you; and God is the Protector of the believers", their Helper and Preserver. **[Ibn Kathīr]** This verse means: "The people who have the most right to be followers of Ibrāhīm are those who followed his religion, and this Prophet, Muḥammad ﷺ, and his Companions from the Muhājirīn, the Anṣār and those who followed their lead." **[Ibn Kathīr]** "And Allāh is the *Walī* (Protector and Helper) of the believers" — Allāh is the Protector of all those who believe in His Messengers. **[Qurṭubī]** *Awlā* means "more entitled"; it is said: to help and support; and it is said: to the proof. **[Qurṭubī]** "Those who followed him" — upon his religion and his *sunnah*. **[Saʿdī]** God informed that the people most entitled to Abraham are Muḥammad ﷺ and his followers, and the followers of the Friend before Muḥammad ﷺ. **[Saʿdī]** "And God is the Protector of the believers": the stronger the servant's faith, the more God takes charge of him with His kindness, and eases him to the easier way and keeps him from the harder. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 68 declares that the closest of people to Abraham were those who followed him during his time, and now the closest to him is the Prophet of Islam, Muḥammad al-Muṣṭafā ﷺ, and so are the believers in him and in the past prophets. **[Maʿārif]** These believers are the community of Muḥammad ﷺ, and their guardian is God, and He will reward them for their belief. **[Study Quran]** "Worthiest of Abraham" means having the most claim on him, the most right to be associated with him. **[Study Quran]** "Worthiest" translates *awlā*, a term related to *walī*, "protector" or "friend", which appears later in this verse in the description of God as the Protector of the believers; in this sense, to say that they are *awlā* means they have the strongest bond of *walāyah* — inheritance, protection, friendship, alliance — with Abraham.

**Language.** **[Qurṭubī]** "And this prophet" — He singled out his mention to magnify him, just as He said, "In both of them are fruits, and date-palms and pomegranates" (55:68). **[Qurṭubī]** *Hādhā* is in the nominative, conjoined to *alladhīna*; *al-nabiyyu* is an adjective of *hādhā* or an explicative apposition; and were it put in the accusative, that too would be admissible in speech, conjoined to the *hāʾ* in *ittabaʿūhu*. **[Study Quran]** *Awlā* is related to *walī*, so the claim to Abraham and the protection of the believers are expressed through the same root. **[Ṭabarī]** "Followed him" is glossed as travelling his road, singling out God, establishing his *sunnah* and legislating his laws.

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7214 — Qatādah, on "the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him": those who followed him upon his religion, his *sunnah*, his way and his natural constitution; "and this prophet" — the prophet of God, Muḥammad; "and those who believed" with him, meaning the believers who believed the prophet of God and followed him; the Messenger of God ﷺ and those with him among the believers were the people most entitled to Abraham. **[Ṭabarī]** 7215 — al-Rabīʿ said the like of it. **[Ṭabarī]** 7216 — from Sufyān al-Thawrī, from his father, from Abū al-Ḍuḥā, from Masrūq, from ʿAbdullāh b. Masʿūd, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: "Every prophet has protectors (*wulāh*) from among the prophets, and my protector among them is my father, the Friend (*khalīl*) of my Lord" — then he recited "the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him, and this prophet, and those who believe; and God is the Protector of the believers". *(the editor of the printed Tabarī grades this isnād ṣaḥīḥ muttaṣil)* **[Ibn Kathīr]** Saʿīd b. Manṣūr recorded that Ibn Masʿūd said that the Messenger of Allāh ﷺ said: "Every Prophet had a *Walī* from among the Prophets. My *Walī* among them is my father Ibrāhīm, the *Khalīl* of my Lord, the Exalted and Most Honoured" — and the Prophet ﷺ then recited this verse. **[Study Quran]** A ḥadīth of the Prophet states: "Every prophet has protection (*walāyah*) from among the prophets, and my protector among them is my father, the Friend of God (*Khalīl Allāh*), Abraham"; the *walāyah* mentioned in this ḥadīth can also evoke the sense of succession and inheritance in the spiritual sense. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn ʿAbbās said: the chiefs of the Jews said, "By God, O Muḥammad, you know well that we have more right than you and anyone else to the religion of Abraham, for he was a Jew, and you are motivated by nothing but envy" — so God sent down this verse. **[Qurṭubī]** And from Ibn Masʿūd, that the Prophet ﷺ said: "Every prophet has protectors from among the prophets, and my protector among them is my father and the Friend of my Lord" — then he recited "the people most entitled to Abraham are those who followed him, and this prophet".

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** Entitlement to Abraham is measured by following his road — singling out God, sincerity, his *sunnah* and his laws — not by descent or by claim. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Those with the most right to be his followers are those who followed his religion, and this prophet, and his Companions and those who followed their lead. **[Saʿdī]** God's taking charge of a believer increases with his faith, easing him to the easier way. **[Qurṭubī]** *Awlā* has been taken both as "more entitled to his help and support" and as "more entitled by the proof".

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 3:67 for the dissociation that precedes. **[Qurṭubī]** 55:68 for the device of singling out one item to magnify it; 2:139 for his treatment of the same idea in Sūrat al-Baqarah. **[Ṭabarī]** 1:489, 564 and 5:424 for his treatment of *walī*; 3:65–67 for the dispute that leads here. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:69, which follows.

**Reflection.** Having cleared Abraham of both claimants in 3:67, the sūrah now says who gets him — and the answer is not a lineage but a description: the people who walked his road **[Ṭabarī]**. The Prophet is named separately from "those who believe", and Qurṭubī explains why: singling him out after a general group is a device of magnification, the way dates and pomegranates are named after "fruits" **[Qurṭubī]**. There is a deliberate word-play in the verse that the Study Quran draws out — *awlā*, "most entitled", sharing a root with *walī*, "protector", so that the claim to Abraham and God's protection of the believers are bound into one another **[Study Quran]**. And the ḥadīth placed here closes the circle: every prophet has a protector among the prophets, and this one's is Abraham — his father and the Friend of God — after which he recited the verse itself **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. The occasion report Qurṭubī records gives the verse its edge: the Jewish chiefs had accused the Prophet of envy, and the answer is that entitlement is a matter of following, not of blood **[Qurṭubī]**.

## 3:69 — وَدَّت طَّآئِفَةٌۭ مِّنْ أَهْلِ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ لَوْ يُضِلُّونَكُمْ وَمَا يُضِلُّونَ إِلَّآ أَنفُسَهُمْ وَمَا يَشْعُرُونَ

*"A party of the People of the Scripture wish they could lead you astray; but they lead none astray except themselves, and they do not perceive it."*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by "wished": hoped for and desired. **[Ṭabarī]** "A party" — a group. **[Ṭabarī]** "Of the People of the Scripture" — the people of the Torah among the Jews and the people of the Gospel among the Christians. **[Ṭabarī]** "They could lead you astray" — they say: would that they could turn you, O believers, away from Islām and bring you back from it to the unbelief they are upon, destroying you by that. **[Ṭabarī]** And "leading astray" (*iḍlāl*) in this place means destruction, as in the saying of God, "And they say: when we are lost (*ḍalalnā*) in the earth, shall we then be in a new creation?" (32:10) — meaning, when we perish; and as in the saying of al-Akhṭal satirising Jarīr: "You were the speck in a dark, foaming wave — the torrent cast it and it perished (*ḍalla*) utterly", meaning it perished completely. *(digression: al-Ṭabarī goes on to discuss a variant reading of al-Nābighah's line and rejects al-Aṣmaʿī's explanation of it)* **[Jalālayn]** When the Jews called Muʿādh b. Jabal, Ḥudhayfah b. al-Yamān and ʿAmmār b. Yāsir to join their religion, the following was revealed: "There is a party of the People of the Scripture who yearn to make you go astray; yet they cause none to stray except themselves, because the sin for their leading others astray falls upon them, while the believers do not heed them in this; but they are not aware of this." **[Ibn Kathīr]** Allāh states that the Jews envy the faithful and wish they could misguide them; and Allāh states that the punishment of this behaviour will fall back upon them, while they are unaware. **[Qurṭubī]** It was revealed concerning Muʿādh b. Jabal, Ḥudhayfah b. al-Yamān and ʿAmmār b. Yāsir, when the Jews of Banū al-Naḍīr, Qurayẓah and Qaynuqāʿ called them to their religion. **[Qurṭubī]** On this reading, *min* is partitive; and it is said: all the People of the Scripture, in which case *min* specifies the genus. **[Qurṭubī]** The meaning of "they could lead you astray" is: they make you acquire sin by turning back from the religion of Islām and opposing it. **[Qurṭubī]** Ibn Jurayj said: "they lead you astray" means they destroy you, and from it is the saying of al-Akhṭal: "You were the speck in a dark, foaming wave — the torrent cast it and it perished utterly", meaning it perished completely. **[Qurṭubī]** "And they do not perceive" — they are not aware that they will not reach the believers to mislead them; and it is said: "they do not perceive" means they do not know the soundness of Islām, while it is obligatory upon them to know, because the proofs are manifest and the arguments are dazzling. **[Saʿdī]** This is among God's favour upon this community, that He informed them of the plotting of their enemies among the People of the Scripture, and that out of their eagerness to mislead the believers they vary their evil plots. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 69 exposes the cherished desire of the People of the Book to mislead Muslims away from the true faith they are on; the text then firmly declares that the victim of their effort to misguide is no one other than themselves, something they do not realise. **[Study Quran]** Some People of the Book go astray themselves insofar as they reject the truth that exists before them in the person of the Prophet Muhammad and the revelation of the Qurʾān; or they go astray because they wish to turn you back into disbelievers after your having believed, out of envy in their souls.

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** *Waddat* is glossed as hoping and desiring; *ṭāʾifah* as a group; and *iḍlāl* in this place as destruction, supported by 32:10 and by a line of al-Akhṭal. **[Qurṭubī]** The *min* of "a party of the People of the Scripture" is either partitive or specifies the genus, depending on whether the whole or a part is meant. **[Qurṭubī]** "And they lead none astray except themselves" is a negation followed by an affirmation. **[Jalālayn]** "They cause none to stray except themselves" — because the sin for their leading others astray falls upon them, while the believers do not heed them in it.

**Context.** **[Qurṭubī]** The verse was revealed concerning three named Companions whom the Jewish tribes of Madinah invited to their religion. **[Jalālayn]** The same occasion: the Jews called Muʿādh b. Jabal, Ḥudhayfah b. al-Yamān and ʿAmmār b. Yāsir to join their religion. **[Saʿdī]** The plotting described here is varied and persistent, and God's informing the believers of it is counted as a favour to the community. **[Ibn Kathīr]** What is described is envy of the faithful and the wish to misguide them.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The harm they intend is destruction, and it rebounds on the plotters. **[Jalālayn]** The sin of the attempt falls on those who make it, since the believers do not heed them. **[Qurṭubī]** Their unawareness is twofold in the reports: unaware that their plotting will not reach the believers, or unaware of the soundness of Islām, which it was incumbent on them to know because the proofs are manifest. **[Saʿdī]** The plotters did not know that the religion of God is the truth: when its reality reaches hearts, its possessor only increases in faith and certainty the longer time goes on, and doubts only increase him in holding fast to his religion and in praise and thanks to God for His favour upon him. *(Bayān al-Qurʾān)*

**Cross-references.** **[Qurṭubī]** 2:109, "Many of the People of the Scripture wish they could turn you back into disbelievers after you have believed, out of envy", as the counterpart verse; 3:72 for the specific plot described later. **[Study Quran]** 2:9, "They would deceive God and the believers, yet they deceive none but themselves, though they are unaware"; 2:57, "They wronged Us not, but themselves did they wrong"; 3:118, "They wish you to suffer"; 4:89; 60:2; 68:9. **[Ṭabarī]** 32:10 for *ḍalalnā* in the sense of perishing; 2:495–496 for his earlier treatment of the root. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:70, which follows.

**Reflection.** The verse is built on a rebound: the harm intended lands on the one who intended it **[Jalālayn]**, **[Maʿārif]**. Al-Ṭabarī takes the verb in its strongest sense — *iḍlāl* here is destruction, not merely confusion — and supports it from the Qurʾān itself and from a line of satire poetry **[Ṭabarī]**. The occasion reports make it concrete: three named Companions, invited by three named Jewish tribes to leave Islām **[Qurṭubī]**, **[Jalālayn]**. Qurṭubī adds a small grammatical fork with a large consequence — whether "a party of" means some of them or is simply identifying the kind, that is, whether this is a faction or the whole community of the People of the Scripture **[Qurṭubī]**. And the clause that stings most is the last: they do not perceive. Ibn Kathīr and Qurṭubī both turn that into the failure it really is — not a failure of cleverness but a failure to see what the proofs in front of them plainly show **[Ibn Kathīr]**, **[Qurṭubī]**. Saʿdī reads the whole thing from the believers' side: being told is a favour, and the plot that was meant to unsettle them only settles them further **[Saʿdī]**.

## 3:70 — يَٰٓأَهْلَ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ لِمَ تَكْفُرُونَ بِـَٔايَٰتِ ٱللَّهِ وَأَنتُمْ تَشْهَدُونَ

*"O People of the Scripture, why do you disbelieve in the signs of God while you bear witness?"*

**Meaning.** **[Ṭabarī]** God means by this: "O People of the Scripture" — of the Jews and the Christians — "why do you disbelieve", meaning: why do you deny — "in the signs of God", meaning: in what is in the Book of God which He sent down to you upon the tongues of your prophets, of His signs and His proofs — "while you bear witness" that it is the truth from your Lord. **[Ṭabarī]** This is a rebuke from God to the people of the two scriptures for their disbelief in Muḥammad ﷺ and their denial of his prophethood, while they find him in their books, together with their testifying that what is in their books is the truth and is from God. **[Jalālayn]** "O People of the Scripture! Why do you disbelieve in God's verses" — the Qurʾān, that includes all the descriptions of Muḥammad ﷺ — "when you yourselves bear witness?", when you know that it is the truth. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Allāh criticises them: "O People of the Scripture! Why do you disbelieve in the Āyāt of Allāh, while you bear witness?" — you know for certain that Allāh's Āyāt are true and authentic. **[Ibn Kathīr]** And: "Why do you mix truth with falsehood and conceal the truth, while you know?" — by hiding what is in your Books about the description of Muḥammad ﷺ, while you know what you do. **[Qurṭubī]** "While you bear witness" — that is, to the soundness of the signs you have in your books, according to Qatādah and al-Suddī; and it is said the meaning is: while you bear witness to the like of them among the signs of the prophets, which you affirm. **[Maʿārif]** Verse 70, addressing the People of the Book, asks them why they disbelieve in the signs of God, such as the verses in the Torah and the Gospel which prove the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ — because the denial of his prophethood amounts to declaring these verses to be false, which is infidelity, although they themselves confirm these as true. **[Maʿārif]** This is an admonition on their error in belief. **[Study Quran]** These verses are usually understood to refer to the rejection of the mission of the Prophet by Jews and Christians, even though he is foretold, according to Islamic belief, in their sacred books; and some say that their being witnesses means that they bear witness to the truth of the prophets in their own tradition, who themselves manifest the same truths. **[Saʿdī]** What drove them to these reprehensible deeds was envy and transgression and the fear of being argued against — as God said, "Many of the People of the Scripture wish they could turn you back into disbelievers after you have believed, out of envy from themselves, after the truth had become clear to them".

**Language.** **[Ṭabarī]** "Signs of God" is glossed as what is in the Book God sent down upon the tongues of the prophets, of His signs and proofs; and "bear witness" as testifying that it is the truth from their Lord. **[Ibn Kathīr]** The bearing of witness is explained as knowing for certain that God's signs are true and authentic. **[Jalālayn]** "God's verses" is taken as the Qurʾān, which contains all the descriptions of Muḥammad ﷺ. **[Maʿārif]** The words "you are yourselves a witness" (3:70) and "you know" (3:71) should not be taken to mean that disbelief would become permissible for them if they did not attest to the truth or had no knowledge of it — this is because *kufr* is intrinsically an abominable thing to practise and is prohibited under all conditions; however, the mention of disbelief after knowledge and attestation is to increase the impact of the reproachment. *(Bayān al-Qurʾān)*

**Ḥadīth & āthār.** **[Ṭabarī]** 7219 — Qatādah, on "O People of the Scripture, why do you disbelieve in the signs of God while you bear witness": you bear witness that the description of Muḥammad, the prophet of God ﷺ, is in your book, and then you disbelieve in him and deny him and do not believe in him, while you find him written with you in the Torah and the Gospel: "the unlettered prophet who believes in God and His words". **[Ṭabarī]** 7220 — al-Rabīʿ said: you bear witness that the description of Muḥammad is in your book, then you disbelieve in him and do not believe in him, while you find him with you in the Torah and the Gospel: "the unlettered prophet". **[Ṭabarī]** 7221 — al-Suddī: "the signs of God" is Muḥammad; and as for "you bear witness" — they bear witness that it is the truth, finding it written with them. **[Ṭabarī]** 7222 — Ibn Jurayj: that the religion with God is Islām; God has no religion other than it. **[Ibn Kathīr]** Allāh's reply to their concealment was to guide the hearts of the faithful to perfect faith through the clear signs, plain proofs and unequivocal evidence that He sent down to His servant and Messenger Muḥammad ﷺ — and this occurs, O you Jews, even though you hide the description of Muḥammad ﷺ, the unlettered Prophet whom you find in your Books that you received from the earlier Prophets. **[Jalālayn]** The report that the Jews called Muʿādh b. Jabal, Ḥudhayfah b. al-Yamān and ʿAmmār b. Yāsir to their religion stands as the setting for this and the preceding verse.

**Belief.** **[Ṭabarī]** The charge is not ignorance but contradiction: they testify that their books are true and then deny the one their books describe. **[Ibn Kathīr]** They know for certain that the signs are true and authentic, and the concealment is of the description of Muḥammad ﷺ found in their own Books. **[Qurṭubī]** The testimony is either to the soundness of the signs in their books or to the like of them among the signs of the prophets they already affirm. **[Maʿārif]** Denying his prophethood amounts to declaring those verses false, which is itself unbelief — and this is an admonition on an error in belief, not merely in conduct.

**Cross-references.** **[Study Quran]** 3:99, "O People of the Book! Why do you turn those who believe from the way of God, seeking to make it crooked, while you are witnesses?"; 3:71 and 2:42 for "confound not truth with falsehood, nor knowingly conceal the truth"; 7:157 for the foretelling of the Prophet. **[Ibn Kathīr]** 3:71 for what they conceal; 3:72–74 for the plots that follow. **[Qurṭubī]** 2:109 for the envy. **[Ṭabarī]** 3:69 for the preceding verse.

**Reflection.** The question is rhetorical and the sting is in its last two words — "while you bear witness" **[Ṭabarī]**. What they bear witness to is spelled out in the reports: the description of the unlettered prophet, written in the Torah and the Gospel, which they themselves attest is true, and then deny **[Ṭabarī]**, **[Ibn Kathīr]**. Al-Suddī goes so far as to say that "the signs of God" here *is* Muḥammad **[Ṭabarī]**. Maʿārif adds the careful point that mentioning knowledge and testimony is not to make unbelief conditional upon them — unbelief is abominable in any case — but to deepen the rebuke **[Maʿārif]**. And Ibn Jurayj's terse gloss closes the matter from the other side: the religion with God is Islām, and He has no religion besides it **[Ṭabarī]**.
